```text


THE CORRUPTION OF CO-DESIGN
Designers are often depicted as social change agents that serve the good in the world.
Similarly, co-design tends to be described as a democratic mode of creativity that is
somehow beyond reproach. But is change a virtue in itself, and do participatory
practices always produce socially beneﬁcial outcomes?
Such questions are becoming more pressing as co-design has emerged as a
dominant practice in planning and urban design, while also informing corporate
management and public administration. In this book, Otto von Busch and Karl
Palmås suggest that designers tend to overemphasize the place of ideals in design,
leaving them ill-equipped to deal with a social world of power-wielding and
zero-sum games. Seeking to reorient the concerns of the Scandinavian tradition
of participatory design, they suggest that co-design processes are rife with
betrayals, decay, and corruption, and that designerly empathy has morphed into a
new form of cunning statecraft.
In putting forward Realdesign as an alternative conception of design practice,
von Busch and Palmås ask: What hard lessons about the social must today’s
designers learn from realists like Machiavelli?
Otto von Buschis Professor of Integrated Design at Parsons School of Design,
The New School, New York.
Karl Palmås is Associate Professor at Chalmers University of Technology,
Gothenburg.

“Who knew critical design theory could be funny?” 
Lucy Kimbell, Professor, University of the Arts London, UK 
“This is an urgently needed book, providing Social Designers with political the­
ories to correct the too-often naïve expansion of their material practice to social 
challenges. The Corruption of Co-Design manages to be a robust political argument 
that is nevertheless attentive to the experimental particularities of design practice. 
It marks a welcome step-change in what responsible co-designing entails.” 
Cameron Tonkinwise, Professor, University of Technology Sydney, Australia 
“Machiavelli for democratic designers! A contradicto in adjecto? Not so for von 
Busch and Palmås. Realdesign, the healing cure they ordinate for the participative 
designer is bitter and it hurts, but it is most timely and helpful since codesign and 
design thinking today are fully integrated into neoliberal and public management 
agendas, reducing the ‘good’ designer to an (unconscious) hypocritical moralist. 
‘Good’ maybe dead, but the suggested remedy for the participatory designer does 
not necessarily mean to give up co-created playful democratic utopian dreams, 
but rather to learn how to consciously take into account the rationality of power, 
to be able to seriously deal with betrayal, corruption, cunning and hypocrisy as 
we play along. The Corruption of Co-Design is a good place to start!” 
Pelle Ehn, Professor Emeritus, Sweden 

THE CORRUPTION OF 
CO-DESIGN 
Political and Social Conﬂicts in 
Participatory Design Thinking 
Otto von Busch and Karl Palmås

1 
INTRODUCTION 
The problems of participatory design 
Let’s peek into the design studios and see what is going on. 
In studio one, a designer sets out a participatory city planning process, using an 
online avatar world. Here citizens can suggest or build their own structures of 
how they want the city to look and discuss visions of their future city. 
In another studio, a designer puts up a noticeboard in the stairs of an apartment 
building to listen to what the people in the neighborhood want designed, asking 
for comments and ideas. 
In a third studio, a designer participates in a suggested development of a local 
market. It is situated in an area of the city neglected by planners, and the designer 
considers how to engage the informal transactions taking place. 
In a fourth studio, a designer sets out to engineer an app to tackle traﬃc 
congestion, seeking data that can geolocate cars across the city and foster 
community through carsharing. 
In a ﬁfth studio, a designer starts a social enterprise with the aim of doing good 
for a marginalized population, inviting volunteers to work for the community. 
In a sixth studio, a designer sets out to “hack” into a local economy by suggesting 
an alternative currency system. 
And in a seventh studio, a designer wants to disrupt the status quo of homeless 
shelters, starting a system that will rate shelters and guests to allocate beds and 
funding better. 
Perhaps you have been around some time in the world of design and recognize 
the social and relational material shaped in the examples above. Maybe you can 
see dark clouds gathering in each of the studios. Like so many other materials a 
designer works with, the social realm has its recalcitrant properties. But instead of 
irregularly swelling wood, twisting ﬁbers, or a stalling kiln, you may have 
encountered how “real life” comes to usurp even the best-planned social 
DOI: 10.4324/9781003281443-1 

2 Introduction 
scenarios. Of course, you may say, the avatar world became a cesspool of abuse, 
the friendly noticeboard was ﬁlled with insults, unsolicited goods were traded at 
the market, the alternative currency became a tool for traﬃcking, real estate 
moguls exploited the social enterprise, and so on and so forth, and all the rest of 
it. Something is going on when designers engage in social sculpture as the rela­
tional materials of humans turn out as unruly as other natural materials. We must 
ask: What happens along the way with all the good intentions? 
Indeed, if you have followed the ﬁeld of design over the years, it would not be 
uncommon that you could have encountered any of the cases above in one form or 
another. Perhaps you have even suggested or instigated one along these lines. The 
examples above are quick glimpses of how the discipline of design has moved from a 
profession in proximity to engineering, with reasonably modest aspirations and 
straightforward prompts, into a ﬁeld with lofty ambitions for understanding and 
shaping societal processes, if not even claiming to redesign cultures themselves. 
While some of the glances into these everyday scenarios of design may be stepping 
into ethical dilemmas or more or less methodological mineﬁelds, they all also share a 
fundamental premise for the social settings of design. The “social” is a material with 
certain propensities. The social realm is a material that can be shaped towards vir­
tuous ends, designers suggest. Relationships can be modiﬁed and recircuited with 
design. People are basically good. And they all want more design. In the end, the 
social world is just another material, waiting patiently to be shaped under the sensible 
hands of the empathetic designer. 
Under the banner of design thinking, there are few limits to the endeavor of 
design. Design makes humans more godlike, and each object or service is just an 
instance on the trajectory towards a bright and virtuous future. Under its pro­
gressive course, design is a practice that shapes the future towards the ideals we hold. 
But the profession seldom asks who holds these ideals. Even though much of the 
world is broken and unfair, and has been designed that way, designers still tell 
themselves unreluctantly that more design is the tool to ﬁx what design originally 
left unsatisfactory. It is seldom reﬂected that it could be the designers themselves 
who are misled or ill-equipped to act in and aﬀect power dynamics. But this is 
hard to see, as design keeps ﬁlling its vocabulary with terms wrapped in virtuous 
connotations. After all, don’t we all want more participation, function, and beauty? 
Who does not want more democratic design? Are these not the unparalleled paths 
towards peace and justice? Even if not spoken aloud, these terms form the 
underlying values that shape design today, making some of the opening scenarios 
such a recurring sight in design studios. 
But there may also be another perspective. People are headstrong, making 
social relations challenging to manage in ways foreign to the traditional materials 
of design processes. The underlying forces that give values, loyalties, and habits 
their form are unlike those that shape the materials and commodities of con­
sumerism. The human material kicks back against designerly intentions in ways 
that may undermine even the most righteous course of action. Virtues are 

Introduction 3 
corrupted as they face the hell of other people. While the plasma of social rela­
tions may be intangible or more abstract than the traditional materials of the 
design workshops, it is real just the same. Indeed, the social world of design may 
just be too damn real: Real as in realist. 
There is an urgency in unpacking the properties of social design, as collaboration, 
participation, and co-design. Not only do they seep into design practices on a wide 
scale, but they are commonly seen as essential tools in handling the critical need to 
tackle the injustices of the world. With current resurgence in issues around social 
justice it has become apparent that the social systems across societies are not broken 
by unintended ﬂaws, but designed to exclude, extract, and plunder. It is becoming 
apparent to designers that these are more than “wicked problems”. They are real 
issues. Systemic, and also social, issues. And even more; they are realist issues. 
This more skeptical perspective on the design of social matters may not be news to 
feminist and decolonial practitioners. Indeed, as some of the case studies presented in 
this book will show, the anti-social eﬀects of supposedly “social” designs are often at 
their clearest when viewed through feminist or decolonial lenses. Further, some 
essential concepts are recurring in the social designers’ vocabulary – such as 
questioning decision-making through the “ladder of citizen participation” (Arnstein 
1969), and warnings against the “tyranny of structurelessness” of horizontal organi­
zation (Freeman 1972) – stem from these traditions. Outside the ﬁeld of design, 
feminist thinkers with realist inclinations have long highlighted conﬂict as a core 
political mechanism which is commonly silenced to uphold consensus and power. 
Some of these scholars appear in design school reading lists, such as Sara Ahmed 
(2017), bell hooks (2000, 2014), and Chantal Mouﬀe (2000, 2005). As we will see, 
Mouﬀe’sw o r ki np a r t i c u l a ri sac e n t r a lr e f e rence in the contemporary discussion 
on design and the political. Having said that, realism comes with substantial his­
torical baggage. While contemporary realist thought is slowly becoming less 
male-dominated – with thinkers like Judith Shklar having become a part of the 
canon – the realist tradition tends to be associated with a long list of white men, 
and an unbecoming amalgam of cynicism and conservatism. 
This fact is all the more problematic when introducing this tradition in the 
domain of Scandinavian participative design, which treasures inclusion and care as 
its leitmotif. Nevertheless, it is this very territory – where participation and political 
realism meet – that this book will venture into. To set us oﬀ on our journey, let 
us take a step back and look closer at the context and course of events that have 
put the honorable Scandinavian design tradition to the test. 
Scandinavians mugged by reality 
Gothenburg, 15 June 2001: The trigger of an SIG Sauer handgun is pulled, and a 
fateful bullet blasts out in the summer evening, only a block away from the 
University of Gothenburg’s School of Design and Crafts. For the ﬁrst time in 
seventy years, the Swedish police force uses live ammunition against protesters. 

4 Introduction 
During these peaceful seventy years, the country had established itself as a model 
social democracy, based on consensual agreements and nonviolent bargaining 
between the representatives of capital and labor. This is a perspective that has also 
shaped its perspective on participatory design. The “consensual spirit” (samför­
ståndsandan) that underpinned this politico-economic arrangement had served the 
citizens well: During the post-war era, Sweden held the enviable position of 
being one of the world’s wealthiest nations, while simultaneously enjoying the 
position as the world’s most equal and equitable societies. 
However, on that day in June, when US President George W. Bush visited the 
city in conjunction with a European Council meeting, Gothenburg became the 
stage for an animated dissensus. Drawing inspiration from protests at the 1999 World 
Trade Organization (WTO) Ministerial Conference in Seattle and the 2000 
International Monetary Fund (IMF) and World Bank summit meeting in Prague, 
organizations associated with the Global Justice movement, or the so-called “anti­
globalization” movement, used the event to demonstrate their discontent with the 
Washington consensus-based, corporate-led, neoliberal globalization. While there, 
they joined ranks with anti-EU campaigners inspired by protests during the previous 
European Union (EU) summit, held in Nice in late 2000. 
Intent on avoiding the violence of Seattle, Prague, and Nice, the Swedish 
authorities, local activists, and academics had prepared a strategy for the Gothenburg 
event. This implied that protesters would have a chance to engage in a dialogue with 
the people in power. Despite these eﬀorts, a shot was ﬁred at a protester who had 
attended a Reclaim the City party. The event had been attacked by a parallel protest, 
causing the police to disband the party, which in turn sparked a violent standoﬀ. 
Clashes between protesters and the police had started earlier that day, when the 
Black Bloc had rioted on the central Avenue, literally a stone’s throw away from the 
park where the party was staged, just a block or two from the School of Design 
and Craft. The organizers of the dialogue would later state that they had been too 
naïve when preparing for the event, underestimating the violent potential of 
both protesters and the police. 
The bullet hit the protester in the stomach. He survived but lost one kidney 
and his spleen. On a ﬁgurative level, that shot also torpedoed the Swedish 
authorities’ ambition to demonstrate the power of the “Gothenburg model” of 
dialogue. The assembled world leaders could scarcely see how the outcomes of 
the Swedish dialogue approach diﬀered from other, less romantic approaches to 
dealing with the threat of violence. The assembled protesters, on the other hand, 
who had taken the promise of a dialogue at face value, concluded that it was all a 
charade. Many of them have subsequently admitted to becoming more cynical 
regarding politics and more skeptical towards Sweden’s liberal democratic insti­
tutions. It was as though the violence had done irreparable damage to the dream 
of building a democratic society on dialogue and broad participation. 
This shattered dream forms the backdrop when assessing the Scandinavian 
participative design tradition today. Indeed, in a 2016 lecture at the Rhode Island 

Introduction 5 
School of Design, design scholar Pelle Ehn suggests that he construes it as a 
“melancholic design”. This outlook ties the Scandinavian participative design 
tradition to the Nordic Noir literary genre. Both bodies of work are set against a 
metanarrative of a once-great democratic and social model that seems to be 
withering away. Yet, despite this incipient darkness, the antihero criminal 
inspector Kurt Wallander – lead protagonist in Swedish author Henning Man­
kell’s crime ﬁction – insists that “we must stand up for democracy”. Ultimately, 
Ehn suggests, Scandinavian participatory design is concerned with that very 
challenge – how to cope with the political realities of a democratic and social 
system in retreat. To put it bluntly: It is a design tradition that has emerged from 
the experiences of Scandinavians mugged by reality, a phrase borrowed from 
Irving Kristol, who famously deﬁned a neo-con as “a liberal mugged by reality”. 
This general sense of being mugged by reality is expressed in a particular 
manner for the generation that became socio-politically aware in the late nineties. 
Irony is the word that comes to mind here: Given the violence involved, it may 
seem glib to call these events “ironic” rather than “tragic” but, following Rein­
hold Niebuhr (1952: 2), “tragedy elicits tears of admiration and pity for the 
hero”, whom we believe to be virtuous and benign. Irony, on the other hand, 
allows us to understand tragic outcomes as emanating from the protagonist’s 
foolishness, vanity, or pretentiousness. Irony sheds light on the comic absurdities 
of social life, yet does not shrink to the fact that so many dreams of the future 
tend to be cruelly refuted by history. 
With the beneﬁt of hindsight, we see two signiﬁcant ironies that highlight how 
history tends to debunk political dreams. The ﬁrst irony is evident when com­
paring the political demands of the 2001 protesters with the future world order 
they would soon ﬁnd themselves in. Today, we have arguably passed the end­
point of the era of virtually uncontested liberal globalization, and this upending of 
the previous order has moved at a bewildering pace. The gradual shift from the 
“Washington consensus” to a “Beijing consensus” was evident midway through 
the noughties, and by 2008 the World Bank lending power was superseded by 
that of China. However, the outcome of the 2016 US election punctured the 
Bretton Woods-based international trade regime. Moreover, that same year, the 
UK started the process of exiting the European Union. Fifteen years on from 
Gothenburg, the protesters got their wish: A gradual evaporation of the post-89 
liberal world order. The resulting mood, however, seems to be one of con­
sternation rather than jubilation. With the 2022 invasion of Ukraine, the outlook 
has turned even gloomier. 
The second irony relates to the fate of the modus operandi of political tactics. 
The Reclaim the City party that preceded that fateful gunshot took its cue from 
the Reclaim the Streets parties that had become revered within the global justice 
movement (see for instance Klein 1999). For “RTSers”, the act of staging a party 
was seen as congruent with the Situationist idea of constructing situations as a 
means to reclaim the urban space colonized by corporate power. Reclaiming 

6 Introduction 
involved a two-pronged approach, fusing the carnivalesque with guerilla gardening 
and other constructive and “designerly” elements of protest. In the terminology of 
de Certeau (1984), the RTSers had a clear and eﬀective tactic to counter the strate­
gies of corporations and urban planners. What happened to these tactics? Again, fast-
forwarding into the present, municipal urban planners use those same ideas as 
inspiration for “tactical urbanist” methods. Indeed, in the park where the Reclaim 
the City party was staged, the municipality would go on to assist guerilla gardeners in 
planting potatoes. The Situationist “situation” had morphed into the “pop-up” 
phenomenon, and the Certeauian demarcation between the tactical and the strategic 
no longer seems tenable. Paris ’68 slogans like “Beneath the tarmac … af o r e s t” and 
“Beneath the cobblestones … ab e a c h”, deemed radical in 2001, today sound like 
copy from the policy dossiers of planning authorities.1 
This fate of protest tactics relates to a wider concern within radical social 
theory. Has the ’68 celebration of transgressive playfulness become one of the key 
imperatives of contemporary capitalism? Here, we can point to the argument of 
Luc Boltanski and Ève Chiapello, who suggest that the “artistic critique” of the 
capitalist order has morphed into a new spirit of capitalism, and that the qualities 
that guarantee success in contemporary capitalism are “taken directly from the 
repertoire of May 1968” (Boltanski & Chiapello 2005: 97). In fact, we already 
have done so: In a pamphlet titled Abstract Hacktivism (von Busch & Palmås 2006), 
we argued that the tactics that were fashionable around the turn of the century 
were invariably leftovers from the counterculture of the late 1960s. Our problem 
was not that the tactics increasingly represented a faux radicalism; one that had 
gone from being a threat to the system to being the system. On the contrary, we 
saw them as harmless and – more importantly – pointless. 
In Abstract Hacktivism, we suggested that the fundamental problem of these 
approaches was that they were founded on a social ontology that understands and 
relates to the world as if it were a motor. Hence the notion of “culture jam­
ming”– the idea of changing the system by jamming the motor that is society. 
Referencing philosopher Michel Serres, we pointed to the connection between 
the conceptual models through which we understand the world, on the one 
hand, and the everyday technologies that surround us, on the other. Thus, the 
motor worldview originated from the motor era of the nineteenth and twentieth 
centuries. Distancing ourselves from this worldview, we wondered what political 
action would look like if the world is a computer, and the activities of hackers 
could be adopted for uses outside of the computer context. The idea of hacking 
as general practice, “abstracted” from its original manifestation, represented a 
more designerly and technologically informed view of how to produce social 
change. Indeed, the world of computers had provided us with an abundance of 
terms and ideas – copyleft, mesh network, open source, peer-to-peer, protocol – 
that seemed to open a world of possibilities. 
Fittingly, these technological concepts resonated with key ideas circulating 
within a certain domain within radical theory – with Deleuzian rhizomatics and 

Introduction 7 
with the post-workerist notion of “exodus”. Exodus is the political strategy in 
which change is achieved through the exit from mainstream institutions, and the 
simultaneous building of parallel institutions. These parallel institutions are ideally 
non-hierarchical, democratic, and based on the idea of the common. Exodus thus 
implies a defection rather than protest (Virno 1996: 199). In the terminology of 
Michael Hardt and Antonio Negri, it represents a creative ﬂight of the multitude, 
from capital to absolute democracy. Moreover, the idea of abstract hacktivism 
also chimed with the post-Seattle, post-Prague, and post-Gothenburg sensibilities 
of activists: Much like the politics of the street had once usurped the politics of 
the workplace, the politics of the street was usurped by the politics of creating 
“commons”, either online (such as engaging in ﬁle-sharing) or oﬀ-line (such as 
organizing community gardens and hacker/maker spaces). Again, fast-forwarding 
to the present time, this approach has also failed to deliver. The commons to 
which one may plan one’s exodus pale in signiﬁcance when compared to the 
rhizomatic peer-to-peer infrastructures and platforms constructed by companies 
such as Uber. The hacker ethic of citizens is increasingly mobilized by either 
corporate-led hackathons, or public sector initiatives to co-produce or crowd-
source policies. The exodus path seems to have meandered its way back into the 
status quo. In other words, much like the dreams of the 2001 street protesters, 
our political dreams of the early noughties seem to have been cruelly refuted by 
history, or at least by forces in the social reality we would call “realist”. 
In adherence to Ehn’s proposition that Scandinavian participative design is a 
response to the grim realities of an imperiled democratic and social system, this 
book will explore the nature of this darkened reality, and discuss how designers 
may cope with it. 
The reality of social design 
What is the moral of the 2001 Gothenburg dialogue story, and in what way is it sig­
niﬁcant for design thought? The key political lesson of the story can be gleaned from 
the fact that it highlights a set of tensions that are fundamental to the understanding of 
politics and society. Should politics be evaluated on the basis of intentions, like the 
ones that led the Gothenburg authorities to pursue the dialogue agenda, or is it the 
outcomes of actions that count? Is the political sphere a domain that thrives on delib­
eration and a consensual spirit, or does it inevitably involve dissensus, conﬂict, and even 
the threat of violence? Should we strive for utopias, or should we rather worry about 
the corruption of noble ideas and the decay of the social order? What is the place of 
naïve belief, and of cynical disbelief, in politics? Political philosophers generally debate 
these tensions with reference to “idealism” and “realism”, and the distinction between 
these two fundamental positions is central to the argument of this book. Indeed, the 
argument starts from the assertion that the theory and practice of design tends to be 
based on an idealist account of the world. The next chapter will explore these positions 
and the suggestion that design tends to be idealist, in further detail. 

8 Introduction 
Before going into the speciﬁcs of the idealist-realist debate, however, one may 
ask a more fundamental question: Why should designers, or design theorists for 
that matter, care about the logic of social change and political action? One answer 
is found within the discussions on social design and design activism. Aligning itself 
with those subﬁelds, this book will argue that social change should be at the heart 
of design. Secondly, given the fact that design techniques and design thinking are 
increasingly mobilized for the (re-)shaping of key functions of government, 
design is invariably implicated in the reinventing of democratic institutions, and 
of democracy itself. Indeed, as inﬂuential design visionaries Tim Brown and 
Roger Martin have suggested, design thinking can possibly be used as a tool for 
making liberal democracy work better (Brown, Martin & Berger 2014). The 
stakes are high – again, liberal democratic institutions have recently been sub­
jected to severe criticisms, from supporters as well as detractors – but so are risks. 
Engagement with the harsh realities of power may be perilous. Indeed, that is 
another of the takeaways from the story of the failed dialogue in Gothenburg. 
Despite such complexities, the interest in socially engaged design is soaring. With 
exhibitions such as the 2007 “Design for the Other 90%” at Cooper-Hewitt 
National Design Museum in New York, the virtuous design for human dignity and 
human rights has over the last decade taken center stage and popularized a new 
wave of humanitarian design (Smith 2007), largely building on Victor Papanek’s 
the persuasive classic Design for the Real World (1971/1985). As evidenced in inﬂu­
ential books, like John Thackara’s( 2 0 0 5 ) In the Bubble and Emily Pilloton’s( 2 0 0 9 ) 
Design Revolution, design seeks to move from “Good Design” towards a “Design 
for Good”. This implies solving real problems and dealing with well-being and 
sustainability in a way that neither the market economy-driven design, nor state 
actors, can manage. Here, designers are to work for the poor and marginalized, 
with ecological and social issues, with minimizing “unintended” consequences of 
top-down design, and with pushing “toward activism and humanitarian values” 
(Pilloton 2009: 22). 
This desire for designers to get involved in societal issues is hardly a novel phe­
nomenon. On the contrary: A frustration with the impotence to take on social 
controversies and issues has echoed through the design ﬁeld as long as it has existed. 
For instance, Adrian Forty (1986) recognizes how the division of labor already from 
the beginning of industrialism alienated the designer from the needs of workers and 
society. The frustration with such alienation is, in turn, a continuation of similar 
critiques presented by the Arts and Crafts movement, by the Bauhaus, and by 
observers within the industrial design profession. When Bonsiepe (2006) deﬁnes 
industrial design as a creative activity aimed at building a coherent, harmonious 
environment, the resulting product is meant to fulﬁll the material and spiritual 
needs of humanity, as well as reduce the possibility of domination. 
The term “social design”, however, is a more recent phenomenon, which also 
references social practices within art, notably the social sculpture of Joseph Beuys. 
If one accepts Ilpo Koskinen’s suggestion that social design is a process in which 

Introduction 9 
“the main design object is social”– that it represents design processes which use 
the “social as its material” (Koskinen 2016: 18) – how does that square with the 
lessons from the Gothenburg dialogue story? Given the unpredictable and 
recalcitrant nature of this social “material”, considerably less malleable than silk, 
plywood, or Styrofoam, it is worth reviewing how theorists referenced within 
social design conceive of “the social”. Here, we discuss this question solely in 
relation to social design. We will returnt ot h i sl a t e r i n t h eb o o kr e g a r d i n g t h e 
introduction of co-creation and design thinking in the public sector. 
What is the nature of the reality “out there” in society? In a text often cited 
within the social design ﬁeld, Victor Papanek suggests that designers must work 
with the “real” world and “real problems”. This implies more substantial pro­
blems than the ones designers usually address with styling or their band-aid solu­
tions. Depth is the key metaphor utilized to describe the real world; real world 
problems are much more profound than those that the market focuses on. 
Indeed, in order to illustrate “the design problem” Papanek uses the ﬁgure of a 
triangle with only the iceberg tip on the top as the traditional problem of design, 
“the designer’s share”, whereas the massive bottom part is “the real problem”, that 
is, the real needs or urgencies of the world (Papanek 1971/1985: 57). 
Papanek’s triangle is not a design-oriented rehearsal of the classic surface-depth 
trope that the humanities and social sciences have inherited from Freud and 
Marx. The ﬁgure is not to be interpreted as a reference to false consciousness, or 
The designer’s share 
The real problem 
FIGURE 1.1 The Design Problem, adapted from Victor Papanek’s “The Design Pro­
blem” in Design for the Real World: Human Ecology and Social Change (1971). 

10 Introduction 
to bases and superstructures, or to the broader theme of deceptive appearances 
that disguise the true nature of reality. It merely suggests a reorientation of our 
vision, speciﬁcally related to problem of design: Beneath the shallow deﬁnition of 
a design problem, a chasm of real and contended social issues opens. Small, 
market-oriented tweaks won’t do. The designer needs to make social considera­
tions in order not to perpetuate exploitation and injustices, class systems and social 
status hierarchies. Instead, they must start addressing deeper social and ecological 
concerns. 
In addressing such concerns, “design should begin with people” (Papanek 
1983: 7). To Papanek, the silver bullet to engaging with the deep complexity of 
the real world – to organize the “human”– is democratization. His work thus 
follows a long tradition of designers and architects that have tried to anchor the 
purpose of design in democratic ideals, while also countering some of the 
authoritarian elements in their practices. Architects have long since recognized the 
fact that their structures shape their inhabitants, and tried to come to grips with 
the notion that such structures are originally shaped by ideology. Thus, the ethics 
concerning architecture and design has a long history, with highly inﬂuential 
ideas stretching not least from John Ruskin (1849, 1860), as well as William 
Morris (1903, 1934). Some of these ideas still resonate and intersect with more 
socioeconomic works such as Schumacher (1973). 
Furthermore, politically explicit schools of architecture, such as the Bauhaus, 
made clear their intentions for their political subjects. After the break with 
authoritarian regimes, architects explored how to make their processes and pro­
jects more democratic, using ideas of “open” works and systems (Alexander 1977; 
Chermayeﬀ 1982). This desire to transcend the authoritarian and expert-ruled 
past, and instead implement the ideals of democracy is also visible in design: The 
more universal and participatory, the better. In an article about the seven princi­
ples of Universal Design, Rosemarie Rossetti suggests, “Simply put, universal 
design is good design!” (Rossetti 2006). Across the ﬁeld, ideal-driven design, 
applied to the social world, is good. 
Yet, beyond the call for democratized and virtuous design, little is said about 
the treacherous nature of the “real world”. The same can be said for other, more 
radical approaches to how designers should address the social. Tomas Maldonaldo 
suggests that a new designer role is necessary; one that breaks with the com­
modity-producing technocrat, as well as the normative planning of “technocratic 
utopianism” proposed by modernists like Buckminster Fuller (Maldonaldo 1972: 
29). A radical like Maldonaldo instead suggests a “revolution by design” which 
corresponds to a “design by revolution”, rejecting political nihilism to instead 
release a new “operative political praxis” of design (Maldonaldo 1972: 29). In a 
more modest take, Victor Margolin and Sylvia Margolin suggest there must be a 
“social model” for design, parallel to the typical market-oriented model, which 
has been the dominant model for designers to address the world: 

Introduction 11 
The primary purpose of design for the market is creating products for sale. Con­
versely, the foremost intent of social design is the satisfaction of human needs. 
(Margolin & Margolin 2002: 25) 
In order for designers to answer such social calling, they would need training that 
teaches them to act beyond a design brief: 
Design skills cut across all situations, but skills in relating to vulnerable or 
marginalized populations rather than to a brief from a manufacturer need to 
be developed by future social designers. 
(Margolin & Margolin 2002: 29) 
Dean Nieusma (2004) draws similar conclusions, proposing an “alternative design 
scholarship” that aims to support marginalized social groups, building on universal 
design, participatory design, ecological design, feminist design, and socially 
responsible design. Alternative design scholars analyze how technologies and 
other designed artifacts are implicated in larger social problems, such as rampant 
consumerism, sexism, ecological abuse, lack of user participation and autonomy, 
and restricted access to built environments, among others. Through these eﬀorts, 
alternative design scholarship oﬀers designers an opportunity to think about how 
their work might be directed as wisely and fairly as possible (Nieusma 2004: 13). 
Ezio Manzini, a leading proponent of social innovation, who represents a 
design perspective that merges ideas of individual empowerment, entrepreneur­
ship and social change, has similar goals of betterment. With his perspective on 
small, local, open, and connected (SLOC) design services, he suggests such per­
spective on design may even lead to “an emerging civilization” of “beautiful 
islands of applied cultural and socioeconomic wisdom” (Manzini 2015: 26). Even 
if these islands are scattered in a sea of unsustainable ways of being and doing, he 
suggests social innovation may be a new rising continent of emerging potentiality. 
Manzini cites Indian writer Arundhati Roy’s famous statement: Another world is 
not only possible, she is on her way. On a quiet day, I can hear her breathing. 
For Manzini, social innovation serves to make this future visible; it is what makes 
this future multiply on a worldwide scale (Manzini 2015: 26). This optimism is 
tempered by a recognition of some of the impediments for change. He acknowl­
edges the existence of “powerful forces that are ﬁghting against the emergence of a 
new, sustainable world”, pointing to how they strive to “protect their existing 
interests”,a n d h o wt h e y a r e “leading us in the wrong, unsustainable direction” 
(Manzini 2015: 27). However, he also states that it is not his ambition to focus on 
those forces. Instead, he wants to “oﬀer an oriented overview of the state of things, 
meaning a view that aims to trigger, support, and orient possible design actions” 
(italics in original). Thus, in its default mode, his design for social innovation is a 
virtuous endeavor that produces examples of more sustainable ways of being in the 
world, but nevertheless disregards the contestations of power. 

12 Introduction 
These perspectives all point to diﬀerent aspects of the societal context that 
designers increasingly engage with. There is a recognition of the existence of “real” 
problems with “depth”, of marginalized groups, and of forces that resist change. 
However, of the authors mentioned above, Nieusma goes the furthest in high­
lighting the treacherous aspects of the social, arguing that conﬂicts are inherent in 
participatory processes. He is therefore skeptical towards the user-centered approach, 
where the social context is reduced to primarily take the side of the user, thus losing 
some important grounding. Nieusma suggests an alternative design scholarship that is 
more attuned to conﬂicts, called “appropriate design” which accounts for diversity, 
conﬂict, and disagreement. Furthermore, Nieusma warns that a social design should 
not be too conﬁdent in “solving” issues, as most social tensions are never solved but 
only temporarily displaced. Thus, 
we also should recognize the dangers of feeling satisﬁed that alternative 
design scholarship has found the correct path: that it has arrived. Like 
democracy, appropriate design is an ongoing activity that can never be fully 
or ﬁnally achieved. 
(Nieusma 2004: 24) 
This democratically oriented perspective on design, recognizing contestation, 
social tensions, and compromises, has subsequently been further elaborated upon 
by design scholars such as Björgvinsson, Ehn and Hillgren (2012a), DiSalvo 
(2012), Keshavarz and Maze (2013), Markussen (2012), and McCarthy and 
Wright (2015). These conversations on agonistic approaches to design serve as a 
point of departure for the argument that we will pursue in Chapter 3; the ﬁrst 
substantive chapter of the book. These somewhat more cautious approaches to 
social design – which in eﬀect places a question mark over the notion of the 
social as an object to be designed – resonates with the political-theoretical realist 
perspective on politics and society. Arguably, such realism may provide a richer 
description of the nature of the social that designers are increasingly engaging 
with. That, at least, is what this book hopes to show. 
The structure of the argument 
Apparently, Deleuze once told a friend that any worthwhile book must perform 
three functions (Culp 2016: 1). First, it must start from polemics: The book must 
show that other scholarship commits an error. Secondly, it must involve recovery, 
leading to a rediscovery of an essential insight overlooked within that other 
scholarship. Thirdly, the argument must contain an element of creativity; the 
creation of new concepts. 
As regards this particular book, the reader will already have a sense of the polemic 
that will be laid out in the following pages. Borrowing from the terminology of 
political theory, we are suggesting that design scholarship has put an undue emphasis 

Introduction 13 
on idealist understandings of what design is about. However, we also hope to 
show that topics and phenomena discussed within the design discipline may be 
reconstrued in a realist manner. We will perform this recovery operation through 
revisiting classical case studies from the ﬁeld, as well as through discussing new 
empirics. In the end, we will present a new concept of design, which we hope 
practitioners may ﬁnd useful. 
Accordingly, this argument is laid out in three main parts. The ﬁrst part 
contains two opening chapters: This introductory chapter will be followed by a 
chapter that ﬂeshes out the polemic against idealist design in greater detail. 
Thus, Chapter 2, titled “The realist challenge”, unpacks the fundamentals of the 
distinction between idealism and realism in political theory and international 
relations, and transposes these positions onto design. It also speciﬁes the nature 
of the challenge posed by realism: How can one be a realist without getting 
bogged down with a sense that the world is full of impossibilities? How can one 
ﬁnd a utopianism that is not moralist? How does one reclaim the creative element 
inherent in realism? 
The second part of the book consists of four substantive chapters that discuss 
design thinking and practice in the context of the public sector, civil society, and 
social enterprise. More speciﬁcally, the focus will be on the design of organized 
services, and on the design of environments for living (Buchanan 1992: 9–10). 
Each of the four chapters discuss these phenomena by engaging with themes and 
problematics sourced from the realist lexicon: Betrayal, corruption, cunning, and 
hypocrisy. 
Chapter 3, “Betrayal”, continues the interrogation of the political stakes of a 
dialogue, as introduced above. However, rather than discussing protesters on 
the streets, it explores the notion of dialogue in processes of co-design. The 
chapter ﬁrst reviews how citizen participation – used as a governance techni­
que – has been challenged from political theorists on the “agonist” end of rea­
lism, and from critics that dismiss such processes as “post-political ” 
choreographies of neoliberal governance. Discussing the case of a citizen dialo­
gue in the context of urban design in Gothenburg, the chapter introduces 
actor-network theory as an alternative account of how post-political eﬀects may 
result from participatory design processes. This theoretical shift implies shifting 
the focus from ideology critique to a close examination of the material modes 
of co-design. The chapter argues that the notion of betrayal is key to post-
political eﬀects of participation. Thus, the concept is used on two levels of 
abstraction: First, there is a sense in which citizens’ and co-designers ’ interests 
are betrayed in the process of co-design, partly as a result of the fact that policy­
making invariably involves the reduction of dissensus, and the construction of a 
consensus. Second, borrowing from the ontology of actor-network theory and 
its notion of translation, the chapter argues that the representation of citizens’ 
ideas for co-design inevitably – like any other representation – involves a 
betrayal of the entity that is being represented. 

14 Introduction 
Chapter 4, “Corruption”, continues the discussion on the tendency towards 
drift, co-option and betrayal in design. However, the focus will be on how the 
socially oriented institutional designs decay over time. That process of corrosion is 
not inevitable. However, in order to prevent such corruption, idealist-creative 
inclinations of designers must be tempered with realist intuitions. The chapter 
tells the story about participatory budgeting (PB): The beacon of hope of the 
Global Justice movement, which failed to maintain its integrity and ended up 
being a standard item in the World Bank policy toolbox. The chapter introduces 
the work of Machiavelli as a way of thinking about the tension between main­
taining the state of things in the context of change. The argument will proceed to 
study the trajectory of microﬁnance; another instance of a “social innovation” that 
spread across the globe and ultimately fail e dt om a i n t a i ni t s i n t e g r i t y .A r g u a b l y , 
the corruption of microﬁnance – which preceded participatory budgeting as the 
pet idea within global development circles – has led to more disastrous out­
c o m e st h a nt h a to fP B . A ss u c h ,t h e chapter seeks to use Machiavelli’si d e a so n 
innovation and corruption as a means to help well-intentioned designers avoid 
unintended outcomes. 
Chapter 5, “Cunning”, orients the view towards the place of design in emer­
ging modes of governance. The argument starts from a case description of how 
“design for government” is construed in the context of Swedish municipal and 
regional politics. Speciﬁcally, it interrogates how the imperative of empathy is 
deployed as a means to transform citizens with rights into users with needs. 
Building upon the idea of empathy as a trap, the chapter surveys how political 
thought has historically viewed design as a kind of trap-laying. Here, the focus 
will be on how certain forms of cunning and craftiness are associated with the 
concept of design, and on how this mêtis has been banished from the oﬃcial 
language of design. The chapter then discusses such “metic” aspects of design in 
the context of tactical urbanism, and moves on to how design ﬁts into the notion 
of “neuroliberal” government. Speciﬁcally, the argument surveys neuroliberal 
aspects of the design-informed government in Finland, tying it to cybernetic 
modes of governing. Thus, the thrust of the argument is that even if design trades 
in notions of empathy, and may describe itself as being on the side of the weak, it 
nevertheless seems to have reinvented how sovereigns govern their subjects. 
Chapter 6, “Hypocrisy”, attempts to ﬁnd a suitable sensibility for the realist 
designer who recognizes that design is ridden with betrayal, corruption, and 
cunning. Indeed, can a realist designer practice design in good faith? Can that 
designer avoid charges of hypocrisy, or lapsing into cynicism? The chapter ﬁrst 
examines the stakes of this problem by studying how good intentions, empathy, 
and other virtues came to play a dominant role in design. These sentiments are 
traced back to the work of Victor Papanek, whose straddling of the Cold War 
technocracy and the counterculture serves as an entry point to the question of 
hypocrisy in design. The vice of hypocrisy is discussed in the context of the work 
of political theorists Judith Shklar and David Runciman, as well as through a 

Introduction 15 
reexamination of the famous case of Robert Moses’ design of the parkways 
leading to Jones Beach. The chapter then moves on to discuss whether – and if so 
which kind of – hypocrisy can be accepted, following the work of philosophers 
like Hobbes and Bentham. Through these thinkers, the chapter lays the founda­
tion for a sensibility that maintains a skeptical position, while at the same time 
steering clear of stiﬂing cynicism, thus remaining committed to making a pro­
ductive diﬀerence in the world. 
The third and last part contains one chapter. Chapter 7 sketches the outlines of a 
realism-inspired mode of doing design that we call “Realdesign”. Thus, the aim is 
not so much to repeat the argument of the book, simply reiterating the ugly, yet 
incontrovertible facts of social life. Rather, this concluding chapter seeks to distill a 
set of principles from which to do design in an imperfect world. Again, doing so 
requires us to face the realist challenge outlined at the start of the argument: How 
to ﬁnd a realism not bogged down with impossibilities, ﬁnd a utopianism that is 
not moralist, and reclaim the creative element inherent in realism. 
Note 
1	 There is a further irony here: The 2001 Reclaim the City party was not staged in an 
urban space colonized by corporate power, but in a public park. One can imagine how 
Olof Palme, the late and great social democrat prime minister, would have relished this 
fact – just as he did during the 1968 occupation of the Stockholm University student 
union building. Addressing the protesting students, the then Minister of Education 
quipped: “I was told that you have occupied your own house?” 

2 
THE REALIST CHALLENGE 
Power and possibilities 
The design critique at the School of Design and Crafts in Gothenburg was just like 
any other of the classic format I had earlier encountered at other design schools. 
The students had prepared projects about future scenarios around the theme of 
mobility in the city. Posters and models were set up along the walls of the studio 
and the class professors were browsing through the little exhibit of materials before 
the presentations began. The mood was polite, even if the students had turned 
even more apprehensive as the visiting architecture professor from Chalmers Uni­
versity of Technology solemnly dressed in black turned up, followed by a visiting 
guest professor and a planner from the city. Now, this was no longer just the 
average class crit. With outsiders coming in to evaluate and give feedback, the 
stakes of projects suddenly turned more real. 
The presentations followed the usual format. Projects explored transportation 
in the city, from tram accessibility, transformed parking spaces, and public bike-
sharing systems. Students presented their situation and the research they had 
done, followed by ideas and models, highlighting how their design process had 
evaluated many options to ﬁnally reach and render what they considered the best 
outcome. The renderings showed something looking almost as if it existed at the 
chosen place in the city; the more high-resolution and realistic the presentations 
the easier it was to recognize and evaluate the pros and cons of the design. Some 
projects were more critical, questioning the brief, challenging some parameters, 
making something barely functional, but suggesting interesting twists and turns 
on the principles guiding the design process. But in the presentations, the students 
all said a variant of the same formula; “look here, there is an issue, I researched 
the concerns of these stakeholders, made these surveys, here are some ways to 
frame and address the issue, and this design process shows a preferred suggestion 
to redesign the situation”. 
DOI: 10.4324/9781003281443-2 

The realist challenge 17 
The critique followed a similar script of habit. The class teacher was mainly 
chairing and ﬁlling in details as the visiting professors were given the opportunity to 
give their feedback. Following the considerate traditions of Scandinavian design 
education, most comments were carefully packed in critique sandwiches. First, 
some positive words to hold on to (“interesting”), then a layer of meatier skepti­
cism, perhaps smeared with a leading question and a salad of sustainability (“have 
you really thought about X and Y?”), and then ﬁnished with another constructive 
layer of a helping hand, suggesting possible improvements (“perhaps you could 
think in this way?”). Some issues concerned the choice of materials and techniques, 
pointed to inclusion and possible obstacles, others raised awareness to budgetary 
limitations and city plans and protocols. Even some projects of less substance, or 
presentations with the most distressed students, got some constructive feedback to 
bring home. Fellow students ﬁlled in with encouraging and enthusiastic questions, 
paying attention to social and civic conditions with the transport scenarios. In the 
end, everybody thanked and made polite comments about the projects and the 
constructive outcome of the class as they left. Everywhere, even if not everything 
seemed equally possible to realize for the city; empathy and win-win was the 
mantra of the day. It is humbling to think of all good design can do. Sure, a lot of 
issues in the world are ﬂawed because of clumsy design, but they are just oppor­
tunities waiting to be ﬁxed by a new design. Designers sleep well at night. They do 
important work, bettering the world. In the end, the arch of history may be long, 
but it bends towards good design. 
What was curiously lacking in the critique was the presence of competing 
interests. All the ideas presented were projected against a backdrop of how vir­
tuous public transport is (which most present in the room would probably agree). 
As students presented bike-sharing systems, it was as if the launch of this service 
was simply a matter of convincing already good citizens to get habituated to do 
the right thing, simply making biking more user-friendly. Curiously, for a design 
critique taking place in the hometown of Volvo, no one in the room was taking 
into account the interests of the car industry, the fossil fuel industry, or the lobby 
organizations working for the prioritization of car traﬃc in city planning. It was as 
if the whole room had tacitly agreed on sidelining, or at least ignoring, the most 
vested and dollar-rich interests in the region and globally. Virtuous designers had 
simply to persuade users to start collaborating with these new services, as if the 
best argued and presented project will win in the end. 
What stands out in the presentations and critiques, and still plagues most project 
critiques we have participated in, is the lack of a language to address competing 
interests and power dynamics in design projects. Some projects may point to a 
“weakness” or “threat” (in the SWOT analysis), or a possible competitor. But in 
general, the preferred solution will win, if only consumers are convinced they get 
their value propositions delivered, or the goods and services are of the quality and 
function they desire. Even projects that act in the “real world” can be realized 
when the project pitch is made with the right partners. There may be some 

18 The realist challenge 
friction, but no real opposition to a design. How could there be? The new design is 
virtuous, it is the ideal solution to the issue. In the end, designers are the good 
guys, aren’tt h e y ? 
Design projects, even those with critical or political aspirations, work in worlds seen 
through idealist-tainted glasses. The world may be corrupt, citizens betrayed, and 
politicians hypocritical. Capitalism may be cr u e l a n dl a w su n j u s t , b u td e s i g ni saf o r c eo f 
good, attuned to the dire needs of the needy through the tools of empathy and design 
thinking. And by being so concerned with the good, there is little need to take into 
account the unholy compromises and dirty laundry of real power play. It is as if design 
students spend most of their education dipped in a sentimentality that says if there are 
obstacles, it is simply because people have not yet understood the undeniable virtues of 
my idea. I just need to work a bit more on my model, PowerPoint presentation or 
elevator pitch. We only work with carrots, because we are good. It is those others, the 
bad guys, that come with the whips. Design is always only a matter of conviction, 
never enforcement. This is at least the story designers tend to tell themselves. 
The realist challenge 
What the scenario above points to is a realist challenge to the idealism that serves 
as the dominant paradigm within contemporary scholarship on design. During 
the argument, it will be evident that there are sound reasons why design scholars 
and design practitioners tend to adopt an idealist position. Indeed, while an una­
bashed idealist may well choose to become a designer, it is more unlikely that a 
doctrinaire realist would engage with design. Idealism trades in possibilities, causing 
arch-realists to accuse them of being naïve about whether those possibilities are 
attainable, and about the outcomes of realizing idealist ambitions. Realism, on the 
other hand, trades in impossibilities, in highlighting the limited space of the fea­
sible, pointing to all the ways in which the “real” world crushes the ambitions of 
the idealist. Constantly obsessing about power, and the ﬂawed conditions and 
relations between human beings, the arch-realist is accused of being hopelessly 
cynical. Indeed, political realism is highly contested, even loathed, for this sup­
posedly cruel cynicism. Michael Williams states: 
To some, being a Realist represents the height of wisdom: the mark of a 
clear-sighted ability to understand the world the way it is, a willingness to 
confront the dynamics of power and interest that are held to govern world 
politics. To others, Realism is a mark of failure: morally obtuse and his­
torically anachronistic, it represents a lack of political understanding and 
imagination that is misleading at best, pernicious and destructive at worst. 
(Williams 2005: 1) 
This rendering of the idealist-realist debate may suggest that design thought, as a 
creative and visionary endeavor, invariably must be anti-realist. Indeed, such a 

The realist challenge 19 
proposition seems plausible when browsing the classics thinkers of design. Note 
for instance Herbert Simon’s touchstone for distinguishing design sciences from 
the natural sciences: Whereas the “natural sciences are concerned with how 
things are”, design “is concerned with how things ought to be” (1996: 114). 
Thus, natural sciences seem to be inherently realist, while design sciences must be 
inherently idealist. Note also how design is wedded to the notion of intentionality 
in contemporary interpretations of design thinking, where empathy becomes the 
main skill of the designer to attune their inquiry with the needs and ambitions of 
the users. Again, given that the arch-realist cares only about outcomes, and is 
skeptical towards the virtue of good intentions, one may surmise that design 
thinking must be antithetical to realism. Finally, note Buckminster Fuller’s famous 
words of encouragement, directed at innovators: “Dare to be naïve”. Surely, that 
clinches the argument against realism within design thought? Doesn’t realism just 
hamper the visionary possibilities of design? 
It is essential to not limit realism to a conservation of the status quo and zero-sum 
games. While these are recurring traits, they are not the only prism of the realist 
perspective. Both idealists and realists engage in games of designs and imagination. As 
realist thinker Raymond Geuss (2010) highlights, imagination stands behind all forms 
of politics, spanning from recurring ideas such as a “natural order” to the triumph of 
“justice”. What drives politics, Geuss argues (and we could certainly add design 
here), is “the fantasy of [the world’s] plasticity, of a potentially inﬁnite process of 
change and improvement, of the unlimited transformation of the world into some­
thing more perfect and more to our taste” (Geuss 2010: viii). While it may be easy to 
think that conserving the current order of things does not require creativity, Geuss 
puts it the other way: That “even the deepest kind of political conformism and any 
defense of the status quo require acts of imagining of some kind, albeit a particular 
kind of productive imagination” (Geuss 2010: x). Humanity keeps inventing new 
brute realities; they are not reserved to Hobbes’ natural condition of an imagined 
past. And design certainly plays an important role in the execution of brutality. 
As we will see, there are constructive ways to present the realist challenge to 
design thought. This is because elements of realist thought already reside within 
contemporary design thought. Moreover, the idealist and realist schools of 
thought are more nuanced than a quick introduction may suggest. The debate 
between realists and idealists in political thought has its disciplinary home in two 
related ﬁelds: Political theory and international relations. While those two ﬁelds 
are distinct and do feature somewhat diﬀerent approaches to realism, the inter­
disciplinary conﬂicts between the two ﬁelds are not of major relevance to the 
main argument of this book. Moreover, there are schisms and fractions, such as 
realists standing against neorealists, debating if the impossibilities originate within 
the nature of human beings, or from without, in relations and power games. In a 
similar vein, defensive realists argue against oﬀensive realists, tying their perspec­
tives to the many driving forces behind security competition in the unpredictable 
anarchy of international political systems where intentions and capabilities are 

20 The realist challenge 
obscured and gamed with. With its aﬃrmative and constructive stance, design easily 
misses the competitive and dangerous world of realism: Uncertain to discern the 
true intentions and capacities behind the actions of its constituent units, and under 
the social varnish of cooperation, there lies a layer of uncertainty, if not fear. 
Our point is not to frame design within any of these speciﬁc realist schools, but 
bring attention to the blindness design shares with idealist perspectives, a wilful 
ignorance and rejection of power politics in liberal dreaming of universal progress. 
Just to give an example, a poignant observation of oﬀensive neorealist John Mear­
sheimer (2018) is how the international politics driven by the US liberal agenda has 
traditionally strived to remake the world in its image (such as protecting human 
rights, establishing democratic systems, promoting peace), but has in reality become 
a highly militarized state ﬁghting global wars that undermine peace, harm human 
rights, and even cripples liberal values also within its borders. In a similar vein, the 
promotion of design as a liberal progressive method for development also rebounds 
to undermine the civic agency it sets out to promote. We will return to this in the 
coming chapters. 
As a general framework, realism is associated with the dark arts of politics laid 
out by Machiavelli, as well as with the unscrupulous international dealings of 
statesmen that is labeled realpolitik. This chapter will later return to the notion of 
realpolitik, and ﬁrst introduce realism by way of Machiavelli. Who, then, was 
Machiavelli? Here is one introduction: 
Machiavelli, a republican at heart, established the foundations of democracy 
in his Discourses on the First Decade of Livy. In spite of this he is often taken as 
a dangerous and amoral cynic because he wrote The Prince. In practice, 
however, the two works are one and the same: if democracy is to be stable 
the harsh realities of power have to be understood. 
(Latour 1988) 
In these opening sentences for his well-cited chapter on Machiavelli, Bruno Latour 
captures two key points of entry into the thought of the Florentine diplomat and 
political theorist. The most obvious one relates to Machiavelli’s reputation for being 
a cynic for pointing out the harsh realities of power. Indeed, writing in the early 
sixteenth century, he was an early exponent of understanding politics studying the 
“eﬀectual truth” of political life, rather than our “imagination of it” (Machiavelli 
1998: 61). While this approach may seem reasonable, it nevertheless clashed with 
the dominant view of his contemporaries, who argued that politics must invariably 
start from high-minded ideals; from the cardinal virtues. As we shall see, the pro­
position that politics should be understood in terms of ideals remains prevalent 
today. Secondly, the quote highlights the seeming contradiction between The 
Prince – a manual for power wielders, addressed to a monarch – and the post­
humously published Discourses, which is generally read as a founding text of 
republicanism. This disparity has been debated among scholars: Did Machiavelli’s 

The realist challenge 21 
political views change over time, or did he simply lack solid political convictions? 
If, like Latour suggests, he was always “ar e p u b l i c a n a t h e a r t”, was he insincere 
when writing The Prince, under political pressure to please the ruling monarch? For 
Latour – and for realist authors such as Leo Strauss – there is no contradiction to 
explain. The two works are presenting the same argument, representing diﬀerent 
perspectives on the same essential truth. The democratic order rests on a clear 
understanding of how power really operates. Obversely, a reluctance to accept the 
harsh realities of power represents a threat to the democratic order. 
Thus, as political scientist Eckard Bolsinger puts it, realists like Machiavelli insist 
on highlighting the “unpleasant” and “somber” sides of politics, and “bluntly point 
to the central structural features of modern power and politics that are normally 
hidden in a myriad of moral and legal justiﬁcations” (Bolsinger 2001: xv). Using 
Lenin and Carl Schmitt as further examples of realist thinkers, Bolsinger points to 
the uncomfortable necessity for political thought to confront its critics: 
Much as one might reject [Lenin’s and Schmitt’s] views of politics and state 
as politically dangerous or morally repelling, political theory has to come to 
grips with the problems that they raised and the solutions that they oﬀered if 
it wants to understand the whole experience of political life. 
(Bolsinger 2001: xvi) 
In contrast, idealists – which political philosopher Bernard Williams refers to as 
“moralists”– base their analysis of politics on moral claims related to terms like justice 
and rights. Thus, they represent “the priority of the moral over the political,” making 
“the moral prior to the political” (Williams 2005: 2). Moralist political theory can 
thus be understood as a form of “applied moral philosophy” or “a branch of legal 
philosophy”. Realism, on the other hand, sets oﬀ from use of “distinctively political 
concepts, such as power, and its normative relative, legitimation” ( 7 7 ) .T h i sd o e sn o t 
mean that realists are uninterested in moral issues, or that moralists are uninterested in 
power – the demarcation between the two indicates where the moralist and realist 
start their analysis of political life. It also indicates what categories – power and 
legitimation, or rights and justice – they choose to emphasize. “To think politically”, 
political philosopher Raymond Geuss suggests (2010: 53), “is to think about agency, 
power, and interests, and the relations between these”. 
Given that categories such as justice and rights tend to be general and all-
encompassing, moralists tend to lean towards a universalist understanding of 
political virtues. Realists, on the other hand, tend to pay closer attention to 
the particular situation in which politics unfolds. Thus, moralists believe that 
general conceptions of justice and rights cut across local speciﬁcities related to 
particular power arrangements. Realists, on the other hand, insist that the 
particularities of a given power relation override universalist moral claims. 
Thus, the political truths are valid, as Bernard Williams phrases it, “now and 
around here” (Williams 2005: 8). 

22 The realist challenge 
More speciﬁcally, they argue that universalist concepts always have a history – 
such abstractions always emerge at speciﬁc points in time, for speciﬁcr e a s o n s – and 
that their supposed “naturalness” can be interrogated through a genealogical 
method. To take a famous example in design, Victor Papanek’si nﬂuential book 
Design for the Real World (1971/1985), has overall been primarily read as a discourse 
and method of design turning away from the narrow demands of capitalism and the 
fabricated desires of consumerism, towards the real needs of people in the urgencies 
of the everyday. In their eﬀorts to dismantle modernist technocratic hierarchies and 
beaux arts aesthetics, a new generation of students read Papanek as a prophet for a 
design with a humanist face. But as elegantly suggested by Alison Clarke (2021), 
Papanek’s “real” needs also align with a turn in political conceptions of power at 
the time; a turn from militaristic means for power projection, towards the soft 
values of humanitarianism and social design as fronts for liberal and US-dominated 
power dynamics. By turning design from merely a provider of rational goods 
for the people into a social and moral judgment, the inherent virtues of 
design appeared at center stage, suggesting true design being democratic and 
anti-totalitarian, thus playing along as yet another theater of conﬂict in the 
cold war. Under the guise of idealist designs, Papanek’s approach implicitly 
suggests a turn for design institutions towards a Realist world. 
In practice, moralists and realists see institutions in a diﬀerent light. For the 
moralist, the institutional arrangements of practical politics can be derived from 
moral principles. In other words, institutions are primarily understood as means to 
realize more abstract conceptions of justice. For realists, institutional frameworks 
are more than mere instruments for the realization of ideals. Institutions, they 
argue, can in themselves inﬂuence the trajectories of political life. Crucially, 
institutions too have histories, and may take on a life of their own, shaping poli­
tical agendas, cementing power structures, and so on. Given that institutions have 
such lives of their own – outside moral categories – the purposes of an institution 
may drift over time. More speciﬁcally, the institution may be corrupted, and may 
eventually perish. 
The ﬁnitude of political institutions is also evident in the anti-utopian sentiments 
of realism. Rather than looking forward to the positives that the future may bring, 
the realist fears all the negatives. The realist thus emphasizes the possibility for poli­
tical regress, which means that the securing of order – rather than seeking justice – 
represents the “ﬁrst question” of politics. Again, realists are not dismissing the virtue 
of justice: Rather, their anti-utopian inclinations cause them to believe that the 
sacriﬁcing of order when seeking justice may produce disastrous outcomes. Conse­
quently, realists are skeptical towards good intentions that do not deliver good out­
comes. This skepticism towards mere good intentions ultimately stems from a 
pessimistic view of human nature. Realists see humans are fundamentally ﬂawed, 
arguing that political developments are driven by the protagonists’ passions and 
emotions, rather than the rationality that idealists tend to assume. Moreover, when 
rationality prevails, the realists would question the extent to which rationalism can 

The realist challenge 23 
tame the wild contingency of the real world. Given that humans are seen as driven 
by “baser” motives, realists see the political as a sphere characterized by dissensus and 
conﬂict. Again, this can be contrasted with the idealist concept of the political as a 
space for consensus-seeking. 
The realist is, however, not turning a blind eye to the question of intentions. 
Certainly, intentions are needed for action to take place. But the realist is mindful 
that intentions are merely the direction or vision that leads action – it is the 
outcomes that count. Here, we may revisit – and revise – Papanek’s model of the 
design problem. While designers traditionally focus on intentions, rendering their 
best visions as convincing as possible, the realist takes the ﬂawed conditions of 
human relations into account to make sure outcomes are best assured. A realign­
ment of the vision of the designer is needed; a focus on outcomes engenders a 
sober perspective on power. Thus, Papanek’s triangle may be redrawn as follows: 
Intentions 
Outcomes 
FIGURE 2.1 The intentions or outcomes problem. 
We may now return to Machiavelli. The points above – the interest in the 
particular institutional arrangements, the fear of regress, the ﬁnitude of institu­
tions, the focus on securing order, the emphasis on outcomes over good inten­
tions – are all evident in Machiavelli’s thought. As argued by Pocock (1975: viii), 
his work can be grasped through the triad of virtue (virtú), fortune (fortuna), and 
corruption (corruzione). Virtue is that which is employed in order to counter two 
threats – the element of chance introduced by fortune, and the general tendency 
towards decay and corruption of institutions. Therefore, virtue is not a matter of 

24 The realist challenge 
possessing the excellence of character, in the Aristotelian sense of the word 
(Mansﬁeld 1996: 45). Rather, it responds to the situation at hand, in particular 
the power relations invested in the situation. Thus, virtue is something “alert, on 
the make; it is not a habit”. Virtue is never in and of itself, but exists for the sake 
of something outside of virtue. In response to the threat of decay and unintended 
chance events, true virtue in politics is to know when to break with general 
conceptions of good deeds. A fourth concept that is central to Machiavelli’s 
thought is innovation (innovazione). Ultimately, The Prince is “an analytic study of 
innovation and its consequences” (Pocock 1975: 156), inasmuch as it assesses the 
possibilities and threats facing an innovative prince. The upside of innovation is 
that it may assist virtue in imposing form on fortune. The downside, however, is 
that the introduction of an innovation is an inherently “uncontrolled act having 
uncontrolled consequences in time” (156). In other words, the innovating prince 
may reintroduce the perilousness of fortuna. As we shall see in Chapter 4, the 
disruptive potential of innovation is to be treated with care and caution, as it may 
come with unseen corrosive eﬀects. 
Bruno Latour, who introduced Machiavelli above, makes a similar argument in 
one of his contributions to design theory. Calling for an abandonment of the “odd 
idea of mastery that refused to include the mystery of unintended consequences” 
(Latour 2008: 6), he suggests that designers could be the harbingers of a “cautious” 
Prometheus approach to creation. Though political realism constitutes the main 
theoretical toolbox of this book, the work of Latour – and some of his collabora­
tors within science and technology studies (STS) – will be drawn upon as a second 
source of concepts and insights. This choice is hardly unorthodox: Latour has made 
his own contributions to design theory, and some of these broader concepts are 
drawn upon within the design discipline. This holds particularly true for the Scan­
dinavian participative design tradition, speciﬁcally the work on “design things” in 
the inﬂuential works of Pelle Ehn (2008). 
Though not strictly a realist in the political theory sense of the word, Latour is 
certainly no stranger to the classics within the realist canon. As suggested above, 
Machiavelli featured prominently in his early work on science and technology 
studies. Moreover, as Graham Harman states in his book on Latour as a political 
philosopher, Hobbes was his earliest source of politico-philosophical inspiration 
(Harman 2014: 5). Harman goes on to argue that there is “a realist note that is 
clearly audible throughout his political theory” (15), and cites Latour’s assertion 
that in political philosophy, “reactionary thinkers are more interesting than the 
progressive ones” (Latour, Harman, & Erdélyi 2011: 96). Harman is careful to 
stress that Latour himself is no reactionary, yet still remains interesting. Still, the 
realist label is problematic in relation to Latour’s work, which is also engaged in a 
debate on the merits of metaphysical realism. Harman nevertheless presents an 
alternative distinction – truth politics versus power politics – through which he 
describes Latour’s default position as power-political. In this view, politics cannot 
be understood through referencing a transcendent truth, because how truths are 

The realist challenge 25 
claimed are in themselves outcomes of political struggles. Further, Harman 
distinguishes three phases in Latour’s political philosophy: The early Latour is 
inspired by Hobbes and Machiavelli; the middle Latour draws upon Walter 
Lippmann and John Dewey; and the late Latour involves an inquiry into a 
distinct political mode of existence. Whereas previous work on Latour and 
design – notably that of Ehn and his collaborators – has mobilized the 
“middle Latour” notion of the parliament of things, this book will tend to 
engage with ideas from his earlier writings. 
The idealism of design thought 
Given the increased interest for design to engage with and reshape social matters, it 
is crucial for designers to ﬁnd ways of accounting for the deceptive, unpredictable, 
and recalcitrant nature of this social reality. On this point, the realist position within 
political theory may serve as a useful resource – especially as design thought tends 
to be aligned with the alternative, idealist position. To further explicate how ide­
alism serves as the dominant perspective of design, it is worth returning to Herbert 
Simon’si nﬂuential text on the sciences of the artiﬁcial. Indeed, a series of idealist 
propositions may be inferred from Simon’s oft-cited idea that a designer is someone 
who devises courses of action aimed at changing existing situations into preferred 
ones (Simon 1996: 111). 
One should not read Simon’s comment as isolated to design in a narrow sense, 
but across the practices of innovation and the shaping of the world according to 
human intentions. In Engineering the Revolution, Ken Alder’s (1997) study of the 
signiﬁcance of rationalism, engineering, and arms production in the French 
Revolution, he notes that the relation between engineering and revolution is in 
one sense unsurprising. For “after all, in the broadest sense, engineering is perhaps 
the quintessential revolutionary activity” (15). “In principle”, he notes, “engi­
neering operates on a simple but radical assumption: that the present is nothing 
more than the raw material from which to construct the future”. 
Using Simon’s statement as a point of departure, infused by the radical notion 
of the plasticity of the world, one may raise four questions that highlight key 
points of contention between idealist and realist approaches, as applied within the 
practical realm of design. 
First of all, as hinted in the beginning of the previous section, there is the 
fundamental question whether change is possible or impossible. Or, more subtly 
put, to what extent is the social reality – and the place of “man” (or the human) in this rea­
lity – malleable? Here, the realist outlook can partly be traced back to a pessimistic 
view of human nature. The realists reject Rousseau’s assumption of the presocietal 
noble savage. In the view of international politics scholar Hans Morgenthau, idealists 
believe in the “essential goodness” and “inﬁnite malleability” of human nature. 
Realists, on the other hand, see the world as “the result of forces inherent in human 
nature”, and thus politics needs to acknowledge rather than ignore these forces, 

26 The realist challenge 
however disagreeable they may be on an ethical level (Morgenthau 1967: 3). A 
similar perspective is found in the work of Lippmann (1913), who uses Freudian 
theory to suggest that rivaling and antagonistic human instincts turn society into a 
psychological battleﬁeld, constantly on the brink of civil war. Freud’s discovery, 
Lippmann famously suggested, ought to prompt an overhaul of political institutions, 
based on the brute facts of man, rather than on the idealistic and romantic dreams of 
reinstituting Greek democracy. 
However, for other realists, the deep “nature” of man is irrelevant. Rather, given 
that the social environment is inherently unstable and unpredictable, actors fall back 
on their self-interest. John Herz, another international relations scholar, argues: 
Whether man is ‘by nature’ peaceful and cooperative, or aggressive or dom­
ineering, is not the question. The condition that concerns us here is not an 
anthropological or biological, but a social one. It is his uncertainty and
```
