anxiety as to his neighbor’s intentions that places man in this basic dilemma, 
and makes the ‘homo homini lupus’ [‘man is wolf to man’] a primary fact of 
the social life of man. 
(Herz 1951: 3) 
Irrespective of the origins of self-interest, designers must take this brute fact into 
account when taking on the role of a change agent. Machiavelli’s words of warn­
ing – the innovator “has all those who beneﬁtf r o m t h e o l do r d e r s a s e n e m i e s” 
(Machiavelli 1998: 23) – may have attained cliché status among innovation scholars. 
Nevertheless, the idea of human nature as selﬁsh or otherwise ﬂawed does not fea­
ture in this literature. Simon, on his end, discusses selﬁshness in relation to the sup­
posedly rational and optimizing behavior of the homo economicus of the economic 
sciences. In other words, the idea of a political selﬁshness akin to that of Machiavelli 
does not feature in his commentary on the design of the “evolving artefact” (Simon 
1996: 139) called society. 
Secondly, there is a question to be asked regarding the issue of moving from 
the “existing” to the “preferred”: How do we know that preferred is better than what is 
currently existing? This requires the existence of a clear conception of virtue or of 
“the good”. It may also imply an end goal, or at least the presumption of an arc 
of “progress”, implicitly in line with Martin Luther King’s saying that “the arc of 
the moral universe is long, but it bends towards justice”. In contrast, as shown in 
the previous section, Machiavelli’s view of virtue denotes a capacity to stave oﬀ 
the element of chance, as well as decay. There can never be a virtue in itself; 
there can only be techniques to prevent the worst from happening. Similarly, the 
anti-utopian sentiments of realists cause them to settle for order and a modus 
vivendi – an arrangement for peaceful coexistence (at least to some degree) – 
rather than aim for speciﬁc end goals, or belief in the necessity of progress. 
Alternatively, a conception of the “preferred” may be arrived at through rational 
deliberation. As also mentioned in the previous section, the realist would point to 

The realist challenge 27 
the limits of deliberative rationality. Ultimately, the political realm is conﬂict-ridden, 
thriving on passionate emotions rather than consensual-seeking rationality. As Geuss 
points out, “politics is a matter of human, not merely mechanical, interaction 
between individuals, institutions, or groups” (Geuss 2008: 1). These factors disturb 
the notion of a readily accessible and easily agreeable conception of what is preferred. 
Thirdly, also in relation to the “preferred”, the realist may ask: Preferred by whom? 
For a realist like Geuss, the primordial political question is that posted by Lenin: Who 
whom? Geuss unpacks this question into: “Who <does> what to whom for whose 
beneﬁt?” (Geuss 2008: 26). This opens the ﬁeld for an interrogation of who holds 
power, of who is subjected to this power, and – crucially – of the oblique interests 
that are enacted in this power relation. That type of interrogation tends to be skated 
over in Simon’s meditation on design, where the preferred is discussed in relation to 
the distinction between optimizing and satisﬁcing rationality. More crucially, societal 
power relations tend to be suﬀused through the language of “the client”;t h e “who 
whom?” question is expressed as “who is the client?” (Simon 1996: 150). Inciden­
tally, the “who whom?” question may also be directed against the seemingly neutral 
design dictum “form follows function”. Again, who decides what function is proper, 
and in whose interests? 
As suggested by Geuss’ query, it may be that the idea of simply trying to meet 
the needs of a client obscures the fact that there are indirect forces at play in the 
designer-client relation. 
Finally, there is a fourth question: Can design be a truly universalist endeavor? 
Given that designers are expected to produce “good” or “preferable” outcomes, 
there is an implicit understanding that the advancement of design into the social 
realm follows liberal ideals of the public good; the universal good. Again, recall 
Rossetti’s suggestion that “universal design is good design”– a proposition that 
jars with the realist skepticism towards political thinking as “applied ethics” 
(Geuss 2008: 6). The universalism of universal design’s call to design “for all” is 
an ethical as much as practical promise. The “for all” suggests the aim is to facil­
itate the dissemination of design leverage to those usually excluded from design. 
By virtue of being for all, it also suggests the leverage is equally distributed across 
the population: Bringing the consent of “all” under a universal law of design. 
On this point, there are two factors that complicate matters. First, there is the 
practical matter of designers usually working on commission from a client. This 
client has preferences, and it is the designer’s job to fulﬁll them. Simon hints at 
this problem in relation to the notion of “society as the client” (Simon 1996: 
153). However, there is a more profound issue at stake here. As Nietzsche sug­
gests in the Genealogy of Morals (1994 [1887]), imposing one law equally for the 
vulture and the lamb can easily mean death for all the lambs. This has practical 
consequences for the universal imperative of design “for all”: It may also become 
a way to treat all users or participants equally; both the strong and the weak, that 
is, both vultures and lambs. As suggested by Geuss (2008), many forms of inclu­
siveness may come at the cost of the lambs, as the very formula of vague equality 

28 The realist challenge 
hides the unequal distribution of agency and leverage, and eﬀectively fails to 
recognize those who may need it the most. Thus, the realist perspective upsets 
the very notion of a design that can be universally good. 
Cultural theorist Mark Fisher frames another type of realism than that of the 
tradition of political realists, yet with results which overlap in anti-idealist cyni­
cism. Fisher argues for a reframing of the late-capitalist or post-modern cultural 
condition as a “capitalist realism”, which he paints as “the widespread sense that 
not only is capitalism the only viable political and economic system, but that it is 
now impossible to even imagine a coherent alternative to it” (Fisher 2009: 2). 
Following Badiou, Fisher shows how this condition is not only a consequence of 
the “end of history” after the fall of the Berlin Wall, but also through a con­
tinuous political discourse that paints the current socioeconomic model as an 
ideal, even if ﬂawed. Nevertheless, even with its apparent failures, the current 
model is always better than any other could-have-been, 
To justify their conservatism, the partisans of the established order cannot 
really call it ideal or wonderful. So instead, they have decided to say that all 
the rest is horrible. Sure, they say, we may not live in a condition of perfect 
Goodness. But we’re lucky that we don’t live in a condition of Evil. Our 
democracy is not perfect. But it’s better than the bloody dictatorships. 
Capitalism is unjust. But it’s not criminal like Stalinism. […] The ‘realism’ 
here is analogous to the deﬂationary perspective of a depressive who believes 
that any positive state, any hope, is a dangerous illusion. 
(Fisher 2009: 5) 
This mood of spectatorial cynicism replaces rich possibilities of engagement and 
involvement with pre-conﬁgured avenues of action where limited possibilities are 
presented as the best that can be oﬀered. It is “post-political” in the sense of rob­
bing the horizon of possibilities of any depth. In the threat of passivity on the one 
hand, and Hobbsian war of all against on the other, all stakeholders prefer a curated 
possibility of engagement as a compromise. To “get real” under capitalist realism 
means to accept the conditions of not asking for too much, that is, an alternative to 
the status quo. This condition Fisher calls reﬂexive impotence, where people “know 
things are bad, but more than that, they know they can’t do anything about it. But 
that ‘knowledge,’ that reﬂexivity, is not a passive observation of an already existing 
state of aﬀairs. It is a self-fulﬁlling prophecy” (2009: 21). 
As a realism of capitalist constraint on the pathways towards the possible, 
Fisher’s vision of the state of aﬀairs is not Machiavellian in its perspective on 
power dynamics, but its result on participatory processes echo similar conditions. 
Participants are forced into strictly delineated rooms of action, where the over­
arching modus operandi cannot be put to question. Instead of debating utopia 
and building anew, participants are pushed into a zero-sum game where they 
come to struggle over limited resources or budgets and where the overall 

The realist challenge 29 
framework of the participatory situation is left beyond question. In a similar vein, the 
agency guided by the participants cannot be used against the political framework 
itself, or reframe the questions they are asked to address. 
From the argument presented so far, realism may come across as a killjoy 
philosophy that prohibits action and creation. Still, the purpose of this book is 
not to stiﬂe the constructive gesture of design, to propose a passive acceptance 
of hegemonic conspiracies, or to point to an impossibility for design to do 
anything at all. After all, as Tony Fry (1999, 2011) suggests, most design defu­
tures more than it makes possible, and so far, designers have spent most time 
structuring consumerist habit u st om a k eu sb eb o t ht h e p l a n e t’s and our own 
Schmittean enemy. In response to this challenge, the next section will present 
the positive agenda that realism may oﬀer. 
Towards real realpolitik 
At this point, the broad outlines of realism should be relatively clear. The realist 
perspective avoids the “fair weather” approach to politics which takes political 
order and stability as a given, and downplays the prevalence of conﬂicts and 
contestations. It assumes that humans are selﬁsh – or, alternatively, that they dare 
not act selﬂessly – and resigns to the fact that in many practical aspects of social 
life “might is right”. On a more abstract level, it is a concerted move away from 
the perspective of politics as a mere application of rational ethics and moral phi­
losophy. Thus, realism is interested in the brute facts of power, struggle, and 
violence, thus separating the political from the moral. 
However, as hinted above, it is wrong to assume that realism is uninterested in 
moral matters. For instance, Morgenthau shows how, following Hobbes, the 
security of the sovereign state as the fundamental presupposition of moral experi­
ence. That is, the “national interest” is a fundamental concern for security, and its 
pursuit is indispensable to the realization of moral life (Morgenthau 1951: 33). 
Moreover, realism is expressed in diﬀerent forms, which implies that there are 
several ways of accounting for the place of morals. Reinhold Niebuhr’s Christian 
realism is a “sober, not somber realism” with a modest faith in human progress 
(Kaufman 1996: 314). Though the world may be a bleak place, it is ultimately faith 
that impels us to engage in the struggle to make the world a better place. Michael 
Williams (2005) outlines a similar approach to realism, or what he calls “wilful 
realism”.I t h a s t h r e e d eﬁning features, the ﬁrst one being skepticism, which is a 
rational attitude questioning the limits of reason as a political force. Relationality is 
the second feature, which puts emphasis on how constitution, maintenance, and 
transformation shape the relations between political orders which are not necessarily 
in opposition but still guided by political principles of diﬀerence. Thirdly, power 
politics is central to understanding any form of realism, engaging with the eﬀective 
mobilization and exercise of material power. This includes the clashing of multiple 
forms of knowledge claims, forms of subjectivity, and structures of authority and 

30 The realist challenge 
action. Wilful realism posits that the recognition of the centrality of power in pol­
itics does not imply a reduction of politics to pure power, or to the capacity to 
wield violence. It seeks, on the contrary, a politics of limits that recognizes the 
destructive and productive dimensions of politics, and that maximizes its positive 
possibilities while minimizing its destructive potential (Williams 2005: 7). 
A positive program for action may also be found if one ventures from realist 
political theory to the realism of the international relations discipline. In the 
context of the latter, the term realpolitik tends to be associated with the unscru­
pulous statecraft of Henry Kissinger. However, this use of the term is a mis­
representation of its original meaning. In fact, the term was coined in the pursuit 
of moral objectives, by August Ludwig von Rochau, a disillusioned liberal in 
mid-nineteenth century Germany. He too had been “mugged by reality”:H e 
had seen the outcomes of the failed liberal revolution of 1848, and interpreted it 
as a warning example of aimless enthusiasm, misused idealism, and deluded naï­
veté. For him, the revolution represented a mix of hubris and idealism, where 
political romantics “dreamed up superstructures but forgot the base” (Bew 2016: 
26). Crucially, von Rochau agreed with the liberal enlightenment idea that there 
is no moral foundation for the proposition that might is right. However, he did 
not believe that the real world of politics had changed, so as to accommodate these 
new ideals. The power of the strong had been shown to be unjust, but still – as he 
writes in his Grundsätze der Realpolitik (1853) – the law of power governs the world 
of states just as the law of gravity governs the physical world. In practice, sovereignty 
is not a right, but a result of power. It cannot be contracted out to a constitution, as 
power only obeys greater power. 
Given these origins of the term, historian John Bew (2016) thus argues that 
realpolitik should not be understood as the crude and unscrupulous exercise of 
power politics. “Real realpolitik”, as Bew calls it, was crafted as a practical tool for 
coping with the contradictory political problems produced by modernity; 
a combustion of new ideas about liberty and social order alongside rapid 
industrialization, class antagonism, sectarianism, the rise of nationalism (in 
both civic and ethnic forms), and increasing international rivalry. 
(17) 
This was the context for which realpolitik was devised, and some of the character­
istics of that time seem to resonate with today’s world. It is therefore hardly surprising 
that “realpolitik is back in fashion – again” (1). After the fall of the Berlin wall and the 
idealism of the 1990s, history and realist politics has returned with a vengeance. In 
the wake of ideological exhaustion, economic downturn, military overstretch and a 
succession of crises, the “real” seems to usurp the “utopian” (3). 
Still, what does realpolitik oﬀer by way of a positive program for action? First, 
there is something liberating in the stark message of von Rochau. Consider the 
following: Realpolitik does not move in a foggy future but in the present’s ﬁeld 

The realist challenge 31 
of vision. It does not consider its task to consist in the realization of ideas, but in 
the attainment of concrete ends. And it knows, with reservations, to content itself 
with partial results, if their complete attainment is not achievable for the time 
being. Ultimately, von Rochau argues, Realpolitik is an enemy of all kinds of 
self-delusion. 
The focus on the here and now, the attentiveness to the matters at hand, and 
the lowering of expectations may be read as a call for practical action. Indeed, 
those who indulge in what von Rochau calls Gefuhlspolitik (sentimental politics) 
and Prinzipenpolitik (principled politics) run a considerably higher risk of becom­
ing disillusioned. Secondly, von Rochau couples his matter-of-factness with an 
outlook that does not get carried away with trying to negate the forces of the 
world. On the contrary, Realpolitik (real politics) seeks to harness them, reorient 
them, and use them for one’s own purposes. This implies that the cold-headed 
analysis of the forces of the world is not for analysis’ own sake. Rather, it merely 
provides the groundwork for the exercising of realpolitik as a modulation of 
emerging forces. Indeed, “the force which the state does not incorporate, will 
necessarily become its opponent” (von Rochau in Bew 2016: 37). Summing up 
von Rochau’s arguments, Bew (300) articulates four points that may form a 
schematic heuristic for coping with the real world: 
1.	 Who holds power in any given circumstances? Note that this is distinct from 
who claims sovereignty or the right to hold power. 
2.	 What are the social and economic conditions that underlie the political 
system and how are they changing the distribution of power? 
3.	 What is the prevailing cultural context and what are the most important 
ideological undercurrents of the society in question? 
4.	 Considering all these things together, what is the room aﬀorded for political 
action to forward one’s interests or one’s ideals, and what are the risks? 
Towards the end of this book, we will return to this heuristic, and the general 
question of how to act in the world. Though much of the argument will revolve 
around ways of grasping the nature of power in relation to design practices, the 
aim will be to translate this analysis into an assessment of the room aﬀorded for 
action, ultimately prompting creative action. Indeed, realist modes of creativity 
tend to be overlooked, as realists are routinely caricatured as blunt aﬃrmers of the 
“might is right” slogan. Here, there are two key points. For one, a political rea­
lism may be as “innovative” as idealism; the capacity for creation and imagination 
is not a purely idealist trait. As Geuss (2010) points out, one example may be how 
the Bush administration invented weapons of mass destruction – producing fancy 
renderings of chemical labs in trailers – in order to frame the attack on Iraq. 
History may be full of similar examples, and Geuss’ point is to put our attention 
to the creative agency of realist interventions. Realism need not necessarily be 
about preserving the status quo; it has the capacity to break out of its bias towards 

32 The realist challenge 
impossibilities, and actively produce new worlds. Geuss captures this realist 
imagination as a “distinct ability of practical imagination, inventiveness, or 
creativity, of coming up with new possibilities ” for politics that does not deny 
the potency of power (Geuss 2010: 14). 
Secondly, and crucially, this realist mode of creativity may be put to non-reactionary 
uses. As E.R. Carr (1946) suggests, there is a tendency to associate idealism with the 
visionary imagination of true utopianism. Instead, as Geuss posits in his 
interpretation of Carr, it is the inherent moralism of idealism that obscures its 
perspective. Thus, utopianism must be salvaged from its artiﬁcial constructs of 
ideal types, which are too bound to internal consistencies or aﬃnities, and 
instead be given more radical politicala g e n c yb yb e c o m i n g r e a l i s t ( G e u s s 
2016: 26f). We must – as Geuss suggests in his reading of Carr – start prac­
ticing a “utopian realism”, a context-bound and value-judging practice, 
instead of impotent and moralization under the guise of universalism. Such 
utopian realism in turn resonates well with von Rochau’s original realpolitik. 
This is not a realism that is limited to preserving the current state of aﬀairs, or 
the unimaginative condition of “being realistic”, not about accepting existing 
frameworks deﬁning the possible and impossible. A sensible realist project is 
“one of ‘wishful visions’ (rather than ‘wish-dreams’)” (Geuss 2016: 48). We 
will return to this argument in the ﬁnal chapter. 
As we will see in the coming chapters, realism oﬀers a sordid yet still imaginative 
perspective on the power dynamics design ties into. Whereas designers are taught 
to use design thinking to “empathize” with users, while still silently moralizing 
their irrational or debased behaviors to nudge them into a more virtuous direction; 
realism shifts perspective from the Gefuhlspolitik and Prinzipenpolitik of social and 
humanitarian-inspired design towards the possibilities and impossibilities that back 
up design initiatives with power. Our task ahead is to shed idealist blinders, and 
unpack the deceptive traps laid out by popular participatory design methods. 

3 
BETRAYAL 
Post-political participation 
There had been months of meetings and workshops: Mongrel teams of citizens, 
designers, architects, and planners had collaborated, fusing a multitude of ideolo­
gies and perspectives. An ambitious political initiative had radically opened the 
process of planning, with citizens actively working with architects to realize their 
visions. After all this work, the local newspapers were just about to present the 
citizens’ visions for the future Gothenburg. This was good news for democracy, 
and possibly also good news for the newspapers to report. 
Our team, consisting of architects, designers, and interested citizens, had agreed 
on a vision that in many ways questioned the brief. Not only were we making 
inclusive plans for the old harbor area of Gothenburg (enough of condos and café 
chains!) but also proposing bicycle routes over the water. We also set out to 
expand what kind of environments we could expect in the city. Thus, our team 
envisioned ﬂexible infrastructure that would support pop-up bazaars, street mar­
kets and citizen-run events, and we proposed that a segment of the old harbor 
should be turned into a bird habitat and wetland. Ideas of rewilding com­
plemented the ambition to unmake a bit of the rigid programming that is so 
predominant in planning. 
A radical idea had become a key element in our overall proposal; could a part of 
the area have its own constitution, a more ﬂexible program, allowing part of the area 
to remain open for planning dynamics over time? Like a free-trade zone, but for 
citizen-generated initiatives instead of corporations? Governed by a non-dividend 
public interest company, supplied with infrastructure that could support many types 
of civic activities? The whole team was very excited about the idea, and we made 
sure it had a signiﬁcant presence in the public presentation. 
So it came as a bit of a surprise when we read how the newspapers reported on 
the presentations from the diﬀerent teams. What had caught the attention of the 
DOI: 10.4324/9781003281443-3 

34 Betrayal 
journalists and experts was that our team had suggested creating a “swamp” in the 
harbor (Granath 2005: 82). How did our elaborate scheme we felt was so visionary 
become reduced to mud? How did the civic-driven process of participation 
become a morass of failed intentions? 
This chapter starts from an examination of existing streams of research within the 
participatory design (PD) tradition that invoke realist thinking, more speciﬁcally 
agonistic and adversarial design. The argument explores this in relation to the realist 
political theory that underpins it, and then pivots to the associated notion of post-
politics. When used in the context of design, this shift is signiﬁcant – rather than 
focusing on how design may be used as means to cultivate contestability, the 
chapter interrogates how co-design practices may actually suppress contestability. It 
thus examines how participative design and co-design – when used in the context 
of urban design – has been accused of being a post-political choreography. The 
chapter accepts the existence of post-political outcomes, but provides an alternative 
account of the mechanics of such outcomes. Drawing on the routes of investigation 
proposed by actor-network theory, and the theoretical notion of translation, it 
charts how policy-making – no matter how participative – involves a suppression 
of dissensus. It also studies how political representation – like any representation – 
inevitably involves a betrayal. 
Agonistic and adversarial design 
One of the key tensions between idealism and realism outlined in the two opening 
chapters of this book concerns the possibility for – and virtue of – achieving nor­
mative or practical consensus. This tension serves as a useful point of departure for 
explicating the stakes of political realism in the context of design, especially as there 
is already a considerable body of work within design that draws upon the idea 
that dissensus is central to politics. Indeed, over the past few decades there have 
been calls for the exposing of disagreement and so-called “agonism” in design, 
and critiques of the tacit consensus sought and desired by designers. Instead, this 
body of work suggests, designers must acknowledge disagreement and the 
contestation of interests. 
The notion of “agonism” is borrowed from political theorist Chantal Mouﬀe, a 
“leading arch-realist” (Galston 2010: 396) whose work can be understood through 
the delineation between “politics” and “the political”. While the former implies 
“the set of practices and institutions through which order is created”,t h e l a t t e r 
denotes “the dimension of antagonism” that she sees as “constitutive to human 
societies” (Mouﬀe 2005: 9). Whereas the institutionalized practices of formal poli­
tics tend to be studied by political scientists, political theorists debate the nature of 
the political. While some theorists see politics as a domain of freedom and public 
deliberation, for Mouﬀe it is a space of power and conﬂict. 
However, much the theorists of the mainstream “liberal” tradition wish to 
believe that such disagreement can be eradicated, she represents the realist 

Betrayal 35 
contention that it is naïve – indeed dangerous – to believe that this is possible. If 
dissensus is ignored or suppressed, it is bound to re-emerge in an uglier guise. For 
instance, Mouﬀe consistently strives to show how political arrangements based on 
centrist consensus that leaves little room for contestation tends to produce popu­
list or extremist movements. Here, there is a risk that respectful agonism – which 
for Mouﬀe serves as the basis of a properly functioning democracy – is usurped, 
and replaced by a violent antagonism that threatens to undermine the very 
foundation of democracy itself. So, democratic theorists and politicians must stop 
“trying to design the institutions which […] would reconcile all conﬂicting 
interests and values” and instead focus on “the creation of a vibrant ‘agonistic’ 
public sphere of contestation where diﬀerent hegemonic political projects can be 
confronted” (Mouﬀe 2005: 3). 
It is this ambition that design theorists have translated into notions of agonistic 
design and adversarial design. What would it mean for designers to distance them­
selves from the ideals of a cozy consensual world, and instead base their practice on a 
harsh reality of a world in disagreement? Can design disclose a world of power 
asymmetries hidden from view in seemingly consensual discourses, systems, and 
material arrangements? Can it expose wrongs that are committed within such 
arrangements, and put agreements to the test? Building on Mouﬀe’sa g o n i s t i c 
democracy, these scholars construe design as a powerful and colonial tool that wields 
power, which in turn needs to be questioned and resisted. An agonistic design 
practice hopes to expose tensions and contradictions to highlight how society is not 
based on tacit consensus – that all interested parties are agreeing, or that their inter­
relations run smoothly – and instead reveal how the designed object is a ‘thing’;a n 
assembly of conﬂicting parts, a product of imbalanced compromises (Björgvinsson, 
Ehn & Hillgren 2012b). Building on Noortje Marres and Bruno Latour, Ehn (2008) 
exposes how the thing is both an object and a parliament of diﬀerent opinions, 
coming together to address the political. The thing is thus a conﬂict of forces, wills, 
populations, aﬀects, trajectories, and ethics. Ehn orchestrates these trajectories into a 
process of contested ‘thinging.’ 
Similarly, design theorist Carl DiSalvo sees agonism as a central concept in his 
ideas of a practice of adversarial design. This notion implies a critical design 
practice that exposes inconsistencies and disagreements, and becomes a type of 
political design, based on contestation (DiSalvo 2012: 2). This also implies that 
design becomes an “inquiry into the political condition” (5). DiSalvo’s work 
chimes with Mouﬀe’s point about ﬁnding ways of preventing agonism from 
slipping into outright antagonism. Design may be used as a means to generate 
nonviolent discursive disagreement, which exists in a realm of the political. 
Antagonism is contained through discursive agendas in contestation with each 
other, even as these take physical form in design interventions and artifacts. 
Indeed, the ambition of agonism-inspired design scholars can be summed up with 
reference to how Evgeny Morozov (2013) introduces the work of DiSalvo. 
Bluntly put, the agonistic approach can be said to represent a shift in purpose of 

36 Betrayal 
design – beyond the creation of functional artifacts, design is also about creating 
“conversation starters” (Morozov 2013: 329). 
The agonistic critique of participatory design proposed by Björgvinsson, Ehn 
and Hillgren (2012b) also revolves around the creation of a discursively oriented 
space for contestation. Their approach thus focuses on the social process of par­
ticipation; the selection of stakeholders, and the techniques used for voicing 
dilemmas. Their agonistic participatory design thus implies 
a love towards practices where diﬀerences and controversies are allowed to 
exist, and dilemmas are raised and possibilities explored. The design 
researcher role becomes one of infrastructuring agonistic public spaces mainly 
by facilitating the careful building of arenas consisting of heterogeneous par­
ticipants, legitimizing those marginalized, maintaining network constellations, 
and leaving behind repertoires of how to organize social-materially when 
conducting innovative transformations. 
(Björgvinsson, Ehn & Hillgren 2012b: 143) 
These agonism-inspired design theorists have, however, been subject to criticisms. 
For instance, Markussen (2012) uses Jacques Rancière’s ideas about the politics of 
aesthetics as counterpoint in his critique of DiSalvo. So, while DiSalvo’sn o t i o n o f 
political design manages to “reveal and contest existing conﬁgurations and condi­
tions of society” (Markussen 2012: 43, italics added), this perspective neglects the 
aesthetico-political ambition proposed by Rancière: How design may make new 
voices audible and present new visibilities, and thus shift the power arrangement 
that Rancière (2004) calls “distribution of the sensible”.T h i s i m p l i e s a “policed 
order,” based on consensus and control over what is sensible, that “allocates roles 
and functions to people to keep order” (McCarthy & Wright 2015: 42). From this 
perspective, Markussen argues, it is evident that the agonism-inﬂuenced design of 
DiSalvo does not directly intervene in the composition of a given power arrange­
ment, which “manifests itself as a struggle between two or more groups that has as 
its goal a reordering of the relation of power between the existing groups” (Mar­
kussen 2012: 45). Thus, a more Rancière-oriented manner of thinking about 
political design would be to imagine design that could provide “a nonviolent 
unsettling of the self-evidence, with which existing systems of power can control 
and restrict the unfolding of our everyday behavior and interaction”. 
While relating to Rancière’s work, this chapter will not pursue this line of 
critique. Rather, it will continue from the work of Ehn and DiSalvo, but reorient 
the gaze. First, it will shift focus – from hopes of participative design being 
mobilized for supporting contestability, to fears of participative design being 
mobilized for quelling contestability. Indeed, as hinted above, much of Mouﬀe’s 
work seeks to show how contestability is suppressed, and outline the contours of 
the “post-political” Zeitgeist that haunts contemporary liberal democracies. 
Second, drawing upon actor-network theory, it will shift the attention from the 

Betrayal 37 
level of the discursive realm, to a more situated exploration of the circulation of a 
more general set of representations. 
Post-politics 
What does the term ‘post-politics’ entail? The term – which Žižek (1999: 248) 
attributes to Rancière, who in turn denies ever using it (Rancière 2009: 116) – 
designates a stiﬂing politics of consensus that has emerged from the 1990s 
onwards. After the proverbial ‘end of history’, the post-politics thesis argues, 
democracy has been rendered a mere chimera. In other words, the collapse of the 
Soviet system resulted in ‘an internal weakening of the very democracy that was 
assumed to have triumphed’ (Rancière 2004: 3). For David Harvey, the rich 
world has seen the rise of a neoliberal state apparatus, which is proving increas­
ingly authoritarian. In Harvey’s (2005: 78) terms, this process implies “a radical 
reconﬁguration of state institutions and practices”, where the power of the 
executive tends to usurp the power of representative democracy. Civil society 
organizations act as an integral part of this institutional setup, but fail to present a 
countervailing power to the neoliberal state. Instead, they have to yield to man­
agerialist approaches to public administration. 
This holds particularly true to the speciﬁc case of urban politics, and the struggles 
associated with David Harvey’s (2008) famous claims to “the right to the city”.E r i k 
Swyngedouw (2005) argues that cities have become post-political spaces. The 
mechanisms of such post-politics are “new governance innovations”,s u c ha sd i a ­
logues, consultations, and negotiations. Citizens and civil society organizations are 
held hostage when they participate in such processes: 
Ironically, while these technologies are often advocated and mobilised by 
NGOs and other civil organisations speaking for the disempowered or socially 
excluded, these actors often fail to see how these instruments are an integral 
part of the consolidation of an imposed and authoritarian neo-liberalism. 
(Swyngedouw 2005: 1998) 
Thus, argues Swyngedouw, ‘“participation” is invariably mediated by “power”’, 
which implies that processes of participation serve the purpose of cementing the 
power of elites. Indeed, these elites seem to promote techniques of supposed co-
determination, co-production and co-design as deliberate co-optive strategies. 
The power of these elites tends to be exercised through the ‘rule of experts’ 
(Harvey 2014: 122, citing Mitchell 2002). As Žižek (1999: 198) phrases it: “In 
post-politics, the conﬂict of global ideological visions … is replaced by the col­
laboration of enlightened technocrats (economists, public opinion specialists…)”. 
In this way, Žižek argues, democracy is not so much “repressed” as “foreclosed”. 
Citizens may be invited to participate in so-called democratic processes, but the 
very setup of the mechanisms of participation precludes any signiﬁcant inﬂuence. 

38 Betrayal 
Thus, attempts to engage citizens in politics tend not to politicize the status quo. 
As Mouﬀe phrases it: 
There is much talk today of ‘dialogue’ and ‘deliberation’ but what is the 
meaning of such words in the political ﬁeld, if no real choice is at hand and if 
the participants in the discussion are not able to decide between clearly dif­
ferentiated alternatives? 
(Mouﬀe 2005: 3) 
What is the essence of this status quo? As hinted above, the idea of post-politics 
stems from a politico-economic concern with institutional framework of neo­
liberal capitalism. Ultimately, this very system – and the ways in which it is 
legitimated – represents the main culprit in the post-politics argument. So, 
if the problem with today’s post-politics (‘administration of social aﬀairs’)i s 
that it increasingly undermines the possibility of a proper political act, this 
undermining is directly due to the depoliticization of economics, to the 
common acceptance of Capital and market mechanisms as neutral tools. 
(Žižek 1999: 353) 
To sum up, the post-politics discussion features at least four key propositions. First, 
the present post-political situation emerges from the rise of neoliberalism,w h i c h i n t u r n 
is the ideological outcome of the so-called “end of history”. Second, post-politics 
implies the deliberate deployment of a set of “new technologies of government” 
(Swyngedouw 2005: 1992–1995), which renders opposition futile against an ‘aseptic 
post-political administration of public aﬀairs’ (Žižek 1999: 210). Third, this admin­
istration amounts to an expert rule,o nb e h a l fo f a n “autocratic elite technocracy” 
(Swyngedouw 2005: 2003). Fourth, the thing that post-politics ultimately under­
mines is a critique of capitalism, as it operates in symbiosis with the ideology of bourgeois 
economics, which presents market solutions as natural and apolitical. Now, let’st u r nt o 
an actual case of participative design to see how post-politics operates in practice – 
the one brieﬂy introduced in the beginning of this chapter. 
The agent of betrayal: Introducing the Dialogue Process in 
Gothenburg 
During the autumn of 2005, we participated in a citizen consultation, or so-called 
Dialogue Process, on the redevelopment of the harbor area in Gothenburg, 
Sweden. The city in question is Sweden’s second largest, with a population of 
approximately half a million. Like many other harbor cities, Gothenburg was hit 
by the dock crisis during the 1980s, and large areas on the waterfront have been 
exploited and become attractive housing and oﬃce areas. In order to facilitate this 
process, the municipality started a company in the 1990s, Älvstranden AB, which 

Betrayal 39 
led the redevelopment of the harbor area. This company also launched the 
ambitious six-month Dialogue Process, meant to generate “visions” for the future 
development for the area. 
As members of one out of six teams – all consisting of designers, architects, and 
citizens – we partook in “participatory urban planning” or “partnership planning” 
(cf. Hamdi 1991; Hamdi & Goethert 1997). The originator of the process (from 
here on referred to as “the Dialogue Process”) was a municipal company, which 
sought to generate a harmonious negotiation between citizens and developers. 
However, as the process had drawn to a close, we – along with other partici­
pants – were struggling to ﬁnd traces of our input in the ﬁnal outcomes of the 
process. Moreover, we failed to politicize the development of the area: Our input 
did not contribute to the opening of a dissensus regarding the future of the 
harbor (Mouﬀe 2005). In the end, we were reluctantly resigned to the fact that 
very little was at stake in this process of urban co-design. 
The failings of the Dialogue Process have been discussed by sociologist Catharina 
Thörn (2008). She too was involved in the dialogue, and describes how the radical 
proposals presented by her team were ignored, as these propositions implied “a 
politicisation of the process”. Ironically, such a politicization was not “possible to 
handle within the framework of the political-administrative discourse” (65). Thörn 
thus suggests that 
the fact that the proposal was dismissed because it was too political laid bare 
the conditions related to the discourse of city politics, and the unquestioned 
values they contain. 
(66) 
Drawing upon the post-politics argument sketched above, Thörn suggests that the 
unquestioned values are, in essence, the neoliberal mode of urban development. 
Where does that leave us, the designers and academics participating in the 
process? We were intent on deepening democracy in the context of urban design, 
but to what degree did we – as Swyngedouw suggests – become hostages in the 
process? To what degree did we become complicit, even co-conspirators, in it? 
Did we betray our own interests, or the interests of the wider public? One way to 
speak about how betrayals may inﬂuence co-design processes is to invoke the 
terminology from the power games of WWII. Participants in co-design processes 
can be imagined as collaborateurs, a term that denotes an agency that collaborates 
with invading powers against the interest of the resisting population. Whereas 
some studies of WWII focus on the explicit forms of collaboration (cf. Gordon 
1980), Rings (1982) diﬀerentiates between several modes of collaboration along a 
continuum: On one extreme, there is “unconditional collaboration”, that is, 
collaborating with the former enemy for what is seen as a common cause; on the 
other, there is “tactical collaboration”, where a minimal collaboration is actually a 
cover for purposeful resistance. There is also a stronger term, “Quisling”, which is 

40 Betrayal 
used to refer to radical betrayal, a person who collaborates in the former sense, an 
arch-collaborateur engaging in a “collusion with the enemy before invasion, as 
well as after” (Davies 2004: 94). 
A key concern here is to study the precise mechanisms by which participants in 
co-design processes betray the interests of their peers. Who is the culprit in the 
story of the Dialogue Process? Traditionally, the post-politics literature would 
focus on the normal cast of powerful actors – the technocratic public oﬃcial, the 
scheming politician, and ultimately the corporate managers that serve as the 
incarnation of capital. However, the remainder of this chapter will use actor-
network theory to present an alternative description of post-political outcomes of 
participative design processes that focuses on the design aspects of betrayal. As we 
shall see, actor-network theory allows us to highlight an unexpected culprit in the 
story of the Dialogue Process – as it turns out, the role of the “Quisling” is played 
by a non-human. It should thus be noted that we use the term in an abstract 
manner, where the meaning of the term is detached from the original Vidkun 
Quisling. In other words, this argument is about agencies of betrayal, not about a 
historical person’s relation to National Socialism. Along with the claim that the 
Quisling agency is non-human, we will furthermore argue that it is deceptive to 
point to a distinct, pre-existing power structure to which co-design participants 
capitulate. The identity of the Quisling agency can simply not be captured through 
standard social-scientiﬁc models of (human) agency. Again, actor-network theory 
(ANT) provides an alternative and complementary view of how post-politics 
operates in practice. 
Actor-network theory on betrayal 
Approaching the Dialogue Process from an ANT perspective forces us to 
reorient the scope of the analysis. Indeed, in Reassembling the Social,B r u n o 
Latour posits that “the ﬁrst source of uncertainty” for any scholar wishing to do 
ANT relates to the radical agnosticism regarding group formation (Latour 2005: 
29). In other words, an analysis of the Dialogue Process cannot start from a 
preexisting social-scienti ﬁc account of the supposed role of capital, labor, and 
the state in a stylized account of the “neoliberal state”.T h i si s a r eﬂection of 
ANT’s proposition of “radical indeterminacy of the actor” (Callon 1999: 181), 
and the idea that interests are merely “temporarily stabilised outcomes of pre­
vious processes of enrolment” (Callon & Law 1982: 662). It also reﬂects the 
Garﬁnkelian heritage (cf. Latour 2013: 6), which postulates that the concepts of 
the actors are allowed to be stronger than those of the analysts (Latour 2005: 
30). So, while the “sociologist of the social” may proceed from general cate­
gories, “imposing some order beforehand” (23), the “sociologist of associations ” 
must pay closer attention to the situation at hand – in our case the Dialogue 
Process – and conduct an open-ended examination of the modes by which 
groups are assembled. 

Betrayal 41 
Another key source of divergence from the post-politics discussion relates to the 
role of ideology critique: ANT precludes an easy delineation between scientiﬁcf a c t 
and ideological delusion (Latour 2004). Thus, items one and four on the list of 
propositions from the post-politics discussion have to be omitted from the analysis: 
The inquiry may not start from the proposition that we live in a neoliberalism 
borne from the idea of the end of history, nor can it depart from an a priori 
assumption that a supposedly “bourgeois” economics produces post-political eﬀects. 
An ANT analysis is nevertheless amenable for explaining how processes like the 
Dialogue Process may prove democratically deﬁcient. Latour (2005: 22) locates a 
second source of uncertainty in the nature of actions; “in each course of action a 
great variety of agents seem to barge in and displace the original goals”.T h i s i s 
demonstrated in Latour’s (1999: 80–112) description of how the work of physicist 
Frédéric Joliot is propelled forward by aligning his interests with those of the 
French state, more speciﬁcally the National Centre for Scientiﬁc Research. Where 
Joliot’sg o a l i s t o b e t h e ﬁrst to master a nuclear chain reaction, the state’sr e p r e ­
sentative, Raoul Dautry, has another goal – to secure national independence in the 
face of foreign military threats. By aligning their interest, they form an alliance 
around a new composite goal: To establish a nuclear physics laboratory. Thus, in 
such “translation” of goals, there is always “a drift, a slippage, a displacement” (88). 
As shown in Michel Callon’s (1986) classic study on scallops, ﬁshermen, and 
researchers in St Brieuc Bay, such translation processes are rife with betrayals. This 
chapter will suggest that when participating in co-design processes, there is a risk 
that one’s goals are being betrayed or translated away through successive enrol­
ments – though the benefactors of this process can never be known in advance. 
Indeed, Latour (2013) refers to translation as “translation/betrayal” (see for 
instance 341), and the notion of translation as betrayal is ultimately an ontological 
position. All representations are betrayals of the thing represented, and thus “the 
idea that translation is also a betrayal is built into the charter of actor-network 
theory (if we may allow ourselves to imagine that it has a charter)” (Law 1997). 
There is also a Machiavellian aspect of translation. In his book on Latour, The 
Prince of Networks, Graham Harman has stated that “for Latour every actor is a Joliot, 
a medium of translation able to link the most far-ﬂung objects” (Harman 2009: 
102). Susan Leigh Star famously questioned such a “managerial and entrepre­
neurial model of actor networks” (Star 1991: 26). By focusing on successful 
Joliots, it neglects “the world of the non-enrolled” (49), and may ultimately cause 
researches to side with the powerful. While not speciﬁcally studying those who 
are altogether Other to such enrollments, this argument shares an interest in all 
those actors who are not successful Joliots. Indeed, some people may become 
losers in the process of translation, causing their original objectives to be sub­
stantially marginalized. More speciﬁcally, when it comes to co-designed urban 
planning, there is a ﬂip side to the translation of goals, in which participating 
citizens and civil society groups may see their original goals becoming diluted in 
the process of goal translation. 

42 Betrayal 
An ANT perspective would not, however, present such dismal outcomes as 
preordained. The Machiavelli-inspired, micro-oriented conception of power pro­
posed by ANT (Callon 1986; Latour 1988; Clegg 1989: 203) suggests that there 
the proverbial “powers that be” may not always control citizens through the new 
modes of participatory governance. It would, therefore, pace Žižek, be misleading 
to state that democracy is always “foreclosed” in processes of participation – that is 
determined by process of translation. This aspect of ANT also places a question 
mark over items two and three on the list of post-politics propositions: One cannot 
assume that technocratic elites will always be fully in control of the outcomes 
of co-design, and that this is a deliberate strategy on behalf of the powers that 
be. Moreover, as Callon (1991: 143) insists, that there is no such thing as 
“translation ‘in general”’. The ANT researcher must pay close attention to the 
material media in which such translations of goals are “inscribed: roundtable 
discussions, public declarations, texts, technical objects, embodied skills”,a n d 
so on. Hence, in the next section, this chapter will analyze the co-design 
process by studying such inscriptions. 
This leads us to a further key point of divergence from the post-politics 
approach that stems from the third source of uncertainty – that of the nature of 
objects. Accepting that “objects too have agency” (Latour 2005: 63), forces the 
analyst of co-design processes to interrogate the role played in the paraphernalia 
of artifacts that feature in such processes. Indeed, these artifacts may prove inﬂu­
ential in the democratic deﬁcit of co-design. As a preliminary illustration of how 
such a deﬁcit emerges, one may compare the ANT approach – and our focus on 
translation and betrayal – with Rancière’s notion of “mésentente”, which is closely 
aligned with the above-mentioned notion of “the distribution of the sensible”. 
For Rancière, democracy is intimately tied to this French term, which means 
“quarrel, disagreement”, but also “the fact of not hearing, of not understanding” 
(Rancière 2004: 5). Democracy means breaking down the delineation between 
the reasonable “voice” articulated by the patrician, on the one hand, and the 
incomprehensible “noise” coming from the plebeian, on the other. This chapter 
too deals with the problematic of how co-design processes cause some voices to 
be treated as “noise”, causing them to be ﬁltered out. However, whereas Ran­
cière’s argument comes from a context of pre-existing social divisions among 
humans – that is, masters and slaves – our argument is more interested in how 
non-human entities contribute to this very sorting operation. In other words, 
our inquiry is into the role of the quasi-objects as protagonists in this story. 
More speciﬁcally, we are interested in the quasi-objects that cause voices to be 
interpreted as noise. 
Here, we will borrow the term “protocol” from media theory. On a basic level, a 
communications protocol is a system of rules that regulate data exchange, the most 
famous being the TCP/IP which governs the internet. Alexander Galloway, how­
ever, deﬁnes it more abstractly as “a technique for achieving voluntary regulation 
within a contingent environment” (Galloway 2004: 7). As such, protocols are not 

Betrayal 43 
power-neutral, as they produce “a distributed management system that allows con­
trol to exist within a heterogeneous material milieu”. Thus, protocol “is to control 
societies as the panopticon is to disciplinary societies” (13). Thus, though these 
references point towards Deleuze (1988) and Foucault (1977) rather than ANT, the 
notion of protocol is nevertheless useful for the purposes of this chapter. Protocol 
allows us to explore mundane and practical modes of co-design interaction as arti­
factual modes of ordering – more speciﬁcally for sorting out noise from voice. Cru­
cially, it allows us to do so while retaining the ANT proposition that groups are to be 
studied as emerging rather than as predeﬁned categories of “the social”. (See the ﬁrst 
source of uncertainty, discussed above.) It also allows for a depiction of this control as 
an interlacing between humans and non-humans, with a close attention to quasi-
objects – to objects with agency. Thus, we may speak of the protocol as an agent of 
betrayal, and the assemblage of citizens and artifacts as an “assembly of collaborateurs”. 
In the next section, we will make use of this conceptual apparatus when analyzing 
the empirical case of the Dialogue Process. 
Translation and betrayal in the Gothenburg Dialogue Process 
The team that we participated in, “Team Rhizom”, endeavored to assume what 
Thorpe and Gamman refer to as a “responsive” role. This “assumes that as a 
designer you do what you can, working with other social actors to leverage the 
community’s assets to eﬀect positive change, but do not seek to take it upon 
yourself to try to do it all, for other social actors” (Thorpe & Gamman 2011: 
221). Yet, in the end, the format of quasi-subjects (the co-design participants) and 
quasi-objects that constituted the protocol (the material formats for processing the 
communication of development proposals) turned the team into an assembly of 
collaborateurs. 
The process of becoming collaborateurs was not evident at the time. During the 
initial design process, the Rhizom team generated a series of “visions” that indeed 
seemed to represent “outside the box” thinking. One key idea was that urban 
planning is about more than the physical manifestation of the built environment; 
the team wanted to introduce more abstract propositions, such as experimental 
governance arrangements, and new economic models that would safeguard social 
justice. These ideas got ﬁltered out as the proposal was rendered into the draw­
ings and plans that were to be communicated to politicians, the city planning 
oﬃce, and the public. To state that this ﬁltering was related to a supposed neo­
liberal consensus, or by bourgeois ideology, would be to simplify this process. 
Again, the outcome can be accounted for by paying close attention to the pro­
tocols of participation. It is here, by highlighting the Machiavellian micropower 
of the protocols, that the ANT perspective may complement the post-politics 
argument. 
In other words, when tracing how post-political outcomes emerge, the devil 
really is in the details. However, before delving into the speciﬁcs of this protocol, 

44 Betrayal 
we may ﬁrst make a general point regarding the modus operandi of protocols. As 
noted by Galloway, “Protocol is not a superego (like the police); instead it always 
operates at the level of desire, at the level of ‘what we want”’ (Galloway 2004: 
241). In the case of our urban co-design process, this subtle form of power is 
manifested in the following manner: If the process is conceived as a chain of 
sequential data exchanges, each link is governed by desires of the receiver, implying 
that the non-desire is treated as noise. In what follows, we will ﬁrst describe the set 
of protocols that governed the design process of the team, sorting out voice from 
noise, and then focus on the protocols that guided outside audiences’ processing of 
the design proposal. We will do so by examining the inscriptions (Latour & 
Woolgar 1986: 45–53) generated within the design process, thus construing the 
protocol as something that guides the chain of successive inscriptions. 
The design process started oﬀ from the generation of inscriptions in the form 
of Post-it notes. Indeed, in a public presentation of the results (see below), one of 
the citizens designated to the Rhizom team described the co-design process in the 
following terms: “We’ve discussed a lot and moved post-it notes!” (Team 
Rhizom 2005). The Post-it note-generating brainstorming session constitutes a 
prime example of a consensus-oriented “designerly” method, which in itself fea­
tures a form of ﬁltering. One of the typical rules of this exercise is to actively 
avoid negative ideas or to criticize the ideas of others. “Defer judgment” is the 
ﬁrst rule of brainstorming, as design agency IDEO suggests; “creative spaces don’t 
judge” (OpenIDEO 2011). Negative notes stiﬂe the creative process, and thus a 
word of warning may be “ﬁltered” out from the process and a person in the team 
who is critical is seen as “negative” in the group and marginalized. Though not 
deployed in our team during the Dialogue Process, the Appreciative Inquiry 
method is another technique that may produce similar outcomes, focusing on 
appreciation rather than critique (Grant & Humphries 2006). 
Some of the “positive” and “constructive” ideas generated in brainstorming 
sessions were elaborated upon through inscriptions such as word documents and 
emails. This material dealt at length with the more abstract ideas mentioned 
above, such as reformed governance structures of the municipal company that 
owns the land (Älvstranden AB) and the formulation of a separate constitution for 
the planned area. It also included microﬁnance for local entrepreneurship, as well 
as plans for enabling spontaneous and small-scale activation of the urban space 
(though events, festivals, markets etc.). One further idea was that of the area 
being developed on the principle of “purchase on approval”– the development 
trajectory would be subject to constant revising, and housing programs would not 
be set in stone. These ideas were, however, severely truncated as they fed into the 
next step in the chain of inscriptions; the presentation poster. Here, the visionary 
had to be translated into the visualizable. This designerly mode of inscription was 
an obligatory step in communicating the internal work of the team to outside 
audiences – that is, the wider public, as well as the municipal planning profes­
sionals. In other words, the team management is not to be blamed for the 

Betrayal 45 
ﬁltering of non-visual ideas: The foregrounding of the visuality of the physical 
built environment, at the expense of abstract proposals on governance and 
socioeconomic issues, was hardwired into the co-design process. 
The poster represented the interface between the internal process of the team and 
the outside audiences. This inscription was thus featured in the public communica­
tion produced by the municipality (Gothenburg City Planning Oﬃce 2006a) as well 
as in the local newspaper. Another inscription situated at the interface with the out­
side audiences was the PowerPoint presentation, which – much like the poster – 
foregrounded ideas that could be rendered into visual expressions. This implied that 
the “purchase on approval” idea – which could be visualized in the form of a pho­
toshopped receipt – was emphasized, while other abstract ideas were somewhat 
sidelined. The PowerPoint presentation was shown during two occasions in late 
2005: One presentation to planners and local politicians, and one to the general 
public at the city’s largest cinema. These events followed a strict time schedule, in 
which the teams were allotted brief time slots, which undermined any room to 
“question the brief”. Here, our experience is close to that of Tufte (2006), who has 
famously denounced PowerPoint’s tendency to truncate complicated arguments. 
While, in theory, PowerPoint may facilitate a cognitive style that could “allow het­
erogeneous materials to be seamlessly re-presented” (Stark & Paravel 2008), that was 
not the eﬀect it had in our case. 
The ﬁrst actor to add new links to the chain of inscriptions was the local 
newspaper. As mentioned above, the paper reprinted the poster, and reported 
from the public presentation at the cinema. Interestingly, the newspaper article is 
somewhat cynical in relation to glossy, visually striking presentations of the more 
architectural teams. However, the format of the newspaper article forces the 
reporter to simply note that “the buildings are mere illustrations” of the wider 
strategy proposed by our team – a strategy that is not described in the article 
(Granath 2005: 82). The same newspaper subsequently publishes an interview 
with the team leader, which follows the same logic: While failing to commu­
nicate the more abstract ideas of the team, it instead focuses on the proposals that 
directly relate to the built environment (Nyström 2006: 18). This implies that the 
idea of building a swamp in the middle of the river – a proposal that the team 
members originally construed as of minor relevance – was brought forward as key 
to the team’s proposal. 
Along with the media inscriptions, the planning professionals of the municipality 
produced a bifurcation in the chain of inscriptions produced during the process. As 
an integral part of the Dialogue, the planners made their own interpretations 
of the six proposals, condensing them under the following predetermined 
headings: Bus/tram transport; car transport; junctions; parks and greenery; environ­
ment, sustainability and safety; housing; trade; and real estate ownership (Gothenburg 
City Planning Oﬃce 2006b). These themes, in turn, are meant to ﬁt squarely into the 
format of the existing legislation on urban planning, treating any incompatible infor­
mation as noise. Indeed, when questioned about the depoliticizing eﬀects of this 

46 Betrayal 
operation, the planners respond that they can only cater to “the views that are directly 
applicable on a development plan” (Hagberg 2006: 90). Or, in the words of the 
designer of the Dialogue Process, the planners cannot, should not, and do not want to 
process “political proposals”, as this was not part of the Dialogue brief (Strömberg 
2006: 86). Again, this highlights how the speciﬁcs of the protocol exert a subtle form 
of power. The planning profession is not consciously taking an ideological line – it is 
not explicitly ‘neoliberal’– it merely follows the protocol. 
As inscriptions can be seen as the material form of the translation of interests 
(Callon 1991: 143), each new link in the chain of inscriptions also indicates how 
interests and goals have drifted. In this process, the original intentions and interests of 
us (the authors of this text) had to be aligned with the other team members, then the 
team as a whole; then the planners and the media outlets, and so on. Before con­
cluding this section, we may allow ourselves to note the disparate interests and goals 
that are manifested in this series of translations. So, while the newspaper does not ﬁt 
into the cliché of the media corporation whose prime goal is to sell cheap content, the 
inscriptions nevertheless suggest that the newspaper content generation is governed by 
principles of visual popular appeal. Similarly, the planners seem to approach the 
visions with the goal of processing the material into a workable development plan. 
The interests and goals of the architects and designers we worked with partly stem 
from a desire to remain in the game – to seem suitably realistic to win commissions for 
the future development of the area. Indeed, we shared this desire with them. 
In conclusion, we may also point to the limited techno-managerial investment of 
the team, which made the protocols so eﬀective in treating more radical ideas as 
noise. At the outset, there was limited time for us to determine how the Dialogue 
protocol would limit the outcomes of the process. Moreover, in the aftermath of 
the Dialogue, we did not ﬁght hard and long enough to rectify the outcomes 
generated by this protocol. These two limitations are, we argue, related to the 
notion of co-design; the very format of this co-design process did not provide a 
sustained arena for politically challenging the design brief. As designers, our belief in 
the co-design process, and a general positive hope that deliberative democracy 
would automatically be “good” (and not corrupted), led the team to become 
hijacked. Through the protocols, the radicalism of the original visions was disarmed 
by design. As constituent elements of this assemblage, we became collaborateurs. 
Analyzing the Dialogue Process 
There are three themes that emerge from our encounter with the Dialogue 
Process, and from the attempt to deploy ANT as a means to account for the 
potential democratic deﬁcits of co-design. First, we will specify what we mean 
when we speak of a democratic deﬁciency in methods of participation. Second, 
we will outline the merits of introducing ANT in this case, juxtaposing it with 
the type of critique presented by the “post-politics” discussion. Third, we will 
expand on the beneﬁts and perils of using the term “protocol”. 

Betrayal 47 
First, the issue of democratic deﬁcits. In this chapter, we have tried to move 
beyond critical accounts that tend to dismiss all kinds of engagement with the 
world of formal politics. (Note Mouﬀe’s above-mentioned distinction between 
politics and the political.) Therefore, we must be clear that terms like “democratic 
deﬁciencies” must be used against a pre-understanding of the inevitable fact that 
formal politics must be “anti-political”. Formal politics is about reducing a plethora 
of dissonant voices into one consensus, thus facilitating legitimate decision-making. 
In Political Machines, Andrew Barry writes: “Indeed, it is a feature of parliamentary 
party politics (with its concern for party discipline, for instance) that it will have 
anti-political elements. […] To say this is not to denounce party politics or to draw 
a false opposition between the anti-political nature of party politics and the political 
character of alternative forms of direct action” (Barry 2001: 195). Our concern in 
this chapter, though, is the manner in which this consensus is reached. The expo­
sition of translations and drift presented above suggests that the material forms of 
participation – that is, the protocols of co-design – facilitate a consensus-making 
that systematically ﬁlters out certain types of voices. 
Second, as stated above, there are forms of Machiavellian micropower struggles 
that may be exercised in co-design processes. These forms of power, some critics 
of the process suggested, need to be addressed in further detail. One of the great 
beneﬁts of ANT is that its very mode of operation starts from a detailed study of 
these very forms of micropower, not from preexisting social “structures” of 
power. (Remember, as highlighted in the theoretical sections on post-politics and 
ANT, this is one of the main themes of doing actor-network research.) Another 
beneﬁt is the focus on the mundane use of artifacts that invariably inﬂuences 
agency. In this way, it puts the spotlight on entities that might otherwise escape 
our attention. Having experienced a democratically deﬁcient process of partici­
pative planning, one tends to search for culprits among human actors – the 
power-hungry politician, the boring bureaucrat who turns politics into adminis­
tration, or some other villain. ANT, instead, renders other problematics visible – 
in our case, the problem of democratic deﬁcits seems to be related to the material 
modes of collaboration that we call protocols. 
By pointing to such new “culprits”, ANT may shift the debate on participation. 
Indeed, the post-politics argument may create an unproductive schism between the 
professions involved in urban planning and design, on the one hand, and the critics 
of these professions. Here, it is worth bearing in mind that diﬀerent theories are more 
or less useful in particular contexts. Whereas ANT was useful for analyzing demo­
cratic deﬁciencies in our case, the post-politics argument seems apt for capturing 
Thörn’s (2008) experience. Nevertheless, the ANT position, as operationalized in 
this chapter, suggests that some of the democratic deﬁciencies of co-design may be 
assuaged by a reengineering of the protocols of collaboration. 
This leads us to the third and ﬁnal point, about the notion of “protocol”.W e 
have previously used this term for reimagining design management: Protocols 
may be engineered to facilitate decentralized modes of co-design that do not 

48 Betrayal 
require top-down regulation (Palmås & von Busch 2011). However, as this 
chapter argues, the notion of protocol ought to be understood as a non-norma­
tive, neutral term. Organizations or processes governed by protocols are not 
necessarily “good”. The existence of a protocol merely suggests that the organi­
zation or process takes place in an open setting, and is based on voluntary colla­
boration. However, for this very reason – that protocols can be engineered in 
ways that both liberate and restrain – we believe that they should be at the heart 
of the discussion on co-design. 
It is our conclusion that in processes like these, as designers, we need to 
remind ourselves of three points. First, it shifted from the prescriptive hopes of 
agonistic design – that design may be mobilized to facilitate dissensus – to a 
description of how participative desi g nm a y q u e l ls u c hd i s s e n s u s . S e c o n d , 
having made this point with reference to the literature on the post-political 
condition, it introduced ANT as an alternative mode of understanding the post-
political outcomes of participative design. This, in turn, implied that our 
description focused on the material modes of co-design that produced the post-
political outcomes. Using the term translation rather than Rancière’s mésentente 
implied a concomitant reorientation away from studying the discursive realm, to 
a focus on a more general circulation of representations. It also implied that we 
substituted the post-politics authors’ notion of power – which tends to focus on 
hegemonic ideologies – to a Latour-infused Machiavellian understanding of 
power. This, in turn, privileges the micro-political struggles of machines as well 
as machinations. 
There is, however, one fundamental point that comes out from the ANT’s 
notion of translation, and that is the inevitability of betrayal. Again, all repre­
sentations are betrayals of the thing represented, which complicates the idea that 
participative design may accurately represent the wills and dreams of the partici­
pating actors. Indeed, it may be both the strength and weakness of design to be 
disentangled from the political conﬂicts it tries to address, but as sociologist 
Robert Nisbet (1953) posits: “rootless men always betray”. The concluding 
chapters (6 and 7) will reengage with this issue – how can participative design 
learn to live with the inevitability of betrayal? 
In summary, this chapter has shown that co-design processes invariably reﬂect 
the tension between the political, on the one hand, and politics, on the other. 
While the former necessitates the emergence of a dissensus, the latter implies a 
process of turning dissensus into consensus. While there are those who argue that 
co-design processes tend to be post-political charades that merely disguise the fact 
that decisions tend to be made by technocratic elites, the chapter has construed 
the process of co-design as one of translation. By drawing on this actor-network 
theory, it has shown how such a process of translation can be traced, through the 
material modes of collaboration. More generally, the notion of translation denotes 
a process of enrolment of diverse interests, which implies that objectives drift over 
time. In other words, interests will invariably be betrayed. 

Betrayal 49 
The next chapter will explore another aspect of how design objectives may be 
challenged by social processes. As such, it will move from inevitable betrayal of 
participants’ interests in co-design practices, to studying how the original inten­
tions of socially oriented institutional designs may be betrayed. 

4 
CORRUPTION 
Design and decay 
In the introduction to this book, we told a story about the protests of the Global 
Justice Movement. However, from a design perspective, the fate of the key policy of 
the movement is even more interesting. In Fast Policy: Experimental Statecraft at the 
Thresholds of Neoliberalism, Jamie Peck and Nik Theodore (2015) tell the story of the 
cruel fate of the movement’s pet policy: Participatory budgeting (PB). Though for­
mally a contribution to the ﬁeld of critical policy studies, Fast Policy is also a study of 
how designs – speciﬁcally policy designs that deviate from mainstream political 
models – tend to decay over time. Thus, they claim that “(original) ‘designs’ rarely 
hold” as “the institutional and technical character of policies evolves, mutates, and 
often degrades as ‘travel’ occurs” (Peck & Theodore 2015: xx). The case of PB shows 
how such decay can play out: The hopeful experiment of the anti-Davos activists 
degraded into becoming a standard feature in the Davos establishment’st o o l b o x . 
How did this happen? 
This chapter follows the discussion from the previous chapter: Here, too, we 
are interested in processes of drift, co-option, and betrayal. However, there is a 
key diﬀerence: The previous chapter studied the inevitable betrayal of partici­
pants’ interests in co-design practices; this chapter will focus on how the original 
intentions of socially oriented institutional designs may be eroded and betrayed. 
That process of corruption is not, we shall argue, inevitable. However, in order 
to prevent such corruption, idealist inclinations of designers must be tempered 
with realist intuitions. Such realism ought to involve a wariness towards the 
capacity of economic incentives and other social forces to corrode the original 
aims of social designs. 
The argument runs as follows: After completing the story about participatory 
budgeting, the chapter will discuss ways of accounting for its failure to maintain 
its integrity. Thus, the argument will introduce the work of Machiavelli as a way 
DOI: 10.4324/9781003281443-4 

Corruption 51 
of thinking about the tension between maintaining the state of things in the 
context of change. While Machiavelli did not speak in the language of design, 
but rather in the language of innovation, the chapter will discuss design as it 
relates to innovation, speciﬁcally social innovation. The argument will thus pro­
ceed to study the trajectory of microﬁnance, as another instance of a “social 
innovation” that emerged from a speciﬁc location, spread across the globe, yet 
ultimately failed to maintain its integrity. In other words, the case of microﬁnance 
serves as a parallel example to that of participatory budgeting. Arguably, the cor­
ruption of microﬁnance – which preceded participatory budgeting as the pet idea 
within global development circles – has led to more disastrous outcomes than that 
of PB. The chapter ends with a discussion on what Machiavelli can teach us in 
avoiding such outcomes. 
Another world is possible! 
During the ﬁrst few years into the new millennium, the Global Justice (or 
“anti-globalization ”,o r “alter-globalization ”) Movement was at its prime. It was 
propelled by the idea that a world beyond the neoliberal status quo was possi­
ble, and sought political inspiration from the World Social Forum – an annual 
meeting ﬁrst held in Porto Alegre in 2001. In their foreword to the edited 
volume titled Another World is Possible, Michael Hardt and Antonio Negri 
(2003) describe the signiﬁcance of the subsequent 2002 forum: 
The World Social Forum in Porto Alegre has already become a myth, one of 
those positive myths that deﬁne our political compass. It is the representation 
of a new democratic cosmopolitanism, a new anti-capitalist transnationalism, 
a new intellectual nomadism, a great movement of the multitude. These are 
the positive elements of myth-making. The Porto Alegre Forum emerged 
from the beginning as a great network to bring the members of the Brazilian 
Workers’ Party (PT) together with the ‘globalization’ protest movements, 
the local administrators experimenting in new forms of participatory 
democracy together with the utopian schemers of a global democracy. 
(Hardt & Negri 2003: xvi) 
Among the “new forms of participatory democracy” that emerged in Porto 
Alegre, there was one idea and practice that stood out as the star of the show – 
that of participatory budgeting. PB is a process in which community members 
collectively decide how to allocate part of municipal funds to certain projects 
deemed urgent by the community, instead of delegating this process to elected 
politicians or oﬃcial bureaucrats. In various forms, PB is today used in over 3,000 
cities worldwide, often applying many types of game techniques in the processes 
of collective deliberation and decision-making, from social service programs to 
children’s councils (Lerner 2014). 

52 Corruption 
We will soon follow the path of this “institutional design” as it evolved in 
Brazil, and subsequently spread across the globe. As will be evident, PB gradually 
morphed into an approach to municipal budgeting that was compatible with 
pretty much any kind of government. As such, the working practices and tools 
for widening participation in municipal budgeting were made compatible with 
more right-wing, economically liberal politics. Indeed, PB became a go-to policy 
in the toolbox of the World Bank. 
This development is ironic, given the fact that the promise of Porto Alegre and 
PB was that this institutional design presented an alternative to the then prevailing 
political ideals associated with “the Washington Consensus”. This consensus, 
critics suggested, cemented a neoliberal status quo, which was enshrined in the 
operations of the Bretton Woods Trio (the World Bank, the IMF and the 
WTO), and reﬁned during the annual World Economic Forum meetings in 
Davos. In fact, the gatherings that started in Porto Alegre in 2001 were con­
sciously positioned as counter-meetings to Davos. In their foreword, Hardt and 
Negri use geographical metaphors to explain the diﬀerence between the two 
forums: While Davos is freezing cold, Porto Alegre is hot. Davos is a “hierarchy 
blocked on a mountaintop and Porto Alegre an unlimited network expanding 
across the plains” (xvii). Thus, “the networks and the connections among the 
movements form the horizon at Porto Alegre”, which breeds “new formulations 
and experiments”. 
As we shall see below, this very slippery connectivity and frantic experimentalism 
is one of the reasons why participatory budgeting as an institutional design lacked the 
integrity to live up to its political promises. 
The travels of participatory budgeting 
The story starts in 1980s Brazil, in which democratization had contributed to an 
accelerating participation in voluntary organizations, especially among the poor. 
This, in turn, can be placed in a longer tradition of popular education, liberation 
theology, associationalism, and “sundry precursors to what would later be known 
as horizontalidad” (Peck & Theodore 2015: 149). In the 1990s, the idea of parti­
cipatory budgeting was the key program promoted by Brazil’s Workers’ Party, 
which was then in government in the Municipality of Porto Alegre. The pro­
gram implied a deep participation of local, grassroots association in diﬀerent 
phases of the preparation of the budget. This, in turn, created a process which 
paid particular attention to the redistributive aspects of budgeting and public 
investments (148). 
More speciﬁcally, scholars have pointed to ﬁve beneﬁcial outcomes of PB, as 
operationalized in Porto Alegre. (155) First, citizens – particularly the poor – 
were mobilized to a great degree: Over the course of a year, tens of thousands
