across the city participated in the budgeting. Second, the civic sphere was ener­
gized through the emergence of new civil society organizations. Third, the 

Corruption 53 
quality of the provision of infrastructure and other public services increased. Fourth, 
clientelism and corruption decreased, by virtue of more transparent systems of 
priority setting and budget allocation. Last, neighborhood-level decision-making 
implied new spending priorities that favored the poor, thus generating a progressive 
socio-spatial redistribution. 
These outcomes were noted by the nascent global justice movement in the 
rich Global North. For a movement struggling to decolonize its modes of poli­
tical thought, these fruitful experiments were both welcome and hopeful: The 
Global South had shown that another world was possible. Indeed, if there was 
any hope for the global left, it was to be found in places like Porto Alegre. Hence 
the fanfare around PB during the ﬁrst World Social Forums, as evidenced by 
Hardt and Negri’s praise quoted in the beginning of this chapter. 
However, this hope would sometimes underestimate the site-speciﬁc aspects of 
the Porto Alegre experiment. As Peck and Theodore point out, the experiment 
cannot be dissociated from the PT’s long political ascendancy, which culminated 
in Lula’s election in 2002. This, in turn, had been “predicated on an unprece­
dented alliance between labor and social movements on the one hand, and a 
network of municipal and state governments on the other” (150). Therefore, 
“the modo petista de governar (the ‘PT way of governing’) had in fact been con­
structed through PB, as an open-ended and evolving system of ‘co-government’ 
with civil society”. This political environment could scarcely be found elsewhere, 
not even within Brazil. So, as Lula and the PT seized control of the federal level 
of government, it turned out that the Porto Alegre-based PB practices could not 
be readily transferred to the rest of the nation. 
Later, as the PT lost power in the municipality, the practice of PB also changed 
in Porto Alegre itself. The new centrist coalition that governed from 2005 
onwards made a point of retaining PB, but aimed to make it more “inclusive” 
with respect to which stakeholders to include. Whereas the PT had favored the 
participation of civil society organizations representing the poor, the new muni­
cipal government also sought to include local businesses, foundations, universities, 
and churches. Thus, the public was demoted to just another stakeholder among 
other stakeholders, and PB morphed into a quasi-corporatist mode of governing. 
Meanwhile, on an international level, the early 2000s saw considerable eﬀorts 
to emulate the Porto Alegre policy design. As we have already seen, the World 
Social Forum (WSF) served as a conduit for the spreading of the gospel. At the 
2002 forum, Luis Inácio Lula da Silva – the newly elected president and ex-PT 
leader – told the 100,000-strong crowd that “Davos must listen to Porto Alegre” 
(150). He would subsequently attend the 2003 Davos forum, and as it turned out, 
the establishment that the WSF sought to challenge seemed to take notice. In 
fact, PB had been on the World Bank’s radar since the mid-1990s, but from 2003 
the Washington-based institution started to “assume the mantle as the principal 
proponent” of PB (187). However, the PB promoted by the World Bank was 
more in line with the post-PT version of the practice; the one which was more 

54 Corruption 
“inclusive” in terms of the stakeholders it mobilized. Moreover, the Bank high­
lighted other outcomes than the ones that enthused the crowds at the World 
Social Forum. 
By promoting public access to revenue and expenditure information, PB 
eﬀectively increases transparency in ﬁscal policy and public expenditure management, 
reducing scope for clientelistic practices, elite capture, and corruption, thereby enhan­
cing the government’s credibility and the citizens’ trust. PB can also improve 
service delivery by linking needs identiﬁcation, investment planning, tax systems and 
project management. 
(World Bank report, cited in Peck & Theodore 2015: 190, italics in original) 
Framed in this way, PB no longer seems like a policy design that truly chal­
lenges the status quo, like the “Paris Commune and the early Russian soviets” 
(152). Rather, it comes across as yet another “mechanism” for good governance, 
wholly compatible with other, perhaps more technocratic governance tools in the 
World Bank toolbox. As such, it became supported in the Bank’s lending, and 
through its educational programs (191). Therefore, “in so far as it can be under­
stood as a counterhegemonic strategy”, the Porto Alegre policy design must be 
considered a failure (164). 
Resisting corrosive environments 
Peck and Theodore’s account raises questions about whether there was something 
inherently problematic with the PB design. Was there a design fault that made it 
lose its integrity? Indeed, diﬀerent designs carry diﬀerent aﬀordances – they can 
be more or less susceptible to the “corrosive eﬀects” of the political environment. 
On the other hand, the political environment itself may be more or less corrosive. 
A tendency towards degradation is not inevitable, partly because the politics that 
surround these traveling designs is “complex, multidirectional, and unpredictable”. 
Thus, Peck and Theodore advocate a position of “neither design determinism nor 
political foreclosure” (xxi). 
As hinted above, one reason for the failure of PB to maintain its integrity was 
the poor recognition of exactly how dependent it was on the history and local 
particularities of its place of origin. However, beyond this fact, there are lessons to 
be learned regarding how to care for experimental designs as they leave the pro­
totype stage and claim a life of their own. It is not enough to devise a radical policy 
design; the designer has to prepare for the corrosive eﬀects of new environments. 
Peck and Theodore conclude: 
Superior ‘alternative’ designs are no doubt necessary but they are certainly far 
from suﬃcient in this context. If there is a lesson to be borrowed from the 
World Bank’s adopted role as a model-peddler it is surely that eﬀective 

Corruption 55 
strategies for extralocal distribution and dissemination are at least as important 
as innovations in design. 
(237) 
Caring for the future path of the design may be particularly important when it 
comes to a design that proves ﬂexible enough to serve many masters. In relation 
to this, Peck and Theodore suggest that “horizontal networking, long favored by 
some sections of the left, have manifestly not been delivering on a generalized 
basis” (238). This holds particularly true when “centers of hierarchical coordina­
tion and persuasion have been increasingly monopolized by conservative forces”. 
This is a practical question, but also one of theory. Indeed, the politics favored by 
“horizontals”– as opposed to “verticals” (Mason 2011) – tends to favor the 
multiplying of connections. In other words, politics is a matter of – again, using 
the language of Hardt and Negri – forging “networks and the connections 
among the movements”. However, the problem was that the ever-expanding 
assemblage of PB seemed to make connections outside of the movements. In the 
end, PB proved too “nomadic”, too unlimited in its networking. 
This reading of the PB failure ties in with a wider discontent with a particular form 
of Deleuze-inspired mode of thought that took hold of academics and radicals after 
the turn of the millennium. Cultural theorist Alexander Galloway suggests that in the 
future, “intellectual historians will identify something like ‘2000s Realist Theory’, 
[which] excludes the negative in favor of aﬃrmation and sublime positivity through 
expansion and intensiﬁcation” (Galloway 2017). Thus, Galloway’s concern is not so 
much with Deleuze himself – it is directed at “a certain kind of Deleuzian School” 
(Berry & Galloway 2016: 158), including the “Google Deleuzians” for whom the 
maximizing of connections is the prime objective. Similarly, in Dark Deleuze, 
Andrew Culp (2016) attempts to reconstruct Deleuze’s work, rejecting the “canon 
of joy” that tends to be associated with his work, along with the focus on con­
nectivism. Thus, “the ﬁrst step is to acknowledge that the unbridled optimism for 
connection has failed” and seek to “break circuits rather than extend them” (Culp 
2016: 12). The question is not only how to create open-ended designs – it is also 
how to prevent it from corrupting connections. 
To some extent, the failure of PB is also a matter of a splintering of the 
communities that care for the design. Here, Johan Söderberg and Maxigas’ 
(2021) work on hacker movements is useful. In the world of hacking, there are 
multiple examples of radical, open-ended experiments that failed to maintain 
their integrity. Söderberg and Maxigas construe these processes in terms of 
autonomy and recuperation : Hacker projects may initially hold a promise of 
autonomy from either state or capital, but as they progress, they tend to be 
recuperated by such agencies. Recuperation thus “operates by replacing the 
goals and values that were initially professed by a community of hackers (users, 
citizen scientists, etc.) with new ones, which are better aligned with the needs 
of capital accumulation and state control” (44). This process is often resisted by 

56 Corruption 
the communities, but external pressures nevertheless tend to generate inﬁghting. 
Typically, one faction remains attached to the original values and design goals, 
and another seeks to accommodate that design to the external pressures. To 
avoid falling prey to such internal rivalries, they suggest, hacker communities 
must be technologically skilled, share common values and cultural references, 
and understand the history of previous generations of the same technology. 
Against disruption: Mantenere lo stato 
So far, we have encountered several ways of describing design projects whose 
trajectories have shifted as they have moved through the social world. The pre­
vious chapter spoke the language of “translation” and “drift”, and this chapter has 
used the language of “travel” and “degrading”, as well as “autonomy” and 
“recuperation”. These contemporary ways of labeling design failures can be 
placed alongside the language of Niccolò Machiavelli – the Florentine civil ser­
vant-turned-political theorist who was active some ﬁve hundred years ago. 
As already mentioned in Chapter 2, Machiavelli did not say much about design 
as such, but he did discuss innovation. Indeed, as John Pocock (1972, 1975) 
suggests, Machiavelli’s The Prince is not only a manual for how to gain and retain 
power – it is also a book about innovation. This concern goes beyond the oft-
repeated quote about the perils of innovation –“ that there is nothing more dif­
ﬁcult to take in hand, more perilous to conduct, or more uncertain in its success, 
than to take the lead in the introduction of a new order of things”. Rather, the 
question of innovation is at the heart of the argument, to be placed alongside the 
more (in)famous treatment of power. 
As noted earlier, one must understand The Prince through the triad of virtú, 
fortuna, and corruzione (Pocock 1975: viii; von Busch & Palmås 2016: 286). The 
ﬁrst term, virtú, suggests a connection to the term “virtue”, but in the case of 
Machiavelli, one must be cautious with that connotation. As we shall see later, 
The Prince is a satire of previous accounts of princely virtues. At this point, it 
suﬃces to say that virtú is that which a prince employs in order to counter two 
threats. The ﬁrst of the threats is the element of chance introduced by fortuna, the 
second is the general tendency towards institutional decay; corruzione. 
This tendency towards decay is central to Machiavelli’s worldview. Writing in 
the context of a republic under strain, his inclination was to favor stability over 
what he saw as its alternative – some unknown form of despotism. Machiavelli 
suspected that – in contrast to the D:Ream hit and subsequent New Labour 
anthem – things can only get worse. He was in a mindset in which the phrase 
“another world is possible” constitutes a threat rather than a promise. Thus, the 
actions of the prince should follow one overriding concern: How to maintain the 
state of things; mantenere lo stato. This sentiment can be transposed onto Papanek’s 
triangle: Designers trained to focus on “desired ends” and “preferred situations” 
may lose sight of all the undesired outcomes that their “programs” and “courses 

Corruption 57 
Better 
Worse 
FIGURE 4.1 The better or worse problem. 
of action” may generate. While design interventions must always strive for a 
better world, designers must equally be careful not to recklessly leave the world 
in a worse situation than they found it. 
Here, in relation to fortuna and corruzione, innovation (innovazione) enters as a 
fourth concept. This is what the prince can deploy as a means to restore order in 
a turbulent world. As Benoit Godin points out: 
To Machiavelli, innovation serves to stabilize, not to revolutionize. The 
world is changing constantly, hence the need for innovation to stabilize 
it. There is need to stabilize a ruler’s power and secure it through inno­
vation […] In contrast to modern theorists, the world is too stable and 
needs innovation to change it, even revolutionize it. 
(Godin 2015: 69) 
Machiavelli is thus a healthy antidote to what Jill Lepore (2014) has called “the 
gospel” around the “disruptive” innovation. For Lepore, a historian, the concept 
of disruption says something fundamental about our time: Just as “every age has a 
theory of rising and falling, of growth and decay, of bloom and wilt”, every age 
“also has a theory about the past and the present, of what was and what is, a 
notion of time”. Thus, disruption is construed as the benevolent force that gen­
erates progress. However, she also points out that it is a ﬂawed concept that rests 
on “shaky evidence”. Stemming from the work of Clayton Christensen (1997), 

58 Corruption 
and from a particular understanding of the work of Schumpeter, it is a relatively 
novel conception of innovation. Prior to Schumpeter’s casting of innovation as the 
creation of new products and services, Lepore argues, the term had chieﬂy negative 
connotations. The pre-schumpeterian approach to innovation associated it with 
“excessive novelty, without purpose or end”, and Machiavelli can productively be 
placed in that lineage. 
Strangely enough, Peck and Theodore’s discussion on PB makes use of the 
contemporary meaning of “disruptiveness ”. For them, a policy design like one 
originally operationalized by the Worker Party in Porto Alegre, is disruptive 
inasmuch as it constitutes a break with the status quo. However, this idea sits 
uneasily with their main lesson from the failure of PB. Again, alternative 
designs are necessary but not suﬃcient, as the corrosive eﬀects of the political 
environment cause designs to decay. Creating a disruption is one thing, sus­
taining it is another. Let’s review another example of this problematic, before 
concluding the chapter by reverting to Machiavelli. 
Microﬁnance: The quintessential social innovation 
Much like in the case of PB, there is a founding myth – an origin story – that 
animated the phenomenal propagation of the practice in the 1980s and 1990s. 
This origin story starts in 1976 in Bangladesh, when Muhammad Yunus founds 
Grameen Bank. In the introduction to his bestselling biography Banker of the Poor, 
Yunus explains how the idea for the bank emerged when researching and 
teaching economics at Chittagong University: 
The poor taught me an entirely new economics. I learned about the pro­
blems that they face from their own perspective. I tried a great number of 
things. Some worked. Others did not. One that worked well was to oﬀer 
people tiny loans for self-employment. These loans provided a starting point 
for cottage industries and other income-generating activities that used the 
skills the borrowers already had. […] I never imagined that my micro-lend­
ing program would be the basis for a nationwide ‘bank for the poor’ serving 
2.5 million people or that it would be adapted in more than one hundred 
countries spanning ﬁve continents. 
(Yunus 1999: ix–x) 
But that it did. Indeed, microﬁnance became the policy instrument that preceded 
PB as the “big idea” in development circles. The idea can be described as follows: 
Microﬁnance involves practices related to providing underserved communities 
with ﬁnancial services. Most notably, these practices have been deployed in low-
income economies, and in relation to poorer clients, who would otherwise not 
have access to ﬁnance. Thus, microﬁnance has been hailed as a means to mitigate 
the exclusion of these individuals from the ﬂows of credit, enabling them to 

Corruption 59 
become self-suﬃcient while at the same time generating development for the 
economies they operate in. The credit tends to be provided in the form of small 
loans, so-called microloans. Microﬁnance institutions diﬀer from traditional banks by 
focusing on providing ﬁnancial services for these communities, which – given that 
they tend to lack collateral, steady employment, and veriﬁable credit histories – are 
unlikely prospective recipients of traditional loans. Instead of relying on collateral in 
the form of real assets, the banks instead rely on social forms of collateral – such as an 
individual’s membership in borrower groups and other social networks – in order to 
assess the creditworthiness of clients and to ensure the repayment of the loans. 
Indeed, one of the main innovations of the pioneers of microﬁnance is to develop 
the speciﬁc banking practices that enable microﬁnance institutions to deviate from 
the rules of lending usually adhered to by traditional banks. 
One of the concepts that made microﬁnance such a popular idea was that of 
social innovation. In the rhetoric on social innovation that emerged in the 2000s, 
microﬁnance – and the origin story of Yunus and Grameen Bank – was billed as 
“t h e q u i n t e s s e n t i a ls o c i a li n n o v a t i o n” (Phills et al. 2008: 36). In the 2010s, scholars 
ﬂocked around the study of societal innovation processes, with publishers scram­
bling to put out major edited volumes that promote the concept (Franz et al. 2012; 
Nicholls & Murdock 2012; Moulaert et al. 2013). In many ways, the academic 
study of social innovation built upon previous work on social entrepreneurship 
(Leadbeater 1997; Nicholls 2006), and there are signiﬁcant overlaps between the 
two literatures. 
During the 2010s, microﬁnance also became the go-to example that policymakers 
used when introducing the notion of social innovation (European Commission 
2013a: 34). Arguably, the strong interest in social innovation partly stems from the 
fact that the public sector – notably the European Commission – threw its weight 
behind the concept. Indeed, social innovation can be understood as a “quasi con­
cept” (European Commission 2013b; McNeill 2006); though it may be found 
wanting in its analytical and empirical foundations, it nevertheless provides a dis­
cursive space in which academics and policy makers can meet. Thus, some of the 
handbooks and guides to the concept are published by public bodies and think tanks 
(Murray et al. 2010; European Commission 2013a). The Commission’si n t e r v e n t i o n 
on the topic is useful, as it describes social innovations as “innovations that are social 
in both their ends and their means”. “Social means” implies that the innovation is a 
process of co-creation in a social or collective manner, involving a set of stakeholders. 
“Social ends”, on the other hand, implies that social innovation produces socially 
beneﬁcial outcomes. 
As we shall see, these two objectives – achieving “social ends” through “social 
means”– do not always align with each other. Though there are many proponents 
of “social practice” or “social process”– not least within the design profession – the 
deployment of such design processes does not necessarily produce socially beneﬁcial 
outcomes. Indeed, the leveraging of “the social” may well produce unforeseen 
negative societal outcomes. In reviewing the case of microﬁnance, we will see that 

60 Corruption 
the rhetoric that posits that socially beneﬁcial results will follow from socially 
minded processes represents a false promise. Before embarking on this review, 
however, the next section will explore how the ﬁeld of design has come to 
embrace the notion of social innovation. 
Social innovation in design 
The imperative of social values in design has long been acknowledged, and so 
have the conﬂicts and politics involved in such collaborative endeavors, 
involving users (cf. Pottier & Orone 1995), trade unions (cf. Ehn & Kyng 
1987, 1991; Kyng 2010), and civic stakeholders at large (cf. Miessen 2010; 
Björgvinsson, Ehn & Hillgren 2012a). The use of the “social” preﬁxw h e n 
describing one’s design process has become commonplace for actors who wish 
to highlight the ethical quality of their endeavors. All designs can be socially 
oriented, and sometimes the social of design can emerge in a very speciﬁc 
artifact, such as a poster. For instance, design educator and writer Andrew 
Shea proposes a notion of “Community-Based Graphic Design” by which 
graphic design is to become an integrative part in mobilizing community 
engagement for “real world cases” with, for example, posters, websites, or 
murals (Shea 2012). At the other end of the scale, social design can deal with 
something as vast and open as the future. Thus, critical design proponents 
Anthony Dunne and Fiona Raby (2013) propose an engagement with the 
social in the form of speculative “social dreaming”. 
Dunne and Raby, however, explicitly distance their endeavor from social 
design as it focuses too much on “ﬁxing things” (160). Their straightforward 
rejection of social design as too focused on such ﬁxes highlights how the 
“social” part of design has become an ethical statement on design in itself. The 
fact that an exhibition like “Design for the other 90%” at the Cooper-Hewitt 
Design Museum was subsequently displayed at the UN oﬃce in New York 
suggests that design practices are increasingly deemed relevant for the humanitarian 
agenda (cf. Smith 2007). Similarly, book titles such as Design Like You Give a Damn 
by Architecture for Humanity (2006) and Design Revolution: 100 Products that 
Empower People (Pilloton 2009) show that design is no longer primarily concerned 
with glossy commodities. Indeed, it seems designers are ﬁnally heeding Papanek’s 
call for them to address the “real” world. 
How, then, do social design and social innovation relate to one another? 
Design theorist Ezio Manzini lays out a delineation between the two concepts, 
suggesting that social design primarily addresses particularly problematic social 
situations, often through a charity framework, and situations “to which both the 
market and the state fail to ﬁnd solutions” (Manzini 2015: 64). Social innovation, 
on the other hand, aims to produce “solutions based on new social forms and 
economic models” (Manzini 2015: 64). However, as Manzini acknowledges, the 
distinction between the two gets more blurred, 

Corruption 61 
social design is increasingly oriented toward social innovation, recognizing 
that this oﬀers the only chance for solving the problems it traditionally deals 
with. In turn, design for social innovation, facing the extensions of the eco­
nomic crisis, is more and more frequently involved in initiatives that involve 
socially sensitive issues. 
(Manzini 2015: 65) 
The movement Manzini addresses is also reﬂected in how entrepreneurs and business 
are turning extreme social situations, not least poverty, into new markets at the 
“bottom of the pyramid”. Here, the poor are no longer victims, but potential active 
consumers and self-employed entrepreneurs (Prahalad 2005). It is also reﬂected in 
terms such as “Jugaad Innovation” (Radjou et al. 2012) and “Frugal Innovation” 
(Leadbeater 2014), and, on the other hand, in how the hacker and maker move­
ments are thought to inform action in situations ridden by poverty, crisis, and limited 
resources. This perspective on “hacking” is not least exempliﬁed in the “Random 
Hacks of Kindness” initiative, where corporations such as Google, Microsoft, Yahoo 
collaborate with the United Nations to organize events structured as “hackathons”. 
Such events involve designers and programmers working on solutions for disaster 
management and crisis response, engaging UN Secretary General Ban Ki-Moon as 
keynote speaker (Walker 2010). 
Social innovation also tends to be discussed in relation to how creativity or 
reinvention may ﬂourish even though economic means are scarce. For instance, 
the UK-based design ﬁrm Participle proposes that social innovation implies a shift 
from a “ﬁnancial focus” towards a “resource focus” (Participle 2008: 12). Such a 
shift has previously been discussed in the context of social entrepreneurship: 
Indeed, Leadbeater’s (1997) landmark The Rise of the Social Entrepreneur posits that 
the social entrepreneur’s main asset is the social capital. Though s/he does not 
possess the ﬁxed capital stock that would normally serve as a platform for doing 
business, the entrepreneur may trade on the basis of alternative assets, such as a 
strong standing in the local community. As such, the social entrepreneurial 
endeavor builds upon social capital, as well as enhances it. However, what further 
forms of assets does the term social capital imply? 
Social capital, a term famously popularized by political scientist Robert Putnam, 
denotes “habits of cooperation, solidarity and public service” (Putnam 1993: 89– 
90), and can be divided into three aspects; trust, norms of reciprocity, and net­
works. Within development studies, social capital and trust have been presented as 
key components for building local community and democratic forms of governing, 
and have thus become the subject of research and measurement. Development 
economist Jose Cuesta states that “interpersonal trust is operationalized as an index 
capturing the reported trust in the people of the community; the belief that people 
are ready to help those in need” (Cuesta 2009: 38). As such, social capital appears 
to be a wholly benevolent force in society. However, the use of the concept may 
not always be beneﬁcial. The use of terms such as “social capital”, “community”, 

62 Corruption 
and “trust” can obfuscate the potential corruption of these forms of interpersonal 
relationships. As noted by political scientist Leah Gilbert, the social capital – often 
promoted as the pillar of democracy – also has its dark side: 
Social capital can in fact play an integral role in the achievement of ends 
contrary to the public good. Thus the same social capital that facilitates citi­
zens to collectively lobby for better governance may also enable organized 
crime groups to run an eﬀective racket. 
(Gilbert 2009: 57) 
As Gilbert suggests, one must diﬀerentiate between the “group-speciﬁc” social 
capital, for example that within criminal groups such as the maﬁa, versus “commu­
nity-wide” or generalized trust. However, the group-speciﬁc trust, often processed 
through distinctive customs such as initiation rituals or assignments in targeted vio­
lence, may sometimes overlap with the group-speciﬁc traditions and rites of local 
communities, especially those with very tight family bonds and hierarchies. 
This insight – that there is a dark side of the social – tends to be neglected in the 
literature on social innovation and design. Nevertheless, we argue that designers 
engaged in social design ought to bear this fact in mind. Similarly, designers ought 
not to confuse the “social” with the ideal public realm. According to development 
scholars Irene Guijt and Meera Kaul Shah (1998), participatory projects are often 
ridden by a naïve understanding of the term “community”. They suggest that 
projects often fail to regard the internal dynamics and diﬀerences of their stake­
holders, hiding biases, gender issues and many instances have an inadequate invol­
vement of women. Guijt and Shah especially notice how “participation” itself often 
obscures women’s worlds, needs and contributions to development by a simpliﬁed 
understanding of what community is (Guijt & Shah 1998: 7ﬀ). 
In order to discuss these problematics in further detail, we will now turn to the 
case of microﬁnance. The next three sections will interrogate the social reper­
cussions of this mutating innovation – again, with the aim of demonstrating how 
social means do not necessarily generate social ends. 
Anti-social innovation 
There is a large and diverse literature on microﬁnance, in which some authors laud 
the innovation, while others criticize it sharply. In order to disturb the notion that 
social processes produce socially beneﬁcial outcomes, the three sections that now 
follow will focus on some of the more critical voices on microﬁnance. In so doing, 
it will point to three problematics of this social innovation, outlining the anti-social, 
anti-political and anti-inventive eﬀects produced by its spread across the world. 
Among the numerous forms of critique directed at microﬁnance, some relate 
to economic matters. For instance, economists have argued that the economies of 
scale generated by large business entities are much more eﬃcient than micro­

Corruption 63 
entrepreneurship (cf. Karani 2007). A larger employer creates jobs and increases 
worker productivity, with a higher possibility of sustaining business and employ­
ment. A larger employer also adds more to the overall development of social and 
physical infrastructure than the smaller entrepreneurs. Another form of critique 
stems from the fact that microloans may be used to “smooth consumption”– 
tiding a borrower over in times of crisis. This may cause the recipient of the loan 
to make non-business expenditures, even purchases on status items, as opposed to 
the investments that the loan is supposed to be used for. A third form of more 
economic critique argues that the mechanisms of banking pushes money up the 
ladder towards the ones already wealthy, exchanging non-monetized assets into 
more debt for the poor. 
However, the literature also points to a problematic that emerges from the fact 
that – as mentioned above – this innovation draws upon social means. More 
speciﬁcally, this problematic emanates from the proposition of leveraging social 
capital as a substitute for collateral when ﬁnancial means and real assets are scarce. 
Critics argue that this idea can become corrupted to the point where bonds of 
trust and other modes of reciprocity are being exploited for monetary gain. This, 
in turn, allows the above-mentioned “dark side” of social capital to come to the 
surface. By cutting into the social capital of local traditions and interpersonal trust, 
microﬁnance may stretch social commitments, undermining the very commu­
nities that they are supposed to support. Thus, the leveraging of “social” processes 
produces anti-social eﬀects. This obviously jars with the ambition of social innova­
tions to leverage the reservoir of social capital – trust, norms of reciprocity, and 
networks – in order to enhance it, not deplete it. 
How does this breakdown of social ties work in practice? Social forms of col­
lateral may be created by a joint liability among the poor. In such arrangements, 
anthropologist Aminur Rahman explains, “the group as a whole accepts 
accountability for repaying the individual loans of the group members” (Rahman 
1999: 78). In this way, the group-lending scheme generates “social collateral”;a 
form of “organized social pressure from group members for its microcredit 
extension program”. This social aspect of the system cleverly uses social dynamics 
among the poor to reassure the repayment of the loans, to “punish one person by 
using one’s own people”. In these cases, where borrowers take responsibility for 
recovering the bank’s investment, tensions may arise: 
The collection of instalments in the [microﬁnance] centre by using the bor­
rowers creates feuds among peers. They become hostile to each other, but 
the bank loses nothing and gets back its invested loans. 
(Rahman 1999: 80) 
Critics have also pointed to a gender-related aspect of this issue. Women play a 
crucial – but not necessarily deserving – role in the leveraging of social collateral, 
and this is acknowledged by the issuers of microcredit. For instance, Sharma 

64 Corruption 
(2006) reports on how a district-level oﬃcial of a program, sponsored by the 
ministry of rural development in India, explains why the program focused on 
women: 
Women cannot go anywhere, they can be located easily; they cannot run away 
leaving their homes; they can be easily persuaded to repay as they feel shame 
more quickly and consider non-repayment as a betrayal of family honour. 
(Sharma 2006: 18) 
“Group-lending” or collective liability thus tends to be distributed in small 
communities of women. Here, a micro version of economies of scale emerges, 
inasmuch as the costs associated with asset reviews, project monitoring, and 
repayment enforcement are signiﬁcantly lower when credit is distributed to 
groups rather than individuals (cf. Armendariz 2005). Still, even though the loan 
default rate may be around 1 percent, the interest in microcredits can be as high 
as 50 percent (in the case of NGO lending) or 120 percent (in the case of formal 
microcredit banks) (Epstein & Smith 2007). The microloans are supposed to fuel 
the growth of small enterprises, but as Feiner and Barker notes, these industries 
based on home-based handicraft production “are almost all in the informal sector, 
which is ﬁercely competitive and typically unregulated; in other words, outside 
the range of any laws that protect workers or ensure their rights” (Feiner & 
Barker 2006). 
In this way, Rahman (1999) notes, the common narrative of women’s 
empowerment through credits may actually be the reverse, with “a signiﬁcant 
pattern of women borrowers systematically losing control over their loans; they 
are even victimized by the process” (Rahman 1999: 11). To some degree, 
women are used as hostages between the bank and the patriarchal men: 
Most women borrowers are not the direct benefactors of the credit extended 
to them. Instead, these women appear to be mediators between their male 
household members and the bank. Thus the lending institution invests loans 
in the village to generate proﬁt, but it uses the prevailing patriarchal norms of 
the village society and the positional vulnerability of women (immobile, shy, 
passive) for timely repayment and distribution of loans. 
(Rahman 1999: 23) 
To Rahman, this process of “disentitlement” is the reversal of Amartya Sen’s ideas 
of giving the poor “entitlements” to development, as a form of freedom. Here, 
microcredits instead amplify a form of micro-enslavement. Microcredit institu­
tions like Grameen Bank explicitly claim that women are more likely to repay 
their loans than men, but as Rahman notes, this is not because of some form of 
empowerment, but the opposite, because they are disciplined through cultural 
norms, such as shame, a speciﬁcally strong force contirubting to female 

Corruption 65 
submission. As Rahman sees it, through microcredits patriarchy is intertwined 
with lending mechanisms such as interest, creating “new forms of domination 
over women in society” (Rahman 1999: 51). 
In a similar vein, while the main narrative of microcredit tells the story of the 
woman buying a sewing machine to start a larger business, many of the entre­
preneurial opportunities coming from microcredit are so small and indiﬀerent 
they do not oﬀer any empowering experiences. Evan Selinger’s (2008) study of 
“Phone Ladies”– that is, women lending out mobile phones for short calls in 
Bangladesh – exposes no such empowerment, but rather “when phone ladies do 
their job, they are passive, invisible, deferential, and unremarkable. […] they are 
viewed more as a service than a human being” (Selinger 2008: 37). 
Critics argue that it is exactly this social mechanism that is the asset-turned-capital 
and the foundational social problem of microlending. The pride of individuals and 
social groups in developing economies, where social esteem is worth everything, 
makes them highly trustful customers, explaining the low default rate. And where 
loans are taken on social collateral and exchanged for social relations, the peer pres­
sure to pay is so great it leads many borrowers into both a social and economic debt 
trap, as “borrowers who fall behind realistically fear public embarrassment ” 
(Epstein & Smith 2007). This has increased the rate of suicides and ruined com­
munities, as the social capital is drained, and the meager income gained by the 
newly found micro-labor ends up beneﬁting loan sharks (Biswas 2010). 
Anti-political innovation 
Whereas much of the criticism leveled at microﬁnance focuses on the anti-social 
outcomes, there are also anti-political outcomes. Here, we are following Andrew 
Barry’s (2001: 268f) delineation between “the political” and “the anti-political”. 
According to this distinction, an act or phenomenon is political if it renders “ap r o ­
blem or object […] open to contestation and dissensus”. Conversely, an act or phe­
nomenon is anti-political if it closes down the space for contestation. For instance, 
the case of PB outlined above showed how the original policy design opened a space 
for discussing new ways of governing – another world is possible! – but this space 
gradually closed down as PB was neutralized and placed in the World Bank toolbox. 
Similarly, critics argue that the microﬁnance phenomenon has created a stiﬂing 
consensus on the development debate, ultimately yielding anti-political eﬀects. 
Microﬁnance has scarcely – even in the early days – been promoted as an 
anti-capitalist endeavor. Nevertheless, some of the initial hopes for microﬁnance 
were connected to hopes about how the civil society sector, or the so-called 
“social economy” or “alternative economy”, would claim a larger space within 
the economy as a whole. Indeed, in the early days, Muhammad Yunus’ Gram­
een Bank was run as a non-proﬁt. As it turned out, the spread of microﬁnance 
has buttressed the position of neoliberal capitalism as the dominant mode of 
economic governance. 

66 Corruption 
In order to unpack the claim that microﬁnance has had anti-political eﬀects, 
we may start from the proposition that the phenomenal spread of microﬁnance 
has come to buttress Muhammad Yunus’ assertion that “all people are entrepre­
neurs”. On that basis, the 2006 Nobel Peace prize laureate argues that credit 
should be seen as a “human right”, and the recognition of such a right would 
play a “strategic role in removing hunger from the world” (Yunus 1999: 143). 
This perspective is embraced not only by policy makers, private sector repre­
sentatives and non-governmental organizations (NGOs) – some artists also 
endorse this idea. For instance, the inﬂuential Danish group Superﬂex, draws on 
this idea when mixing art, development, and entrepreneurial thinking into a form 
of social art practice (Nacking 1999). 
However, this consensus has displaced alternative propositions on how to deal 
with world poverty. By fragmenting initiative and responsibility onto individuals, 
micro-solutions may render larger political and social change more unlikely. 
Feminist economists Susan Feiner and Drucilla Barker write: 
The key to understanding why Grameen Bank founder and CEO Muham­
mad Yunus won the Nobel Peace Prize lies in the current fascination with 
individualistic myths of wealth and poverty. Many policy makers believe that 
poverty is “simply” a problem of individual behaviour. By rejecting the 
notion that poverty has structural causes, they deny the need for collective 
responses. […] For neoliberals the solution to poverty is getting the poor to 
work harder, get educated, have fewer children, and act more responsibly. 
(Feiner & Barker 2006) 
Feiner and Barker thus suggest that neoliberals are active proponents of microﬁnance 
programs because they “do not change the structural conditions of globalization”,b y 
which they include “loss of land rights, privatization of essential public services, or 
cutbacks in health and education spending”. For Feiner and Barker, these structural 
conditions are the true factors that reproduce poverty among women in developing 
nations. However, the microﬁnance phenomenon has contributed to a political 
consensus that tends to overlook such factors. 
Such anti-political eﬀects have already been discussed in the context of parti­
cipative design. Cooke and Kothari (2001) point to the problems that arise from 
the idea that if the designer can just improve the participative nature of design 
practices, such practices will get better and form the basis for “best practice”. 
However, Cooke and Kothari suggest that there is a tyrannical potential in this 
tendency, as the focus on “how the practitioner operates” or on “the speciﬁcities 
of the techniques and tools employed […] embodies the potential for an unjus­
tiﬁed exercise of power” (4). Thus, participation has the tendency to mask the 
delegation of power and continued centralization – all in the name of “democ­
racy” and decentralization. This, in turn, resonates well with philosopher Jacques 
Rancière’s ideas on the current state of democracy. He posits that the type of 

Corruption 67 
participatory regime we normally refer to as “democracy” merely concerns the 
tiny space left unoccupied by the dominant order (1995). For Rancière, then, 
democratic participation has become disarmed, so as to deal with minor or 
even illusionary choices in matters where citizens do not threaten power. 
Thus, participation masks our inability to change the ruling order. 
Cooke and Kothari therefore suggest that practitioners of participatory methods 
need to ask themselves some important questions: “Do participatory facilitators 
override existing legitimate decision-making processes?”, “Do group dynamics lead 
to participatory decisions that reinforce the interests of the already powerful?” and 
“Have participatory methods driven out others which have advantages participation 
cannot provide?” (7f). Thus, we need to be more careful about 
the naivety of assumptions about the authenticity of motivations and beha­
viour in participatory processes; how the language of empowerment masks 
real concern for managerialist eﬀectiveness; the quasi-religious associations of 
participatory rhetoric and practice; and how an emphasis on the micro level 
of intervention can obscure, and indeed sustain, broader macro-level 
inequalities and injustice. 
(Cooke & Kothari 2001: 14) 
In the participation regime, the conceptual tools of development have been 
reformulated in a way that aligns it with the tenets of microﬁnance: Action has 
become an individual responsibility, and empowerment has lost its political con­
notation for a struggle for real power. In both cases, the answer to marginalization 
has become a question between perceived free and equal individuals, placing the 
locus of political debate far away from more structural issues. 
Anti-inventive innovation 
As a third problematic, one may also point to the anti-inventive eﬀects of micro-
ﬁnance. Again, we may draw upon Barry (2001), who posits that the dichotomy 
inventive/anti-inventive deals with “the degree to which a technological or poli­
tical change opens up the space of possibility” (268f). For instance, a certain tech­
nology may be anti-inventive if it “displaces or blocks oﬀ other [technical] 
possibilities”– note for instance how the internal combustion engine blocked oﬀ 
the development of electric automobiles during most of the twentieth century. In a 
similar fashion, the massive spread of economic practices and techniques associated 
with microﬁnance can be said to have blocked the emergence of alternative eco­
nomic practices. As such, the outcomes of the microﬁnance innovations run 
counter to the very promise of innovation itself – the idea that innovation always 
opens up new prospects for the future. 
In recent years, microﬁnance providers have sought to provide business train­
ing along with the ﬁnancial solutions oﬀered to clients (Karlan & Valdivia 2011). 

68 Corruption 
Some providers account for this development by referencing the above-men­
tioned issue of clients using the microloans for non-business expenditures. The 
Swedish industrialist-turned-microﬁnance philanthropist Percy Barnevik explains: 
Unfortunately, too much of the micro-credits have been spent on personal 
consumption, and that does not raise the standard of living. […]M i c r oﬁnance 
is, in itself, not a solution to the poverty problem, unless it is combined with 
comprehensive training. 
(Barnevik cited in Palmås 2011: 59) 
For this type of microﬁnancier, then, the provision of credit is merely one aspect 
in a larger project: To teach the recipients of loans to think in more businesslike 
terms, and to forge an economic mindset among the poor. This economic 
mindset is meant to correspond to the classic models of economics: The market 
mechanism as the thing that balances supply, demand, and prices; the rational, 
self-interested, utility-maximizing homo economicus as the preferred model of 
human subjectivity. The business training oﬀered to the poor serves the purpose 
of spreading this ontology of the market economy, and to supply the receivers of 
microﬁnance with the tools and techniques that accompany it: Elementary 
know-how on marketing, or on business ﬁnance. Through this operation, a 
replica of the classic market model for governing economies is put in place, 
instilled in the minds of the poor, but also hardwired into the tools that facilitate 
economic action. 
While this may not necessarily be a bad thing – well-functioning market 
arrangements can deliver excellent results – this aspect of microﬁnance invariably 
implies that the possibility for alternative modes of economic life is being blocked 
oﬀ. Indeed, there is a politics involved in the spread and adoption of the tech­
nologies that shape the economy. Callon (2005) brings this type of politics to the 
fore, and suggest that radical scholars that want to change how the economy 
works ought to study the “devices intended for all Davids dreaming of ousting 
Goliaths” (Callon 2005: 12). Researchers, he argues, can assist in the experi­
mentation with alternative economic logics, thus participating in an “inventive” 
eﬀort to pave the way towards radically diﬀerent ways of organizing economic 
life. Indeed, given that the economic arrangements based on self-interest follow 
a European lineage (Hirschman 1977), one may argue that alternative arrange­
ments are more likely to emerge in the Global South. However, the spread of 
microﬁnance currently seems to have the opposite eﬀect, cementing the mode 
of economic life that prevails in the richer parts of the world. In this way, a 
social innovation like microﬁnance may have eﬀects that are anti-inventive. 
Though innovation is invariably associated with the creation of the new, it may 
equally – paradoxically – buttress the old. 
So, to recapitulate, we have seen how the microﬁnance case sheds light on 
three problematics that social designers and innovators must relate to: Social 

Corruption 69 
innovations may produce anti-social eﬀects (corroding social capital, generating 
frictions); anti-political outcomes (displacing debates on political alternatives); and 
anti-inventive outcomes (suspending the development of alternative modes of 
economic life). 
At this point, it is important to note that Yunus does not seem to endorse this 
aspect of contemporary microﬁnance. On the contrary, he criticizes the way that 
contemporary capitalism is promoting a narrow-minded and self-interested 
human subject: 
The biggest ﬂaw in our existing theory of capitalism lies in its mis­
representation of human nature. In the present interpretation of capitalism, 
human beings engaged in business are portrayed as one-dimensional beings 
whose only mission is to maximize proﬁt. Humans supposedly pursue this 
economic goal in a single-minded fashion. This is a badly distorted picture of 
a human being. 
(Yunus 2010: xv) 
Yunus’ r e m a r k ss u g g e s t t h a t t h em o s tr e c e n tm u t a t i o no fm i c r oﬁnance produces 
eﬀects that run counter to the original aims of this social innovation. As such, 
he can be placed alongside the PB innovators in Porto Alegre, and the hackers 
that Söderberg and Maxigas study. Again, innovations are, by their very nature, 
unpredictable, and original intentions and objectives may well become displaced 
as they meet the corrosive eﬀect of the socio-political environment. This takes 
us back to the Machiavellian problematic raised earlier in the chapter: How can 
designers maintain the integrity of their designs? How can they, as it were, 
mantenere lo stato? 
Virtú and values in social design 
W e a r en o w i n ap o s i t i o nt or e t u r nt ow h a tM a c h i a v e l l i m a yt e a c hd e s i g n e r s 
that engage with social ends and means. As the reader may recall, we put the 
question of the real meaning of virtú aside, simply stating that it is the quality of 
the prince who manages to keep the twin threats of fortuna and corruzione at bay. 
Success in statecraft is, Machiavelli points out, a matter of luck – or lack thereof. 
Hence, virtú is the capacity to get lucky, and to prepare for cruel twists of fate. 
It is also the capacity to deal with the general tendency for decay. Still, 
Machiavelli does not provide a detailed list of the characteristics of such virtú, 
and this is because he is making a negative argument. Indeed, the chief con­
tribution of The Prince – and the thing that has made the book infamous – is his 
description of what virtú is not. 
As pointed out by Quentin Skinner, Machiavelli writes in the context of an 
already existing literature on princely virtues, and this literature essentially extol­
led that the cardinal virtues should guide princely conduct. Midway through The 

70 Corruption 
Prince, Machiavelli satirizes elements of these virtues, namely liberality (in the 
sense of being generous) and clemency (in the sense of being merciful). While 
accepting these virtues as worthy, he nevertheless argues that in practice, no 
prince has ever accomplished anything by following them in a slavish manner. 
On the contrary, such virtues are likely to get in the way of producing good 
social outcomes. This, in turn, is due to his bleak view of the social world: 
Those who “in all respects, will carry out only [their] professions of good, will 
be apt to be ruined amongst so many who are evil”.I no r d e r t o mantenere lo 
stato,o n e “must learn to be not always good, but to be so or not as necessity 
may require”. 
In other words, Machiavelli presents a dark view of politics and society – 
one that had previously not been presented. This, in turn, became a founding 
principle of the realist school of political theory. More than that, however, his 
book sought to shift the debate on statecraft from a previous preoccupation 
with intentions, to instead focus on political outcomes. In so doing, the book 
challenged the Christianity-based ediﬁce upon which polities had been built. 
That being said, Machiavelli’s position still represents a challenge to the 
design discipline. If we turn to current debates on values in design, they still 
tend to focus on intentions. Note for instance the interest in Value Sensitive 
Design (Friedman & Henry 2019), and in how to devise prescriptive design 
methods which moderate the values of participants, while also actively instilling 
human values into a design process. Here, we may also note how the notion of 
intentionality is central to contemporary interpretations of design thinking 
(Buchanan 1992; Nelson & Stolterman 2012). 
In summary, this chapter has reviewed two cases of social innovations – or, 
alternatively, two social designs – that both promised to remake society. Both 
of these – PB and microﬁnance – can be understood as failures, as they did 
not maintain the integrity of the original design, meaning that the original 
objectives were displaced. As such, they fell victim to the corrosive eﬀects of 
the socio-political environment. The chapter argued that Machiavelli’si d e a s 
on statecraft and innovation are useful as means to make sense out of these 
processes. Moreover, the argument showed that in the case of microﬁnance – the 
quintessential “social innovation”– good intentions ended up generating socially 
detrimental outcomes. Thus, the chapter pointed to how Machavelli’s focus on 
social outcomes, rather than intentions, may be used as a means to counter the 
tendency for the social design discussion to privilege intentions over outcomes. 
Machiavelli is notorious for his supposed amorality. Here, though, it is worth 
distinguishing between personal morals, on the one hand, and the morals of the 
sovereign, on the other. As we shall see in the discussion about hypocrisy in 
Chapter 6, political theorists have suggested there are good causes for not con­
ﬂating the morals of a person with that of a polity. This, in turn, leads to the 
question of how to position the role of the designer – is the designer just another 
person, or should the designer be understood more as a sovereign? While 

Corruption 71 
designers often see themselves as the former – as the designer on the side of the 
people – one may equally suggest that design has come to play an instrumental 
part of statecraft. The next chapter will thus problematize the notion of the 
designer who stands on the side of the weak, against power structures. This will 
put us in a better position to ﬁnd a new ethical position of designers, more in line 
with a realist mode of thought. 

5 
CUNNING 
Mêtis and designerly statecraft 
In the early years of the 2000s, Nordic governments experimented with setting up 
intra-government teams remitted with fostering design thinking and co-creation in 
public administration. In 2002, the Danish Mindlab started its operations, and in 
2008, Finland followed suit, establishing a lab of its own. For Marco Steinberg, 
design strategist and co-founder of Helsinki Design Lab, the initiative was led by 
one overriding objective: 
Design […] has a kind of capacity that was desperately needed in health care, and 
education, and rethinking systems. And governments were the owners of these 
problems. And so we felt these two communities needed to meet each other. 
(Valtonen 2020: 522) 
Helsinki Design Lab existed until 2013 and was the ﬁrst in the world to focus 
exclusively on introducing strategic design in government (Boyer 2020: 280). In 
an interview with Anna Valtonen, Steinberg explains the rationale for establishing 
a designated intra-government team for this purpose: 
The consultancy approach to trying to change this government was not going 
to work. For as long as design is something that is done to governments, it’sn o t 
going to fundamentally change government. Design needs to be in government 
to change it. And we need to begin to have conversations in government about 
design and good design literacy needs to grow in government. 
(Valtonen 2020: 522, italics in original) 
This chapter will interrogate how the nature of statecraft changes when design is 
in government, and ultimately ask what this means for the position of the 
DOI: 10.4324/9781003281443-5 

Cunning 73 
designer in relation to structures of power. Designers like to think of them­
selves as situated on the side of the people, against structures of power. What 
if contemporary modes of government have turned the tables? 
Thus, the chapter will set oﬀ from a case description of how design for 
government is construed in the context of Swedish municipal and regional 
politics, and then explore the place of design in political thought. Here, the 
focus will be on how certain forms of cunning and craftiness are associated 
with the concept of design, and on how this mêtis has been banished from the oﬃcial 
language on design. It will brieﬂyd i s c u s s s u c h “metic” aspects of design in the con­
text of tactical urbanism, and then move on to how design ﬁts into the notion of 
neuroliberal government. The chapter then focuses on “neuroliberal” aspects of the 
design-informed government in Finland, and concludes by relating the argument to 
previous accounts of cybernetic modes of government. 
Empathy is a trap 
“How do you know if grandma actually needs a hearing aid?” 
We are in a meeting room in a Stockholm Hilton Hotel, and I have just had 
breakfast among just-about-famous local politicians. Once a month, the Hilton’s 
generous conference spaces are rented by the nearby Swedish Association for 
Local Authorities and Regions (SALAR). During these days – Thursdays, by 
tradition – municipal and regional leaders from across the land assemble to gossip, 
state the case for decentralized power, and share experiences on how to improve 
local governance. 
Representing the Green party, I have been appointed to join other political 
representatives in a cross-party inquiry on the governance of publicly funded 
welfare systems. Together, we sit in on a series of private seminars with relevant 
academics and practitioners, and then distill the state-of-the-art in governance 
theory and method into a report, thus disseminating this body of knowledge to 
municipalities and regions. Though the inquiry was instigated by the Left party, 
in response to a growing a backlash against New Public Management (NPM) in 
Swedish welfare provision, this is ﬁrst and foremost a consensus-oriented cross-
party eﬀort. Thus, the two-year inquiry is essentially a two-year exercise in how 
to get politicians to not speak politically about fundamentally political issues. We 
therefore avoid referencing the political reality that necessitates the current NPM 
straitjacket – in comparison with similar countries, Sweden has gone far in pri­
vatizing and liberalizing publicly funded care and education. True, the inquiry is 
chaired by the Left party, but the vice chairman represents the major party on the 
right. Moreover, he is widely recognized as the pioneering politician who once 
instigated the privatization reforms, prototyping them in his local municipality. 
All the representatives in the inquiry are aﬀable people, and the banter is friendly, 
so we focus on purely managerialist matters. 

74 Cunning 
For these reasons, the inquiry ends up discussing innocuous-sounding concepts 
like “trust-based governance”. In the ﬁnal report, we promote the concept, 
although I cannot help thinking that it is a dud: It will never ﬂy, unless the cur­
rent system of privatized and liberalized welfare is repealed. More importantly, we 
also adopt the big idea promoted by the Swedish Association for Local Autho­
rities and Regions – focusing on user needs, implementing the principles and 
practices of service design. Hence the rhetorical question about the elderly lady 
and the hearing aid. 
The proposition that designerly practices may rejuvenate public services has 
been debated at length. In a conversation republished at the Harvard Business 
Review website, IDEO founder Tim Brown and design management scholar 
Roger Martin even suggested that design thinking may rejuvenate the “infra­
structure” of democratic capitalism (Brown, Martin & Berger 2014). These pro­
positions emerged at a time when design thinking had gained traction as a generic 
method for creative problem solving, supposedly applicable in a wide range of 
domains. Getting design thinking into government, Brown and Martin suggested, 
should be a matter of running experiments. Martin states that “what I want to do 
is do experiments, actually try stuﬀ out”, as this method is “the hidden secret of 
IDEO, sophisticated intervention design”. Such experimentalism suggests that 
design in government would generate a more vibrant and creative democracy, 
and more visionary modes of public governance. 
Such hopes aside, the question posed in the Hilton meeting room about grand­
ma’s hearing aid was of a more mundane nature. An administrative oﬃcial from the 
Swedish Association for Local Authorities and Regions is giving me a crash course 
in the uses of service design in governing welfare systems. The argument runs 
something like this: Today’s welfare systems are strained. Resources are decreasing, 
while needs are increasing. Therefore, welfare providers need to examine citizens’ 
needs, in order to ﬁnd where most value or impact is created. Better understanding 
of the user’s needs is crucial. For instance, imagine an elderly lady. She may have 
the right to a hearing aid, but does she really need it? 
I start to conjure an image in my head of a team of designers and civil servants 
engaging in all manner of exercises, “getting under the skin” of the elderly lady, 
intent on acquiring objective knowledge about her “real” needs. As I think about, I 
can’t decide if this use of design is merely sad, or outright sinister. Sad, because 
service design here emerges as a post-political coping strategy for propping up a 
failing welfare system. Sinister, as this activity instrumentally – deceptively, even – 
leverages empathy as a tool for stripping an elderly person of her rights to a hearing 
aid. Here, designerly empathy is deployed as a trap. It is all very IDEO noir. 
I later realize that the argument of using service design as a way of compen­
sating for decreasing resources is well-rehearsed within the Swedish Association 
for Local Authorities and Regions. It is put forward in educational videos and 
other materials, as part of an “innovation guide” for local authorities. One key 
audience for the guide is the growing body of civil servants working within the 

Cunning 75 
innovation units that have proliferated within local government. As with any 
new organizational development approach, it is promoted by a widening base of 
proselytes. It is likely to stay with us for some time. 
This is where the reader may suspect some form of condemnation of this 
development: Citizens with universal rights are recast as “users”, whose needs 
become subject to negotiations, orchestrated by the service-design-governmental 
complex. Yet, it is hard to argue against the very real challenge of having to make 
ends meet within welfare systems. Indeed, someone else may have more use of 
that hearing aid, and maybe the elderly lady is in need of some other, previously 
unimagined welfare service? There is a use for these practices, they do indeed ﬁt 
into the infrastructure of democracy. However, what the IDEO-inspired designers 
seem to underplay are the political negotiations and struggles that established the 
rights in the ﬁrst place. The rights of citizens are not just any chip to bargain with. 
Once this right is lost, grandma will not be in a position to get it back. The func­
tional empathy of the designer is not attuned to the harsh economic negotiations of 
a shrinking welfare state. 
The proposition that “empathy is a trap” is, some may have realized, a nod to 
Foucault’s famous argument about panopticism, in which “visibility is a trap”. 
This comparison only goes so far, but elements from his analysis are suggestive in 
the context of this argument. Thus, this development sees a dyad being dis­
sociated – not one of seeing/being seen, but of empathizing/being empathized 
with. It implies a mode of observation and surveillance, but also a laboratory 
which engenders new forms of knowledge. Indeed, as will be explored later in 
this chapter, designerly modes of entrapment are increasingly deployed for the 
purposes of experimental governance. 
However, there is also a distinct bleakness in Foucault’s analysis of panopticism. 
According to Foucault, the trap means that once subjected to a ﬁeld of visibility, 
the subject assumes responsibility for the constraints of power, inscribing his or 
her own agency within the principles of subjection. Such modes of entrapment 
can be functional and productive, to the point where they become integral to the 
smooth operation of modern organizational life. The fact that the turn to service 
design and user innovation in government may be stained with elements of the 
sad or the sinister is not necessarily grounds for condemnation. Rather, it is a 
problem that warrants further investigation – how to think about a polity that 
uses cunning and deception to pragmatically explore which principles can be 
breached? This investigation may start with a simple assertion: Empathy is a trap, 
but then again design has always been a matter of laying traps. 
Another footnote to Plato 
This proposition – that design has always been a matter of laying traps – can be 
formulated in a more precise manner. The act of designing has always been a 
devious enterprise, but we tend not to recognize this fact, as this type of cunning 

76 Cunning 
intelligence was once banished from the Western politico-philosophical lexicon. 
Therefore, designers tell themselves they work with form, function or well-being, 
rather than the more depraved heritage of their trade. 
As Benedict Singleton has suggestively observed in his 2014 dissertation, On 
Craft and Being Crafty, philosophically inclined design scholars tend to situate 
design in the vocabulary of the ancient Greeks, placing it between poiesis 
-(“making”) and techne (“art” or “skill”). However, the Greeks also used a third 
term – mêtis – meaning precisely the kind of cunning intelligence used for laying 
traps. More speciﬁcally, mêtis implies a “a complex but very coherent body of 
mental attitudes and intellectual behaviour which combine ﬂair, wisdom, fore­
thought, subtlety of mind, deception, resourcefulness, vigilance, opportunism, 
various skills, and experience over the years” (Detienne & Vernant 1991: 3). It is 
deployed in the context of uncertainty and unpredictability (Queiroz 2017: 227); 
“in situations which are transient, shifting, disconcerting and ambiguous, situa­
tions which do not lend themselves to precise measurement, exact calculation or 
rigorous logic” (Detienne & Vernant 1991: 3–4). 
-Singleton argues that mêtis – not poiesis,n o r techne – is the term that best 
captures the essence of design. As hinted above, in relation to traps, the Greek 
term is apt for describing the deviousness of elaborate design schemes. How­
ever, it also applies to more fundamental practices of craft. He references 
Brian Massumi’s description of how a woodworker transforms wood into a 
table –“ she does not indiscriminately plow into it with the plane”.R a t h e r , 
the woodworker “is conscious of the grain and is directed by it”,r e a d i n g i t , 
interpreting it, judging its “capacity to be aﬀected, or to submit to a force” 
(Massumi 1992: 10). Indeed, even in the most mundane forms of carpentry, 
“there is an element of deviousness, implied in the manipulation of the 
environment, coaxing eﬀects from it, rather than imposing eﬀects on it by the 
application of force alone” (Singleton 2014: 110). Or to put it diﬀerently, 
every design process is underpinned by an ediﬁce of cunning – one that 
designers tend to either overlook or shy away from. 
However, Singleton is not alone in seeing the connection between design and 
deception. Such connotations are legion when reviewing the historical usage of 
the term. “Design” can be understood as a scheme, plot, concoction or simula­
tion – indeed, as a kind of deception. Here, he references Vilèm Flusser’s reading 
of dictionary entries on design, in which he found a description stating that a 
“designer is a cunning plotter laying his traps” (Flusser 1999: 17). Thus, design is 
attached to a particular trope that states that traps are the embodiment of a certain 
kind of cunning intelligence – mêtis. This, in turn, is considered devious and 
ignoble. Like so many other thematics in Western thought, this trope owes a lot 
to the work of Plato. 
It is commonly held that Plato, in the texts Republic and Laws, lays the foun­
dation for Western political philosophy. Both seek to ﬁnd the Just and the Good, 
outlining what a perfect society – or at least a perfect political system – should 

Cunning 77 
T echne−/Poiesis 
Mêtis 
-FIGURE 5.1 The techne/poiesis or mêtis problem. 
look like. In the latter of the books, Laws, Plato makes it abundantly clear that 
traps do not have a place in this utopia. Whereas traditional modes of hunting – 
say, with spear or bow and arrow – are seen as virtuous, the practice of laying 
traps should be banned. To Plato, it represents a sneaky form of evasion from the 
proper eﬀort of hunting. 
This is not a simple matter of how to gather food. The stakes of using such lazy 
contrivances are higher than that: More abstract virtues are at play. Strength, 
honesty, and fair eﬀort should hold a privileged place in Plato’s perfect polity – 
deviousness and inauthentic artiﬁce should be rooted out. The underlying 
assumption is that practices of hunting, whether virtuous or ignoble, may inﬂu­
ence citizens’ wider approach to the social and political world. In Singleton’s 
words, Plato was concerned about “untrustworthy artifactual work that might 
bleed into the organisation of human aﬀairs” (102). The hunter’s spear is the tool 
of
 the hero, whereas the trap is the cunning device of the coward. The use of 
poison may poison the souls of the republic. This is understandable, given the 
nature of Plato’s overall project. Again, he was seeking a ﬁrm foundation for 
building the perfect polity. His endeavor was to ﬁnd fundamental truths that can 
serve as a bulwark against the treacherous tos and fros of an Athenian democracy 
made toxic by sophistry. 
Mêtis sits uneasily within such a program. The Greeks construed it as a cunning 
intelligence which could prove eﬃcacious in relation to means. However, it was 
deemed completely useless for judging whether a certain end was Good or not. 
Its uselessness for determining the Just and the Good meant that it cannot assist in 

78 Cunning 
Plato’s project to search for foundations for the Good life and the Just society. 
However, more importantly, the pure orientation towards eﬃcacy pits it against 
any ambition to establish a normative order, from which to judge social and 
political action. The problem lies in its potential for promiscuousness: Given that 
it lacks judgment, mêtis can serve any political program. It is an unreliable partner 
for both the Good and the Evil. Rather than being outright immoral, mêtis 
represents amorality. Singleton suggests that “Plato’s trap presents itself as a phi­
losophical riddle that he felt we should not try to solve, because we may learn 
something dangerous” (130). 
For all of these reasons, Plato expunged it from the lexicon he created. Singleton 
continues: 
Plato was able to write the moral and political ambiguities of mêtis out of the 
Laws because the project is to describe (what he sees as) a utopia; in the 
actual world, mêtis oﬀers itself as both a problem and as a resource. 
(130) 
This juxtaposition between a perfect utopia and the present world is one way of 
explaining why mêtis sits uneasily in Plato’s thought. However, there is also 
another juxtaposition at play here – that between Plato’s “world of forms” and 
what he called the sensible world. Plato famously argued that the former is the 
true reality, and the latter is a mere chimera – hence the eﬀort to build the per­
fect republic on abstract ideals. However, this ediﬁce is threatened by amoral 
designs, plots and schemes, concocted and played out in the mundane world of 
practice. The acid of the sensible world, burning through the principles of the 
world of forms. As such, it is precisely the kind of practice that realists see as 
quintessential to the understanding of politics and society. 
So, let’s return to the government practice of using designerly modes of empa­
thizing with grandma, in order to see if her right to a hearing aid can be deemed 
irrelevant. It is a practice that ﬁts the description of mêtis: It balances on the thin 
line between pragmatism and deception. It is a plot. The empathy may be real, the 
relationship between designer and user sympathetic, but the trap is still laid. Aﬀect 
is the bait. It is the honeytrap bringing designers and users together, luring them 
into shifting the scale from Rights to Eﬀects, with the aim of bypassing political 
contestation. It is about eﬃcacy, neither more nor less. It seeks to work with – and 
coax eﬀects from – the propensities of the “material” or “environment”.I ti s 
amoral – neither inherently Good, nor Evil. 
The art of the strong 
Again, one of the key suggestions that emerge from Singleton’s work is that 
-design scholars tend to describe design through the language of poiesis or techne, 
failing to see that mêtis is a more ﬁtting description of the essence of design. To a 

Cunning 79 
great degree, this is because Plato removed this mode of action from the political 
lexicon. Thus, there has been a collective forgetting of this “lost word”, causing 
scholars to often overlook the inherent deviousness of design. 
However, the concept of mêtis is not completely lost – it does indeed feature in 
prominent studies within the social studies and humanities. Singleton cites James 
Scott’s Seeing as the State, in which mêtis represents an adaptive, context-based 
mode of action, based on rules-of-thumb that can scarcely be codiﬁed. As such, it 
stands against the top-down, programmatic planning that is the subject of Scott’s 
general critique of states in action. Mêtis is the countervailing power that may be 
turned against the megalomaniac state. It is fair to say that Singleton shares the 
hope that this may be the case. 
The most famous proponent of this hope is, however, Michel de Certeau. The 
Greeks construed mêtis as a type of intelligence that could be used either by the 
strong against the weak or by the weak against the strong (Queiroz 2017: 228). 
However, in his hugely inﬂuential The Practice of Everyday Life, de Certeau states 
that mêtis “is close to everyday tactics”. In so doing, he assigns the Greek term to 
the tactics of his famous tactics-vs-strategy schema, in which mêtis and tactics are 
collapsed into an “art of the weak”. 
In other words, while Plato removed mêtis from the political lexicon, de Certeau 
reintroduced it – but in a way that fashioned it as a mode of action emerging from 
below, from the hands of the weak. In his usage, it is an acid that corrodes power – 
not, as per the Greeks, as an acid that corrodes principles. There is a signiﬁcant dif­
ference here: In the case of grandma’s hearing aid, mêtis is a principle-corrupting 
practice used not by the weak, but by the strong. In the terminology of de Certeau, 
the strong may still rule through strategy, but also through tactics. 
This implosion of de Certeau’s tactics-vs-strategy schema is highly visible in 
recent developments within urban design – incidentally the context in which he 
introduced the schema. Here, the trend has been for planning to move towards 
modes of action that are explicitly “tactical”, temporary, and minor. During the 
past decade or so, there have been a number of critiques against this develop­
ment, partly arguing that this shift implies that the tactical mode of action is 
indeed used as a tool for the powerful. 
For one, there are concerns that the interest in temporary use in the cities of 
the rich Global North fails to represent a break from the neoliberal urban devel­
opment, especially as it may be used as yet another means to boost private 
property values and drive gentriﬁcation. Moreover, as pointed out by urban 
sociologist Fran Tonkiss, current temporary use practices must be understood in 
the context of austerity urbanism, at times serving the purpose of keeping “vacant 
sites warm while development capital is cool” (Tonkiss 2013: 318). Further, there 
are voices pointing to the paradoxes emerging from the fact that professional 
planning has increasingly adopted tactical methods. This implies, urban scholar 
Oli Mould suggests, that they “cease to be tactical”, and merely become a part of 
the strategy of planning authorities (Mould 2014: 533). Similarly, when planning 

80 Cunning 
policy embraces temporary use, it may relegate previously informal activities into 
becoming illegal ones. Taken together, these developments upset de Certeau’s 
original schema. 
Along with these general and sweeping concerns, it is worth examining the ways 
in which such tactical urban design projects incorporate elements of mêtis and trap-
laying. First, one may argue that de Certeau’s concept of tactics is the notion of mêtis. 
For instance, the notion of “place proper”– a central dividing line between tactics 
and strategy – is sourced from mêtis. Thus, strategy is deﬁned as 
the calculation (or manipulation) or power relationships that becomes possible 
as soon as a subject with will and power (a business, an army, a city, a scientiﬁc 
institution) can be isolated. It postulates a place that can be delimited as its own 
and serve as the base from which relations with an exteriority composed of 
targets or threats (customers or competitors, enemies, the country surrounding 
the city, objectives and objects of research, etc.) can be managed. 
(36) 
Conversely, a tactic is “a calculated action determined by the absence of a proper 
locus” (37), which implies that the “space of a tactic is the space of the other”. 
Here, the original idea is that in a city, strategy is projected onto places proper, 
be they controlled by municipalities or private organizations. Tactics are then 
employed by the citizens, who are sneakily making do in the space of the other. 
However, in the case in which the service design-inspired government is probing 
grandma’s needs regarding a hearing aid, it is the strong who is performing tactics 
in the “proper place” of the weak. In order to determine grandma’s true needs, 
her personal cognitive space has to be charted. Empathy is a trap, because it 
constitutes a tactics for probing the desires and propensities of private individuals: 
In aﬀective proximity, power-plays disappear in the fog-of-war. 
In the case of grandma, this tactic implies “getting under the skin” of the 
individual user. However, in the case of tactical modes of urban design, it is the 
desire structures of broader publics that are probed and mobilized. One celebrated 
example of this mode of urban design can be found in the neighborhood that 
served as a case in Chapter 3. Let’s return to Gothenburg, and the regeneration of 
the harbor area. 
In the warmth of empathy 
Ten years on from the 2005 dialogue process described in Chapter 3, not much 
has changed on the banks of the Göta river. On the northern bank, there is an 
area called Frihamnen, consisting of great expanses of asphalt left desolate after the 
cessation of shipbuilding and shipping activities. Nevertheless, as the city of
