Gothenburg approaches its 400-year anniversary, the plan is for this area to be 
regenerated. The process has been set in motion by the creation of the temporary 

Cunning 81 
“Jubilee Park”– an enclosed part where concrete piers meet the asphalt desert – 
on which experiments in place-making have been set in motion. 
Around this time, in 2015, the most discussed experiment in the Jubilee Park is 
a public sauna. The structure stands on the waterfront, looking like a cross 
between a giant spider and a gray troll. The exterior of the brutalist construction, 
made from recycled industrial materials, gives it a menacing expression – a bit like 
an alien spaceship. Inside, however, there is a sleek birchwood interior, designed 
in the spirit of Scandinavian minimalism. Perhaps it’s what H.R. Giger and Alvar 
Aalto would have come up with after a long night together at the harbor tavern. 
The result is convincing, as the sauna has been widely covered in the media and 
won the Architecture Award of Swedish Association of Architects Gothenburg 
Region. The motivation reads: 
Like a stranded container it stands there. A slight unshaped ﬁgure with a 
corrugated surface, in the old basin of Frihamnen. The sauna gives vitality, 
contributes to new life and a new future. With its zest for life and will to 
create, the sauna becomes more than just a creative haven it also becomes a 
vitamin injection for the whole town. 
The structure was created in 2014 by the Berlin-based architecture ﬁrm Raumlabor. 
Bearing a name that is German for “space laboratory”, the studio is famous for its 
experiments in community engagement, and for pushing the boundaries of the role 
for architects in urban development. The studio has also been associated as one of the 
twenty-ﬁrst century trendsetters in tactical and temporary modes of urban design. In 
Gothenburg, their design brief ﬁtted well with this specialization. How does one 
build community engagement in an asphalt desert during a ﬁve-year timespan, in 
preparation for the big-scale development that will ensue after the Gothenburg 
jubilee? 
Like so many other cities with a history of heavy industries in the harbor areas, 
Gothenburg sees its future in the development of its rustic, declining, and emptying 
harbor areas. The Frihamnen area, or the “free harbor”, is centrally positioned 
geographically, just by the wide river that once served as the trading route on 
which the city emerged. With its location between the posh city center and the 
more neglected areas north of the river, the harbor area is in line for redevelopment 
and gentriﬁcation. According to Raumlabor, the project set out to “preserve the 
memory of the site by attempting to intertwine with old structures, both physically 
and emotionally”. 
A sauna is emotional architecture. As the studio worked with city residents to 
build the structure, the aim was to mobilize the construction process as a meeting 
place. This shared eﬀort, the joint labor of architects and citizens, would 
strengthen the sense of community across the river, as well as across cultures. In 
an interview with the design site Dezeen, Raumlabor argues the sauna serves a 
public function beyond merely recreation: 

82 Cunning 
Public baths were once an intense place for social gatherings in our cities […] 
They were places not only for relaxation and sport but also for politics, dis­
cussion, business deals, eroticism, hedonism and crime. This has been lost in 
our cities and substituted with the more bleak and leisure-based public 
swimming pools and spas. 
(Frearson 2015) 
It is an interesting statement by the architects, inclusive and idealistic, as the city 
of Gothenburg does not – at least in the public narrative – usually sponsor the 
construction of structures explicitly designed for eroticism, hedonism, and crime. 
This is a testament to the particularity of tactical modes of urban design: They 
seem to be able to circumvent some of the formal norms and rules of traditional 
modes of planning. In her dissertation Towards a Minor Urbanism (2019), Ida 
Sandström describes how Raumlabor’s sauna operated on the basis of a logic of 
trials and prototyping. In the Jubilee Park, this was a means 
to gain acceptance for ideas and designs that would probably not be approved 
if presented as permanent solutions. Although built in public, the making of 
urban prototypes seems to ﬂy below the radar of formal instances concerned 
with the city’s building practises; meaning that the built prototypes […]a r e 
not perceived as buildings, but rather as events, by some instances of the city. 
(212) 
Here, Sandström references Jill Stoner, whose previous work suggests that these 
modes of urban design turn buildings into “opportunistic events in response to latent 
but powerful desires to undo structures of power as such” (Stoner 2012: 6–7). 
Indeed, there is a way to tell the story of the Raumlabor sauna in a manner that is in 
line with the standard de Certeauian refrain: The tactics of the less powerful under­
mined the strategies of the powerful. Architects and citizens join hands and together 
build the Good. While the design brief implied that the sauna would be a temporary 
structure to be demolished in 2020 – again, in Tonkiss’ terms, keeping a vacant site 
warm while development capital is cool – the brutalist spaceship is still standing tall. 
In fact, the sauna became more popular than most people thought it would 
be. Notably, planners failed to anticipate this popularity. So, while the sauna 
was not on the maps of long-term plans in 2015, it was gradually accepted as a 
permanent structure. While “the planning authority was not initially able to 
assimilate the catalysing power that the prototypes were generating” (Dahl 
2017: 83), it subsequently yielded to the interest in the structure, and had to 
“recognise the temporal construction as something more lasting” (84). In other 
words, the below-the-radar sauna – championed by the lower-level planners in 
charge of the Jubilee Park – ended up trumping the larger level planning 
authority. To use the vocabulary used above, the sauna became a trap, in which 
the powerful strategic planners got caught. 

Cunning 83 
This story of the Gothenburg sauna is uplifting, but it is nevertheless worth 
examining the broader development in which it is inscribed. The sauna did indeed 
trap high-ranking urban planners, who may not have been ready for its success, but 
it was also meant to probe the desires of users. Against all odds, the sauna was 
exceptionally successful in producing conviviality – but like all design, it entails an 
element of deception. To varying degrees, these kinds of urban prototypes are like 
the sauna featured in the classic Icelandic ﬁlm The Shadow of the Raven of 1988: The 
protagonist is invited into a sauna by his antagonist, ostensibly to make peace, but 
as it turns out, the structure is set as a trap, a boiling cauldron of deceit, under the 
unexpecting stakeholder. 
Sandström suggests that urban prototyping represents “a more general tendency 
in contemporary western planning to replace long-term planning policies by 
aﬀective and performative interventions with an immediate feedback loop” (212). 
Taking its cue from “lean” entrepreneurialism, it is deemed “a quick way to get 
started with an idea without having to process policies or spend too much time 
analysing possible outcomes”. However, what urban prototyping seems to borrow 
from contemporary start-ups is also the tendency to mobilize and exploit the 
aﬀective realm. In mobilizing the “aﬀective and ludic”, Nigel Thrift suggests, the 
capitalist ﬁrm has moved “from an emphasis on an overall strategy to something 
more closely approximating de Certeau’s tactical interventions”. Again, as suggested 
above, powerful organizations are “increasingly utilizing the weapons of the weak – 
contextual ﬂeeting practices – to make themselves strong” (Thrift 2005: 13). 
So, what urban design shares with the lean start-up scene is the proﬁciency in 
acting in the “space of the other”. This is a space of uncharted and as yet 
unspoken user desires; a mysterious place to which there is no map. Rather than 
the panoptic vision enjoyed by the strategic actor (de Certeau 1984: 36), the 
predominant tactician of today probes these unknown territories through trial and 
error, one beta test at a time, giving rise to what could be referred to as “an 
opaque and blind mobility” (93). 
This turn to tactics in urban design has a number of implications. If nothing else, it 
prompts us to reconsider notions of power in how cities are designed and planned. 
For instance, David Harvey’s (1989) well-rehearsed analysis of how the managerialist 
modes of planning of the 1960s gave way to entrepreneurialist modes of urban 
governance. Harvey famously pointed to how the entrepreneurial mode of urban 
development that emerged in the 1980s involved PPPs (public-private partnerships). 
However, present-day entrepreneurialism – as displayed in the Jubilee Park – also 
enrolls civil society organizations in urban development. Second, Harvey pointed to 
the “speculative construction of place” (1989: 8), suggesting that ﬁnancial and real-
estate speculation played a part in entrepreneurial urban governance. Today, the 
speculative element in urban governance also entails the kinds of speculative pro­
cesses borrowed from start-ups – the probing of potential desires of would-be users, 
modulating empathy into an operational tool for transforming the area according to 
the underlying plans of “cleaning up” or “civilizing” the area. 

84 Cunning 
However, the comparison with Harvey’s work on cities leads us on to the 
wider implication of introducing design-oriented approaches in governance. 
Harvey’s approach to urban development has famously been wedded to the idea 
of neoliberalism (Harvey 2005, 2008) – a concept that featured in the previous 
chapter, in the context of post-politics. As suggested in that chapter, it is still 
productive to understand some practices of co-design in that context. However, 
there is a politico-economic development in which design is playing a sig­
niﬁcantly larger role. As it has converged with behavioral economics, the cunning 
intelligence of design has come to form a novel approach to statecraft. 
Neuroliberal experiments 
As hinted above, the tendency to chart and mobilize the aﬀective realm stretches 
beyond urban design, to the point where the use of design-informed experiments 
in public governance has been dubbed “the new spirit of policy-making” (Kim­
bell & Bailey 2017). Thus, design is not only deployed as a practical means to 
improve and streamline welfare provision, as in the case of grandma’s hearing aid. 
It has come to inform the very modus operandi by which states govern. Here, 
one could point to the introduction of various forms of nudging in government. 
However, nudging practices – though phenomenally successful in terms of their 
proliferation across the world – need to be understood in the context of a broader 
shift in statecraft. 
Among other things, the neoliberal project entailed wholesale attempts to 
privatize assets, marketize relations, and incentivize individuals. The aim was to 
secure social coordination and achieve policy goals through the harnessing of 
actors’ supposed rational self-interest. However, the current position of this 
mode of governing has been called into question. There are, of course, a 
number of critiques of neoliberalism, put forward by those who are ideologically 
opposed to such policies. Arguably, there is a growing recognition that negative 
outcomes of neoliberal policies, such as rising personal debt or climate change, may 
need to be addressed in a more state-interventionist manner. Thus the allure of the 
“libertarian paternalist” aspects of nudging, which allow for more interventionism, 
while staying clear from harder, more politically unpalatable forms of regulation. 
However, the waning inﬂuence of neoliberalism also stems from the fact that the 
neoliberal account of human nature has fallen out of favor. Famously, behavioral 
economists have long argued that in actual, everyday situations, people tend to not 
act rationally – placing a huge question mark over any kind of neoliberal policy 
reform. Given these developments, Rhys Jones and Mark Whitehead suggest, 
we may be witnessing somewhat of an existential crisis among state practitioners 
as they come to realise, belatedly, that they have lost their capacity to know, 
predict, control and discipline their populations and economies in eﬀective and 
politically acceptable ways. A behaviourally informed and experimentally 

Cunning 85 
predisposed state is said to provide a technocratic and seemingly neutral solution 
to these conundrums. 
(Jones & Whitehead 2018: 326) 
The governing principle of this behaviorally informed and experimentally predis­
posed state is neuroliberalism. As suggested above, this may be understood as the 
successor to the neoliberal project – as a reaction to perceived shortcomings of 
neoliberalism. However, more fundamentally, it rejects the neoliberal under­
standing of humans as rational market actors, and suggests that human behavior is 
composed of a much more varied set of rational and “irrational” drivers. As such, it 
deliberately targets and mobilizes “the desire lines of human behavior”, including 
inertia, herd instincts, emotional responses, and short-term thinking. Again, this pits 
it against neoliberal policies, which eﬀectively set out to suppress these very irra­
tionalities (Whitehead et al. 2017: 25). In short, neuroliberalism entails an 
increasing capacity of states, corporations, and non-governmental organizations 
to govern through a series of more-than-rational registers of human action 
(including habits, heuristics, emotions, aﬀects, and social and environmental 
contexts), and to skilfully fuse behavioural power with liberal notions of 
freedom. 
(Whitehead et al. 2019: 633) 
This ambition is made possible by the introduction of design approaches in the 
process of governing. If previous modes of governance focused on changing 
minds, thus promoting a certain kind of rationality, neuroliberalism is a matter of 
changing the context in which decisions are made. This shift in emphasis has 
been borrowed from the ﬁeld of cognitive design. So, while behavioral econo­
mists demonstrated predictable forms of irrationality hardwired into our brains, 
cognitive designers like Donald Norman showed how our ergonomic environ­
ments can shape our behaviors (66). So, a designerly approach to thinking about 
human action is at the heart of neuroliberal modes of governing. 
This link between design and the experimental state can be demonstrated by 
studying the case of “Experimental Finland”: A high-visibility initiative to promote 
an experimental culture in Finnish government, rolled out by Prime Minister Juha 
Sipilä’s Government during its 2015–2019 term. Early on during the initiative, the 
vision of a design-informed experimenting state was set out in reports such as 
“Place to Experiment! Finland on the road to an experimentation society” (2013) 
and “Design for Government: Human-centric governance” (2015). The latter 
report proposed an “operating model” for how to run experimentation programs, 
suggesting that the Sipilä Government should devise a two-year window to run 
one or several such programs. 
The Design for Government operating model implied a three-stage approach: 
First, understanding the policy design problem, second, running an experiment, 

86 Cunning 
and third, evaluating the outcomes. During the ﬁrst stage, the “design of the 
experiment would ﬁrst concentrate on building empathy and broad under­
standing of the problem by using qualitative methods and design thinking”. The 
later phases would “emphasise measurability of its impact”, and a “thorough 
evaluation of its results”. In order for this to work, the proposed operating model 
speciﬁed that experiments would be run by external parties who had experience 
from design and behavioral science. 
The ﬂagship experiment that was implemented on the basis of the Design for 
Government operating model was the much-publicized basic income trial. 
Implemented during a two-year period – between January 2017 and December 
2018 – the experiment caught the attention of international observers, pre­
sumably because it fused utopianism with scientiﬁc rigor. It was a mandatory 
state-led endeavor, involving 2,000 randomly selected unemployed individuals. 
The selected persons received a monthly basic income of 560 Euros from the 
National Social Insurance Institution (Kela). In true RCT (randomized controlled 
trial) form, a control group was set up, consisting of 178,000 citizens who 
received regular unemployment beneﬁts. 
As described by Mona Mannevuo (2019) the trial was positioned somewhat 
uneasily between being a project about well-being and health projects, while also 
aiming to create stronger work incentives, encouraging citizens to take responsi­
bility for their own lives. The design-informed experiment thus set out to explore 
a key question that tends to re-emerge in any discussion about the idea of a basic 
income: How do unemployed individuals react to receiving a basic income, in 
which the amount is equivalent to the regular unemployment beneﬁt, but the 
income is unconditional, does not require means testing, and is not reduced for 
earned income? 
As it turned out, the results from the experiment were inconclusive, at least as 
regards unemployment outcomes. This is partly due to the fact that midway 
through the experiment, the government introduced a new activation policy. 
However, it seems the basic income recipients enjoyed better economic well-being 
than the control group, which received the traditional unemployment beneﬁt. It 
seems they also grew more trusting of institutions and of other people. Still, the 
breakthrough insight that emerged from this experiment was not about basic 
income, but about the very experiment itself. Mannevuo suggests that 
the experiment was actually a trial not about basic income but about using 
randomised controlled trials in public policy-making. Finnish economists 
have frequently stated so, most recently in an interview published in The 
Guardian: ‘People think we’re launching universal basic income. We’re not. 
We’re just trialling one kind of model, with one income level and one target 
group’, meaning that this is not a breakthrough for basic income but more ‘a 
real breakthrough for ﬁeld experiments’. 
(Mannevuo 2019: 5) 

Cunning 87 
For one, the very fact that the government was able to run a mandatory ﬁeld trial 
with unemployed – one that probed their socio-psychological response to an 
experimental policy – was a feat in itself. Indeed, Mannevuo observes, the Finnish 
Constitutional Law Committee had to bypass Article 7 of United Nations 
Document A/2929, which prohibits states from conducting medical and scientiﬁc 
experiments without the free consent of the individuals involved. Article 7 was 
deemed irrelevant, as it is intended to prevent abuses in medical experiments. 
This experiment, they argued, was intended to increase well-being – and it 
appears that this rhetoric of good intentions won the day. 
Further, it is worth noting that this is – to borrow the words of Roger 
Martin – an exceptionally “sophisticated intervention” if there ever was one. As 
yet, it is probably the most rigorous large-scale exploration of the cognitive and 
mental predilections of a signiﬁcant number of private citizens. In true neuro­
liberal fashion, the intervention – and its modes of evaluation – focuses on both 
the behaviour, and the mental state, of the persons involved. Thus, under the 
guise of empathy, its object of inquiry is the nexus between actions and cogni­
tion. This, Mannevuo observes, follows an essentially cybernetic understanding of 
human action. More speciﬁcally, it dovetails with cyberneticist William Ashby’s 
contention that “the brain is not a thinking machine, it is an acting machine. […] 
It gets information, and then it does something with it”. In concluding this 
chapter, the argument will now explore how this aﬃnity between design and 
cybernetics inﬂuences contemporary statecraft. 
Cunning uses of feedback 
As hinted above, there is a clear connection between the emerging neuroliberalism, 
on the one hand, and cybernetics, on the other. To some degree, the missing link 
is provided by the ﬁeld of design. Indeed, there is a shared history between 
cybernetics and the ﬁeld of design. In the 1950s, the design education at the Ulm 
School of Design (Hochschule für Gestaltung Ulm) set oﬀ on an ambitious attempt 
to turn design into a scientiﬁc activity, linking it to the new science of cybernetics 
(Glanville 2007: 1176). 
Cybernetics, as deﬁned by Norbert Wiener (1948), is “the scientiﬁc study of 
control and communication in the animal and the machine”. Wiener founded 
this new science of control and communication, which hinges on how to close 
the gap between the desired behaviour of a system, on the one hand, and the 
actual behaviour of that system, on the other. Cybernetics can thus be con­
strued as a generalization and universalization of the ﬁeld of control engineer­
ing: What if everything – machines, plants, animals, humans, man-machine 
interactions, societies – can be understood through the principle that any kind 
action is a mere response to feedback from the environment? If the world can 
be understood in this way, how can order be created – in technological systems, 
and in socio-political systems? 

88 Cunning 
In 2010, as a response to this cybernetic vision, an anarchist collective called 
Tiqqun published a text that can be thought of as a precautionary tale. The Cyber­
netic Hypothesis is an account of the profoundly political eﬀects that have emerged as 
polities have sought to embrace principles from cybernetics. For Tiqqun, the 
cybernetic hypothesis constitutes “a new fable that after the Second World War has 
deﬁnitively supplanted the liberal hypothesis”. Whereas the liberal hypothesis was 
based on the idea of the essentially animal spirits of self-interested individuals, the 
cybernetic hypothesis imagines human behaviour as subject to reprogramming. 
Here, there is a signiﬁcant overlap with the above-mentioned accounts of a nascent 
neuroliberalism. 
According to Tiqqun, the cyberneticist sees “each individual behaviour as 
something ‘piloted’, in the last analysis, by the need for the survival of a ‘system’ 
that makes it possible, and which it must contribute to”. In this way, the survival 
of the system – of the status quo – is secured through intricate mechanisms of 
feedback. “Retro-action” is “key to the system’s regulation”, where signals of 
disturbances are followed by actions that push the system back to an equilibrium 
state. Cybernetics, then, “is the project of recreating the world within an inﬁnite 
feedback loop”. 
This nightmare vision of a totalitarian cyberneticist society – a social system 
which treats all signals of dissent as “events of disturbance” to the equilibrium, 
which in turn are brought into a monstrous apparatus of feedback loops – does 
read like science ﬁction. However, beyond the hyperbole, there is one key lesson 
to be taken away from this account: Polities’ use of constant feedback from citi­
zens is in no way a guarantor of democratic governance – especially if the explicit 
aim is to use this technique to secure the current order. Indeed, the Communist 
Party of China is becoming increasingly skilled in getting close to the needs of 
their user-citizens, experimenting with opinion polls and focus groups. 
This connects to the argument from which this chapter set out: What are the 
actual eﬀects of design in government? Is it a guarantor of a vibrant and creative 
democracy, and more visionary modes of public governance, or can it be used for 
other purposes – in the sad aspects of welfare provision, or even in somewhat 
sinister modes of statecraft? 
Here, one may revert back to the nightmare vision of Tiqqun: For the 
anarchist collective, the fear is that protests or calls for radical change that 
threaten the current institutional framework can be interpreted as feedback 
signals that necessitate a cybernetic response. While statecraft has always been 
about pre-empting such protests, the “neuro” aspect of contemporary govern­
ance suggests that such pre-emption may strike at a pre-protest stage, on the 
level of cognition. Again, this is hyperbole – and again, The Cybernetic Hypothesis 
should be read as a cautionary tale. Still, this is just to reiterate the point that 
design may be used in both democratic and undemocratic government. Again: 
Mêtis can serve any political program, hence the suspicions of Plato. 

Cunning 89 
A more modest take on this question may be arrived at by revisiting the famous 
1920s debate between Walter Lippmann and John Dewey, which concerned the 
role of the public in the ever-more complex, and ever-more mediated, technolo­
gically advanced modern societies. The exchange by the two public intellectuals 
started from Lippmann’s (1922) critique of the idea that citizens in democracies are 
capable of making informed decisions. For Lippmann, ordinary citizens cannot 
perceive all the intricacies of “the real environment” in which they live, and must 
therefore live their lives according to mental images of the world. The fact that 
they have to resort to such “ﬁctions”, with only a limited connection to the real 
world raises a number of risks. Therefore, Lippmann called for the abandonment of 
“the theory of the omnicompetent citizen” (Lippmann 1922: 364) which under­
pins traditional notions of democracy. Instead, he proposed that “centres of man­
agement” should be engaged in “the manufacture of consent”,p r e p a r i n g ac e r t a i n 
number of well-informed opinions. These readymade opinions would then, in 
turn, be presented to the citizenry, which would choose the appropriate one. The 
resulting system would be a form of “guided democracy”. 
Dewey (1927) responded to Lippmann’s critique, arguing that citizens can be 
active participants in the deliberations that lead to well-informed opinions. 
According to him, joint issues of concern “spark a public into being” (Marres 
2005), drawing select citizens into one of a multitude of concurrent deliberations. 
In these, the citizen is a creative participant, not merely choosing from the well-
formed opinions supplied by elites. In this way, Dewey promotes a “democratic 
experimentalism”. Given his preference for direct forms of participation rather than 
representative modes of democracy (Sabel 2012: 40), this implies that the individual 
citizen is the agent of experimentation, actively participating in a cooperative 
inquiry. Thus, rather than being dependent on molders of opinion that elicit the 
voices of citizens, Dewey insisted that democracy implies that citizens develop their 
own vocabularies. This enables them to speak for themselves, even on technical or 
complex issues, using their own words. 
The contemporary discourse on design in government tends to use a rhetoric 
that seems close to the Dewey “democratic experimentalism”. However, the 
cases laid out in this chapter show how design in government seems to be even 
more compatible with Lippmann’s “guided democracy”.1 All the cases described 
above – the use of service design in the Swedish public sector, the sauna in the 
Gothenburg harbor, the Finnish basic income experiment, and the broader neu­
roliberal movement – use the term “co-design” when describing their practices. 
However, it seems diﬃcult to get away from situations in which the designer acts 
as the prime experimenting agent. User-citizens invariably play a secondary role, 
with their inputs being elicited by the iterative interventions of the designer. 
Again, this is not a condemnation of this development. There are clear merits 
to Lippmann’s analysis of the shortcomings of democracy, and most democracies 
need some element of technocratic guidance. However, there are nevertheless 
problems that arise from the idea of a guided democracy. The “manufacturing of 

90 Cunning 
consent” that is a prerequisite for this democratic model does invariably place power 
in the hands of elites. This implies that designers in government are – to varying 
degrees – complicit with power structures, and this fact must be internalized in the 
self-understanding of designers. 
Here, one may note an analogy between how designers, on the one hand, and 
public relations professionals, on the other, may support existing power structures. 
One key contributor to the discussion on guided democracy was Edward Bernays, 
the father of modern public relations. In his landmark text on propaganda, Bernays 
(1928) argued that the “chaos” of diverging views among the citizenry had to be 
“organised”, in order for democracy to work. The new task of organizing this chaos, 
he suggested, was to be ﬁlled by the emerging public relations (PR) profession. This 
analysis proved prescient: Since then, the political realm has become permeated 
by these techniques of persuasion and coercion. Today, one may discern a similar 
connection between design and political power. 
This tendency needs to be recognized within the design profession, as well as 
among design scholars. If designers in government have become powerful persons, 
what kind of power do they wield? Are designers the quintessential technocrats of 
the twenty-ﬁrst century? Alternatively, are they the well-speaking, truth-agnostic 
people that Plato saw as the main reason democracies failed to produce Good 
governance: In short, are they present-day sophists? Most likely, they are both, and 
the next chapter will explore ways of addressing this fact. 
In summary, this chapter has placed contemporary design in the context of 
recent developments in statecraft. First, there is the tendency for governments to 
put service-design methods to work, using them to cut welfare costs by construing 
citizens not as subjects with rights but as users with needs. Second, contemporary 
modes of governing can no longer be construed – following de Certeau – as stra­
tegies, which can be countervailed by the tactics of the weak. Instead, tactics have 
become an art of the strong, to which designers sometimes contribute. Third, there 
is the tendency in which governments translate insights from design and behavioral 
sciences into “neuroliberal” policies of guiding citizen behaviors. Again, these 
observations are not cause for condemning such practices: Like it or not, this is 
how society is made today. However, these developments suggest that designers 
may need to revise their views on their place in relation to centers of power. 
Note 
1	 Note the quote from Italian designer Bruno Munari, printed on the walls of the Design 
Museum in London: “Progress means simplifying, not complicating” (Glanville 2007: 
1196). Much like Lippmann’s “centers of management” simplify the messy reality to 
citizens, one may imagine design thinkers as professionals that simplify the world for users. 

6 
HYPOCRISY 
Of virtue and vice 
“Dare to be naive”.T h o s e a r et h e ﬁrst words in the ﬁrst volume of Buckminster 
Fuller’s Synergetics. Published in 1975, the book famously outlines a distinctively 
Fulleresque approach to geometry, energy and design. The incitement to naïveté is 
meant to counter skeptical claims that in the modern world, there is limited scope 
for further discovery. The “moral of the work”, then, is that the solving of one 
problem always yields a great number of new problems to be solved. Continued 
experimentation and discovery is always possible. 
However, when reviewing the argument so far, we can see how participative 
and socially oriented design can be a sordid business. Chapter 3 showed how co-
design processes are rife with betrayals – how they operate on the principle that 
participants’ interests are likely to be traded away in the process of goal translation. 
The subsequent chapter outlined how social designs fall prey to corruption – their 
integrity is compromised by social processes of decay, causing good intentions to 
lead to negative social outcomes. Further, the previous chapter showed how the 
place of design in contemporary societies is not necessarily one from which power 
is challenged: A designerly worldview has increasingly reshaped statecraft, assisting 
power structures with new forms of cunning. 
If one accepts these dark realities, is it really advisable to be naïve about design? 
Indeed, some readers may surmise that the moral of this work runs counter to old 
Bucky: “Don’tb e n a ï v e !” Well, that is a good starting point, but it hardly suﬃces 
as a guideline for how to proceed as a designer. This chapter will thus search for a 
realist sensibility that does not err towards a wholesale cynicism – towards a critical 
position that undermines any attempts to do designerly work. How does one 
nurture skepticism and still carry on designing? 
As will be evident, this sensibility will inevitably have to engage with the 
problem of hypocrisy. Indeed, if one accepts the power relations inherent in 
DOI: 10.4324/9781003281443-6 

92 Hypocrisy 
design, one may be best served by accepting a disconnect between the actions of 
the professional designer, on the one hand, and the personal beliefs of the person 
behind the professional mask, on the other. Designers should not be worried 
about maintaining such a disconnect; they ought to worry about the risks of 
being too cavalier about the realities of designerly power. 
The chapter will ﬁrst examine the stakes of this problem, returning to the 
question of good intentions in design that was broached in Chapter 4. Such 
intentions can be detected in the shift from “design for” to “design with” citizens 
or users, in the emphasis on designerly empathy, and in the idea of the designer as 
changemaker. This sentiment is then traced back to the work of Victor Papanek, 
whose straddling of the Cold War technocracy and the counterculture serves as 
an entry point to the question of hypocrisy in design. The vice of hypocrisy is 
discussed in the context of the work of Judith Shklar and David Runciman, as 
well as through a reexamination of the famous case of Robert Moses’ design of 
the parkways leading to Jones Beach. As will be shown, this case has become 
paradigmatic due to its deep interest in the sincere intentions of the designer. The 
chapter then moves on to discuss whether – and if so which kind of – hypocrisy 
can be accepted, following the work of philosophers like Hobbes and Bentham. 
As hinted above, the chapter ends with various accounts of how to maintain a 
skeptical position that steers clear of stiﬂing cynicism and remains committed to 
making a productive diﬀerence in the world. 
“With, not for, citizens”–the stakes of designerly virtues 
In 2007, the Cooper Hewitt Design Museum in New York launched their 
famous exhibition “Design for the other 90%”. This marked a profound shift in 
the ethics of design and reignited discussions around design and international 
development. As the concept transformed to speciﬁcally examine cities, the 
exhibit changed names to “Design with the other 90%: Cities”– the “with” 
underlining the co-design and participatory elements of urban design. Being 
much discussed across the design ﬁeld, the exhibits were celebrated for high­
lighting the urgency and desperate need for design to tackle real-world situations. 
However, the exhibitions were also criticized for othering populations who have 
no direct access to the system most designers in the Global North take for gran­
ted. But as many noticed, the shift from designing for to designing with also put 
the spotlight onto another important aspect of design; who is really served by 
design, and who has a say? 
The shift in perspective made apparent at Cooper Hewitt corresponded to an 
overall shift across the cultural ﬁeld at the time, with relational aesthetics and a gen­
eral push for participation and co-design. The switching of prepositions – from “for” 
to “with”– also highlights a move that we touched upon in the previous chapter – a 
move towards empathy as the primary quality and operational methodology of 
design. 

Hypocrisy 93 
This story also elucidates another aspect of how designers seek to position 
themselves. Not only is empathy a key virtue, so is the ambition to act as an 
“agent of change”. As in Tim Brown’s (2009) bestselling book Change By Design, 
the world is just an incomplete and chaotic arrangement of interests eagerly 
waiting for a designer to come and reorder it in an innovative way. It is a per­
spective that acts under the proposition that as design changes things, it makes 
things better, and if it somehow does not live up to our expectations, then we 
can say that it is bad design. Bad design, bad outcomes, can always be ﬁxed by 
new iterations, new editions. 
This perspective also holds that change is good. Thus, the term does not 
include the kind of social corrosion and decay that Machiavelli was concerned 
about (see Chapter 4). Instead, design is construed as the key act of making 
change. From the perspective of designers, change means good design, and is 
never corrupt. Change has a sense of function, honesty, and transparency that the 
tensions and politics of human relations can never have. Design can be optimized, 
whereas the politics of relations is a form of friction per se. Here, then, is a desire 
to circumvent the dirtiness of politics – the kind of grubbiness that a realist tends 
to construe as the key fact of social life. 
Attentive readers may recognize the idea of design as something that cuts 
through the tangles of politics: Indeed, it resonates with the original claims of the 
technocratic movement of the 1920s and 1930s. As Howard Scott writes in his 
1933 Introduction to Technocracy: “In contrast to the devious ways of politics, […]w e 
have the methods of science and technology” (Scott 1933: 37). However, the 
contemporary design thinking discourse cannot be accused of such a crude account 
of politics. In fact, this discourse does have an account of design and social conﬂict. 
This is where the “for” and “with” comes into the picture. In the traditional 
modes of designing “for” people, the agency and interests of the users are not 
taking the front seat. The contemporary focus on designing “with” is a means to 
engage with the question of social relations. The problem of the social – and the 
political – is thus solved by a repositioning of the designer. The designer is no 
longer a helpful dictator or all-powerful social engineer – the designer should 
strive to be your friend. 
Nevertheless, this repositioning disguises something more tricky: Design is, after 
all, a service profession. The designer is paid to orient the user in alignment with 
the client’s intentions. The designer says the work is for the user, but the demands 
are set by the client. Designers want materials malleable by their own visions and 
actions, and the users are a part of this material. This holds particularly true for the 
design of services, which is a “design of people, rather than the design of things for 
people” (Penin & Tonkinwise 2009: 1–2). Thus, in a human-centered approach of 
design, change means aligning the needs and demands of the users to the intentions 
of the client. 
In design literature, this is often noted without much controversy, as the 
designer moves from being a disconnected engineer towards a socially engaged 

94 Hypocrisy 
and empathic collaborator, partner, and friend. For example, design theorist Brigitte 
Borja de Mozota posits: 
Design has an avant-garde spirit. While not generating major innovations, 
the designer anticipates new needs and creates new answers to meet con­
straints, while integrating progressive technologies. Design is a partner and 
initiator of change in society. Therefore, it is a partner in the management of 
change in organizations. 
(Borja de Mozota 2003: 38) 
The proximity to the user creates a complex situation. The designer “partners” 
with the user, even though he or she is paid by a client to manipulate the user. 
Borja de Mozota uses the example of famous industrial designer Philippe Starck 
to underline her point about “change in society”, and Starck desires to make 
design more about change than mere aesthetics. 
The urgent thing today is not to create a car or a chair that is more beautiful 
than another. The ‘beautiful’ is a concept that is obsolete for the time being 
and does not respond to the urgency facing society today, where barbarity 
has reared its head again. What we need to do today is to replace aesthetic 
objects with semantic objects, which results in replacing the beautiful with 
the good. We must start again from scratch so that these objects and 
machines serve us, so that the objects is good for us, in order to live better. 
(Starck, quoted in Borja de Mozota 2003: 38) 
If designers acknowledge any political problems in design, it is usually found in 
the methods, not in the very ontology of design itself. For example, the classic 
paradigm of design-based problem-solving may have an elitist or engineer-like 
touch. Such connotations can be shunted by a more open approach where design 
is more evenly distributed around the planet through social design or open design 
and where “best design is replaced by just design” (Laitio 2011). Taken together, 
these perspectives suggest that both design methods and design practitioners are 
per deﬁnition good: Design rids the world from frictions, conﬂicts, and injustices, 
as well as problems. Thus, designers are the good ones, they are the heroes of 
functionality, progress, justice, and peace. 
At this point in the argument, the reader will recognize that this idea of virtue sits 
uneasily with previous points made in this book. As we saw in Chapter 3, co-design 
processes are political processes, which invariably means that some interests are likely 
to be betrayed. Again, from a realist perspective, politics cannot be circumvented. As 
we saw in Chapter 4, the social world tends towards decay, which means that design, 
from a realist perspective, should be less about disruption, and more about “mante­
nere lo stato”. In the previous chapter, we saw how one can scarcely say that design 
is always on the side of the user, or the citizen for that matter. Therefore, it is worth 

Hypocrisy 95 
searching for another positioning of the designer, or – alternatively put – for another 
designerly sensibility. 
The issue of reexamining dominant virtues has already been broached in 
Chapter 4. Again, Machiavelli’s contribution to political theory amounted to a 
revision of the cardinal virtues that his contemporaries believed should be the 
foundation of good governance. As a replacement of such virtues, he proposed 
his own conception of princely virtú. Before moving on to examine what some 
kind of designerly virtú may mean today, let us examine the origins of today’s 
designerly virtues. Indeed, the notion of the designer as a good changemaker – 
the designer as a part of an avant-garde – is hardly novel. One key contribution 
that constitutes an obligatory point of passage for such an examination is Victor 
Papanek’s Design for the Real World. 
Idealism for the real world? 
Papanek’s 1971 classic is widely recognized as the founding text of the notion of 
social design, and remains hugely inﬂuential. It is still read by design students, and 
as suggested above, the imperative of the designer as activist and changemaker 
constitutes the dominant mode of designerly engagement with the socio-political 
realm. That being said, one can equally argue that today’s operationalization of 
this imperative constitutes a pale imitation of the real thing. 
Indeed, the notion of “the real” is crucial when assessing Papanek’sw o r k t o d a y .I t 
is reasonably clear what kind of real that Design for the Real World is addressing. On 
the one hand, design-as-we-know-it is fundamental in creating the dream worlds 
and illusory needs of the consumer society. On the other hand, this very potency of 
design allows it to be reoriented so as to change the real world – the one existing 
outside the consumerist bubble that tends to be the object of industrial design. 
Although these twin proportions are clearly forged in the context of a broader 
sociological critique of the consumer society, they remain broadly correct. 
In political theory terms, the argument presented back in 1971 can be characterized 
as part realist and part idealist. The book is realist in its attempt to present a hard-nosed 
view on the constraints on design practices, placing them squarely in the context of 
socio-political struggles. Nevertheless, it is also idealist, partly due to the ways in 
which the “how it is” (the ﬁrst part of the book) is connected to the “how it ought to 
be” (the second part of the book). Here, some observers would suggest that the 
second part is too idealistic – it is too unrealistically detached from the “how it is”. 
Moreover, there is a moralist slant to Papanek’s project: It may be understood as 
countering wasteful and consumerist industrial design with good activist virtues. 
Furthermore, as we have seen, it seems the mainstream of design has been able to 
integrate virtues such as empathy without actually severing the ties to consumerism. 
Indeed, Papanek’s work may be the best design-based example of how the coun­
tercultural ideas of the late sixties and early seventies became enshrined in “the new 
spirit of capitalism” (Boltanski & Chiapello 2005). 

96 Hypocrisy 
In her recent biography, Alison Clarke (2021) seeks to historicize Papanek’s 
work by situating it in the intersection between the Cold War technocracy and 
the counterculture. Thus, she presents a story of how an Austrian emigré forged a 
designer career in postwar United States, becoming deeply “immersed in a design 
culture geared exclusively to the immediate postwar and then Cold War eﬀort” 
(158). The academic institutions he worked for “sat at the hub of Cold War 
funding and experimentation” (152), and the applications that he designed for the 
“real” world would be equally useful in military environments. In other words, 
the military leveraged the reimagining of design that Papanek promoted. Further, 
one may wonder if it would have emerged without the Cold War funding and 
experimentation. 
Here, just to reiterate the point about designerly virtues have been mobilized 
in society today, one may ﬁrst note how the relation between design and geo­
politics has changed. Papanek’s design for the real world could ultimately be used 
in war, potentially being deployed in ﬁeld operations in diﬃcult milieus, as 
socially engaged means to survey and aﬀect the population. To some extent, one 
may argue that this idea was rebooted after the turn of the millennium, when the 
US-led invasions of Afghanistan (2001) and Iraq (2003) prompted military leaders 
to embrace design thinking during counterinsurgency warfare in human-terrain 
teams and using design interventions to gain soft-power inﬂuence. Today, how­
ever, it seems that a more strategic design-infused thought has entered the minds 
of military thinkers. Notions of co-creation and co-design have emerged as tools 
with which to navigate the web of treacherous socio-technical interrelations that 
shape today’s world: Think about the debates regarding whether social media 
platforms may become used in psychological warfare, and about the disputes over 
national control of telecom infrastructure. Today, it seems the only way to 
achieve sovereignty is to control the design of the very infrastructures, platforms, 
and everyday technologies in which “gray zone warfare” (the space between 
peace and outright military conﬂict) is waged. From a realist perspective, one may 
point out that the strategists who adopt this kind of co-creation do it in the 
context of a zero-sum game: It is a means to place one actor in a favorable 
situation, while placing other actors in a less favorable one. Co-creation is 
deployed as a tool that includes some actors in order to exclude others (von Busch 
& Palmås 2021). Again, this shows how virtues of participation and all things “co­
” have been mobilized (and weaponized) in contemporary society. 
Clarke’s portrayal of Papanek’s elegant alternation between the world of Cold 
War technocracy, on the one hand, and the counterculture, on the other, gravitates 
towards the question of hypocrisy. Should it not have been plain to see – say, for 
the Scandinavian countercultural students that he taught when writing Design for the 
Real World – that his loyalties were split, that he straddled two opposed worlds? 
Was it ever surmised that the countercultural language served as a veil, disguising 
who Papanek really was? In short, the question of hypocrisy is a key problematic 
that emerges from Clarke’s story. Indeed, the vice of hypocrisy sits uneasily with 

Hypocrisy 97 
the virtues extolled within the current discourse on design. In order to make sense 
out of this mismatch, the next section will turn to a classic argument about the 
vices and virtues – that of Judith Shklar. 
The ordinary vices of design 
Some of the arguments so far may suggest that a realist account of design may 
require a wholesale reversal of designerly virtues, much like the one Machiavelli 
proposed for statecraft. Still, in searching for a position that is neither naïve nor 
cynical, one may instead turn to Judith Shklar’s account of quotidian vices. In her 
1984 classic Ordinary Vices, she reverses the terms of reference for discussing 
political conduct. Rather than discussing and ranking the relative goodness of 
certain virtues – which has been (and remains) dominant since the work of 
Plato – one may lower our expectations from political life, and instead focus on 
ranking the relative badness of vices. Which vices are acceptable, and which are 
the ones to look out for as the really fatal ones? While Ordinary Vices is a medi­
tation on politics – speciﬁcally liberal politics – it is worth transposing some of her 
ideas onto design. In the context of this book, it serves the purpose of engaging 
with sensibilities and vices that are both public and private, yet nevertheless apply 
to the professional role of the designer. 
Shklar’s argument is sometimes condensed into the slogan – cruelty should 
be put ﬁrst; it is the one vice to be avoided. Indeed, this captures the main 
political message of the argument, but there is more to her story. When read 
in the context of design, there are a number of lessons to be learned from 
Shklar’s nuanced and sometimes ambivalent meditation on vice. The ﬁrst one 
has already been touched upon: Conduct should not be evaluated on the basis 
of which good intentions to pursue, but on the basis of which vices to avoid. 
While cruelty is “put ﬁrst”– both in terms of chapter structure, and terms of 
the hierarchy of objectionable vices – the other vices discussed are of no less 
interest to the designer. The vices covered after cruelty are, in turn: Hypoc­
r i s y ,s n o b b e r y ,b e t r a y a l , a n dm i s a n t h r o p y .T h e ﬁrst and last of these – 
hypocrisy and misanthropy – are of particular interest to the designer, and 
serve as reference points for this chapter. As regards the other two vices, suf­
ﬁce to say that snobbery is unbecoming (but irrelevant to our argument), and 
betrayal is sometimes necessary (though Shklar discusses it in a broader 
manner than Chapter 3 in this book). 
Given the challenge outlined above – how to keep on doing design, if one 
recognizes that it is a sordid business? – the vice of hypocrisy will be of particular 
interest to this argument. Citing the Oxford English Dictionary, Shklar deﬁnes 
hypocrisy as “assuming a false appearance of virtue or goodness, with dissimulation of 
real character or inclination” (Shklar 1984: 47). Thus, she continues, the hypocrite is 
someone who “pretends his motives and intentions and character is irreproachable 
when he knows that they are blameworthy”. Along with hypocrisy, there is “a 

98 Hypocrisy 
cluster of attitudes” that are generally referred to as insincerity and inauthenticity, 
which – even if they do not cause harm to others –“ are said to deform one’s 
personality”. 
Shklar notes that the term originates from the act of playing a role on a stage. 
In Political Hypocrisy, David Runciman (2008) points out that the “original 
‘hypocrites’ were classical stage actors, and the Greek term (hypokrisis) meant the 
playing of a part” (7). Over time, however, this mere descriptor became a 
pejorative term, applied to people who are unreliable, based on a suspicion that 
they are wearing a mask – on the suspicion that there may be another, “real face” 
behind the mask. For the purposes of this argument, this original meaning of 
hypocrisy is key: When it comes to doing the sordid business of design, the key 
issue is that of a duplicity that emerges from the concealment of one’s personal 
beliefs. Thus, the common contemporary usage of the term –“ the attempt to 
separate oﬀ one’s personal behavior from the standards that hold for everyone 
else” (8) – is of less interest to us here. 
So, the disdain for this original sense of hypocrisy has a long history, which 
starts in the classical age. Today, Shklar posits, it “remains the only unforgi­
vable sin”: Though “evil can be understood after due analysis”,h y p o c r i s y i s 
inexcusable, and has always been so. “Every age, every form of literature, and 
every public stage has held him up for contempt and ridicule […]h ew a s 
never anything less than utterly despised” (45). Why is this the case? Runci­
man provides two suggestions for why this is the case. First, “on a basic 
human level, there is something repulsive about hypocrisy encountered at ﬁrst 
hand, since no one enjoys being played for a fool” (Runciman 2008: 2). 
S e c o n d ,i ti ss i m p l y v e r yc o m m o n . H y p o c r i s y i se v e r y w h e r e , a n di tb r e a k s 
a p a r tt h e p a t t e r n so fb e h a v i o rh u m a n ss od e a r l yw a n tt op r o j e c t o n t o r e a l i t y 
to make it predictable. 
Still, is the wearing of masks and the concealment of personal beliefs an issue 
in design? Granted, this chapter has already suggested that there is a connection 
here: A designer who recognizes the sordid nature of design, and still goes on 
doing it must be a hypocrite. After all, that designer is stuck in a mismatch 
between oﬃcial motives and intentions, on the one hand, and a personal 
recognition of their shortcomings, on the other. Moreover, the embrace of 
virtues in design – such the imperatives of empathy, and the shift from “for” to 
“with”– raise the stakes of this problem. The cherishing of virtues invariably 
leads to calls for sincerity and authenticity of beliefs. In other words, scholars as 
well as practitioners should resist the temptation to pay undue attention to 
virtue, while failing to address the issue of vice. Again, another modiﬁcation of 
Papanek’st r i a n g l ei su s e f u lh e r e . 
However, much as there is a long history of disdain for hypocrisy in social life, 
the question of sincerity in design is in no way a novel phenomenon. As a case in 
point, the next section will revisit a classic discussion in design and Science and 
Technology Studies (STS) – that of the urban planning of Robert Moses. 

Hypocrisy 99 
Virtue
