FIGURE 6.1 The virtue or vice problem. 
Moses, the misanthrope 
M o s e si sad i v i s i v e ﬁgure. For many, the great planner of New York City is 
the great bogeyman of urban development. For others, he is a deeply mis­
represented person. Moreover, the pro-Moses camp suggests, it seems, the 
new urbanist ideals of Jane Jacobs – often portrayed as Moses’ archnemesis – 
have paved the way for urban commercialization and violent modes of 
socioeconomic exclusion and gentriﬁcation. However, as we shall see, the 
story of Moses is also instructive in relation to the themes outlined in the 
previous section: How do we make sense of potential mismatches between 
the professional conduct and the personal convictions of designers? Moreover, 
how do we assess the work a designer who was – by many accounts – the 
opposite of an empathetic person? 
The story – as taught in countless courses on design, STS, and the philosophy 
of technology – starts from Robert Caro’s 1974 biography of Moses. In The 
Power Broker, Caro describes Moses as a master planner, but also as a bigot, bully, 
misanthrope, and racist. At one point, Caro reports from a conversation with a 
fellow planner and associate of Moses, who suggests that the low bridges over the 
parkways that lead to Jones Beach on Long Island were made so low that “buses 
would never be able to use his goddamned parkways” (Caro 1974: 952). Citing 
this passage, philosopher of technology Langdon Winner (1980) uses this story to 
make a famous argument about design, technology, and politics. In the much-
cited text that has come to deﬁne the politics of design, “Do artifacts have poli­
tics?”, Winner suggests that the low overpasses were a reﬂection and a coercive 

100 Hypocrisy 
solidiﬁcation of the racist views held by Moses. Indeed, if the bridges were too 
low, it meant that the Jones Beach development could not be reached by public 
transport, thus excluding racial minorities and the poor from the beach. Thus, 
the two hundred or so low-hanging overpasses on Long Island were deliberately 
designed to achieve a particular social eﬀect. Robert Moses, the master builder 
of roads, parks, bridges, and other public works from the 1920s to the 1970s in 
New York, had these overpasses built to speciﬁcations that would discourage 
the presence of buses on his parkways. 
(Winner 1980: 123) 
Winner’s objective is to use the Moses story to argue that artifacts indeed have poli­
tics. He thus writes against the prevalent view that technologies are “neutral tools 
that can be used well or poorly, for good, evil, or something in between” (125). He 
thus wants us to train our eyes to see whether artifacts are “designed and built in such 
a way that it produces a set of consequences logically and temporally prior to any of 
its professed uses”. In other words, Winner suggests that the personal convictions of 
designers matter. At the very least, when analyzing artifacts, we should be mindful of 
the agendas of the person that designed the artifact. 
Moses’ use of power may have made him an unpleasant person. As Caro 
suggests, Moses was most likely a person with wrongheaded ideas as well as 
ideals. It is easy to read politics into the person. However, there is a plot twist to 
this story. Scholars have pointed out that the story of the purposefully lowered 
bridges is simply not true: The low elevation of the overpasses were simply a 
matter of complying to planning regulations to exclude commercial traﬃcf r o m 
the parkways, and they argue the bridges could not have been made higher 
even if Moses had wanted to. Moreover, there were other transport routes 
through which buses could reach Jones Beach. There are even photographs of 
the development, clearly showing parking lots for buses. Some twenty years 
after Winner’s ﬁrst article, Bernwald Joerges excoriates Winner’s account. 
Writing in the STS journal Social Studies of Science, he surgically outlines its 
factual inaccuracies, and deﬂa t e s t h et w i nc l a i m st h a ti tr e l i e so n : F i r s t , t h a t t h e 
transport system was designed in a manner that excluded minorities and low-
income communities; second, that this was the outcome of Moses’ racist 
intentions (Joerges 1999: 8). Again, the planning protocols of the parkway reg­
ulations may have been excluding, but the causal reading between the suggested 
political intentions of Moses and the low bridges is misleading. 
Thus, Joerges’ object of critique is Winner’s “the design-version of 
‘artifacts-have-politics ’”, in which a designer “wills a speciﬁc social state, and 
then suitably transfers this intention into an artifact”, thus forming “an 
enduring social relationship [that is] built into the artifact” (Joerges 1999: 2). How­
ever, Joerges’ objective is not only to nuance the “strong” or “design” version of 
techno-determinism. Having discredited Winner’s story, Joerges goes on to ask why 

Hypocrisy 101 
the story has become so successful. What makes it so compelling? First, there is one 
straightforward explanation: Irrespective of context, racism is deeply revolting. 
Joerges, on his end, provides a further explanation, focusing on the rhetorical 
merits of Winner’s account: 
Winner’s Moses example is so winning because in itself it is a particularly 
well constructed artifact, capable of serving a great number of rhetorical 
purposes. The form of the parable is so seductive because it lends itself to 
several things: it leaves room for multiple interpretations, yet it preserves 
concrete, ostensibly historical reference; and it oﬀers in a nutshell a far-
reaching, causally formulated theory of technology well in tune with healthy 
common-sense. […] Enormous interpretive ﬂexibility, unambiguous 
empirical reference, elegant theoretical formula. And all that coupled with an 
urgent political-moral message. 
(14–15) 
Thus, Joerges concludes, “Winner’s story is so masterful because it combines all 
major rhetorical tropes: metaphor, metonymy, synecdoche – except for irony”.I n 
other words, it may be the case that artifacts have politics, but it is equally the case 
that politics have artifacts – such as the well-designed parable put together by 
Winner. 
Joerges inherits the interest in the rhetorical merits of Winner’s story from STS 
scholar Steve Woolgar, who had previously critiqued Winner’s techno-determinist 
story. Technological determinism has long been debated within STS – indeed, one 
may argue that the ﬁeld emerged as a reaction to techno-determinist accounts that 
under-emphasize the extent to which socio-cultural factors inﬂuence socio-techni­
cal outcomes. Much in line with these ambitions, Woolgar argues that stories such 
as that of Winner are simplifying, purporting to give “deﬁnitive accounts of the 
outcome of the technology”,w h e n i n f a c t “the outcome or impact of technologies 
is contentious”, and may be inﬂuenced by a plethora of social factors (Woolgar, 
1991: 34). 
In other words, the STS debate on Moses has tended to focus on the techno­
determinism of Winner – again, the claim that the transport system was designed 
in a manner that excluded minorities and low-income communities. Indeed, the 
debate is ultimately about the extent to which design professions determine the 
shape of society through the artifacts that they bring into the world. Is the use of 
technology a matter of control – emerging from the intentions of the designer – or 
a matter of contingency (in which a great number of unchartable factors co-create 
societal outcomes). Winner is on the “control side” of the argument, Woolgar on 
the opposite “contingency side”, and Joerges seeks to position himself somewhere 
in between the two. Nevertheless, as we have seen, the debate also engages with 
Winner’s second claim – that this was the outcome of Moses’ racist intentions. 
Even if it can be proved that there were no segregatory eﬀects – did Moses in fact 

102 Hypocrisy 
try to segregate? Was he a racist who simply failed in his attempts to exclude? 
Thus, lurking in the background of this debate, there is a lingering doubt 
regarding Moses, his character, and his personal convictions. 
In a response to Joerges, Woolgar and Cooper expand on the question of the 
rhetorical merits of Winner’s story. The key success factors relate to the design 
in question, but also the character of Moses. With regards to the former, there is 
the fact that a bridge is a simple device: Technological complexities do not 
obscure the main message of the story (Woolgar & Cooper 1999: 439). The 
parkway overpass also serves as an iconic visual motif that represents more 
abstract problematics, and eﬀectively connects physical materiality with the 
supposed intentions of the designer. This latter aspect – the supposed intentions 
of Moses – are equally important factors in making the story a success. After all, 
Winner’sa c c o u n ti sa l i g n e d w i t h “the 20th-century obsession with individua­
tion”– that is, “t h ei d e at h a t o n e m a ni sc a p a b l e o f g r e a td e e d s( f o rg o o do r 
ill)”. Heroes and villains are key to any good story. This Manichaean narrative is 
strengthened by juxtaposition between appearance and reality; the stark “con­
trast between the cute, quaint, deliberately rustic quality of the bridges in a 
green belt development, and the arrogant self-serving motives of a heinous 
power-monger (Moses)”. This, in turn, fuels our paranoia about the ugly faces 
t h a t m a yl u r kb e h i n dt h e m a s k so fp o w e r . 
Thus, Woolgar and Cooper question the overemphasis on the psychology of 
Moses. This critique also applies to Joerges: For all the painstaking concern with 
the truth of the matter, Joerges seems to accept the premise that at the heart of 
the matter is the personal convictions and intentions of Moses. 
Winner is in no doubt about the motives of the designer of the bridges; 
Joerges contests Winner’s account of Moses’ motives, but his analysis 
remains, in important senses, at the level of intention. 
(Woolgar & Cooper 1999: 443) 
This focus on the intentions of Moses is reﬂected even outside of the debate 
that unfolded after Langdon Winner’s intervention. Since the publication of 
Caro’s biography, Moses has repeatedly been recognized as a misanthrope. In an 
oft-quoted passage, Caro reports that Moses’ coworker Francis Perkins once said: 
“He doesn’t love the people […] He loves the public, but not as people” (Caro 
1974: 318). Again, this speaks to the paranoia that Woolgar and Cooper point 
towards. Why is it so disturbing for us to suspect that a designer is a misanthrope? 
Or perhaps worse – a misanthrope who simply pretends to like people? The next 
section will explore this question by returning to Shklar’s argument regarding 
ordinary vices. Was Moses a hypocrite? Was he ostensibly creating beaches for the 
public while actually excluding people? Or how shall we understand our search 
for sincere designers doing good for all? 

Hypocrisy 103 
Sincerity and professionalism 
In Winner’s account of Moses, we are dealing with a designer that we suspect has 
a serious character ﬂaw. Even if leave aside the outcomes of the designer’s actions – 
if we accept that the infrastructure was not discriminatory – we may doubt the 
sincerity of the designer’s intentions. In the case of a “power broker” like Moses, 
the stakes are just too high for us not to worry; Woolgar and Cooper’s paranoia 
is, to some extent, warranted. Still, if we leave the speciﬁc case of Moses, there is 
still the more general issue of designerly hypocrisy. If we think that there may be 
a disparity between public mask and the personal convictions of a designer, we 
are overcome by the same anger that has plagued anti-hypocrites since the clas­
sical era. Moreover, what is the place of a designer who personally struggles with 
sincere feelings of empathy? 
As we know, Shklar positions both hypocrisy and misanthropy as a lower-ranked 
vices – vices that are no way close to the wickedness of cruelty. So what does she 
actually think of these two vices? We will study misanthropy in the next section; 
let us ﬁrst focus on hypocrisy. Shklar suggests that we should try to contain our 
“natural” sense of revulsion against hypocrisy. Accordingly, she posits that sincerity 
is overrated. She is, of course, discussing it in the context of political life, but there 
are – as we shall see – obvious parallels to the professional role of the designer. 
Shklar’s downplaying of hypocrisy stems from a concern that an over­
emphasis on rooting out hypocrisy may poison politics: It may reduce it to a 
game in which opponents seek to expose each other’s supposed character 
ﬂaws. “Antihypocrisy is a splendid weapon of psychic warfare, but not a 
principle of government” (Shklar 1984: 69). However, she also points to a 
deeper point about the perils of relying on sincerity as a marker of responsible 
use of power. For sure, good intentions among the people in charge is a 
virtue, but good intentions can also “be used to purify any sort of conduct” 
(65); they can “liberate adherents from the responsibilities and constraints of 
the present ethical and legal order” (66). 
This charge does not only apply to rulers of the past, who justify their actions 
by referring to a sincere adherence to certain religious beliefs. Runciman points 
to Tony Blair as the modern-day representative of such “politics of good inten­
tions”, in particular as regards his conduct in relation to accepting responsibility 
for the invasion of Iraq: 
One of the things that have united the critics of the war in Iraq, whether 
from the right or the left, is that they are sick of the sound of Tony Blair 
trumpeting the purity of his purpose, when what matters is the consequences 
of his actions. There he stands, however, somewhere between the left and 
the right, trumpeting away. 
(Runciman 2006: 33) 

104 Hypocrisy 
In his reasoning around this style of politics, he ﬁnds historical analogies in Max 
Weber’s classical text on professional politics; “Politik als Beruf”. In reviewing 
Weber’s account of early twentieth century European politics, Runciman points 
out that the great German sociologist “warned us against politicians whose saintly 
intentions were taken to sanctify the unforeseen results of their naïvité” (47). 
Nevertheless, a hundred years on, the politics of good intentions have morphed 
into something more self-aware. Here, Blair is the great innovator, since he “has 
been able to talk about his good intentions by making a parade of his awareness 
of their limitations” (51). Of course, the knowing character of his approach – as if 
to say that “I follow my beliefs, which I know to be imperfect, but that is all I 
can do”– prevents him from accepting responsibility. 
Runciman also points to how the same politics of good intentions have been 
applied in other – though admittedly less fatal – Blair projects of public designs 
almost at the scale of Moses. In late 2000, it was clear that the newly constructed 
Millennium Dome in east London was a failure. In the context of the backlash 
against it – in the words of Runciman, it was “a monstrous white elephant that 
no amount of bows and ribbons could disguise”– Blair nevertheless set out to 
defend his decision to go ahead with the project. In the Labour conference of 
that year, he stated that even though the Dome did not achieve what it meant to 
achieve, he would not apologize for trying. Again, a sincerity of ambition is put 
forward as the paramount determinant of a good decision – not the outcomes of 
that decision. Bad outcomes pale in signiﬁcance when placed in the context of 
sincere beliefs and an audacity in personal purpose. 
Examples like these also show the perils of failed political processes and bad 
outcomes. Even honest failures are political as well as designerly ﬁascos. Unfortu­
nately, one of the ﬁelds of design where this is most common is in the context of 
development work, where designers keep reproducing disasters through well-
meaning designs, echoing betrayal, enhancing corruption and cunning post-colonial 
relations of dependency in the name of progress. The design literature is full of 
well-meaning energy systems, playground pumps, water carriers, vehicles and crafts 
that boost project fatigue and end up in landﬁlls, even though the designers had the 
purest intentions but failed execution of their agency and power. 
Again, Runciman’s account concerns a particular style of politics that seems to 
have become prevalent since the turn of the millennium. Still, the same logic – 
and the perils of it – can be transposed onto the discourse on design. Again, note 
the emphasis placed on values and empathy in design, and note how the debate 
on Moses has tended to place the designer’s intentions at the heart of the 
discussion. 
Is there, then, another model of politician that can be put forward as an alter­
native to that of Blair? In her account of hypocrisy in politics, Shklar puts forward 
the example of Benjamin Franklin. In reading his Autobiography, she is struck by 
its complete lack of sentimentality: “Franklin had a pronounced character which 
he presented very acutely, but he did not think of himself as primarily a unique 

Hypocrisy 105 
inner self” (Shklar 1984: 73). Thus, in contrast to romantic notions of an ego that 
is more than what one does, he saw himself as nothing more and nothing less 
than “the sum of his actions” (74). 
Franklin is, Shklar concedes, a role model that is diﬃcult to emulate: He was a 
brilliant mind and an overachiever in terms of career advancements. Still, the very 
breadth of his professional endeavors – encompassing politics, science, philosophy, 
writing – underscores the phenomenal malleability of his social persona. In contrast 
to Blair, he always sought to distance this persona from his private person. Emer­
ging from a political context where “self-improvement and practical philanthropy 
were joined”, Franklin recognized that political labor in such matters “did not 
require zeal, only guile and persistence” ( 7 2 ) .I n s h o r t ,h e w a s “a shrewd calculator 
who took it for granted that the politics of persuasion required hypocrisy”. 
As such, Shklar suggests, Franklin knew exactly what democratic politics 
required, and his own place in it. Still, she does not promote hypocrisy as a 
virtue. Rather, it represents a problem – one which needs to be addressed in a 
manner that fully recognizes its ambiguities, and does not lapse into cynicism. 
The next section will explore the proposition that in the end – whether one 
discusses professional politicians or professional designers – it all comes down to 
distinguishing between diﬀerent types of hypocrisy. 
How to be a better kind of hypocrite 
In their respective discussions on hypocrisy, Shklar and Runciman demarcate 
their projects from those of Machiavelli- or Nietzsche-readers, who merely 
attempt to demask liberal democracy. Shklar, on her end, warns of the cynics 
who – as “disappointed idealists” in the search for purity – have been driven out 
of politics, since they are “unable to bear the pseudo-sincerity of all visible poli­
ticians” (85). Similarly, Runciman’s book on hypocrisy consciously sidesteps 
Machiavelli and Nietzsche, even though they have much to say about the topic. 
Instead, he is more interested in pragmatic approaches to hypocrisy, written from 
inside the liberal tradition. These accounts go further than simply point out that 
there is an underlying reality that is uglier than the shiny surface. The problem – 
what he refers to as “the ‘Shklar’ problem”– is how to “tread carefully” and 
“avoid lumping all hypocrisies together”, thus deciding “which are the ones that 
are worth worrying about” (Runciman 2008: 41). 
The best starting point, Runciman suggests, is the work of Hobbes. As noted 
above, the concept of hypocrisy has connotations to theater, and as such, it 
resonates well with Hobbes’ conception of politics. Indeed, in describing his 
conception of a sovereign – the one that, after the Westphalian peace, ended up 
serving as a model of the modern nation state – Hobbes did not invent a new 
technical term. Rather, Hobbes borrowed one from mythology. The Leviathan is 
a monster, but also an artiﬁcial being, a composite of ordinary human beings – 
which in turn are protected by this monstrous and artiﬁcial creature. In describing 

106 Hypocrisy 
how this arrangement is made operational, Hobbes refers to the importance of 
representation and the performing of roles. Thus, Hobbes’ Leviathan includes a 
chapter on “persons, authors, and things personated”, in which political 
representation ultimately implies wearing a mask: 
Persona in latine signiﬁes the disguise, or outward appearance of a man, 
counterfeited on the Stage, and sometimes more particularly that part of it, 
which disguiseth the face, as a Mask or Vizard. 
(Hobbes, 1651, cited in Runciman 2008: 38) 
The sovereign representative, then, is ultimately a person wearing a mask – a 
mask of power. In so doing, the representative is engaging in the artiﬁcial per­
formance of a theatrical role, which is necessary for the operation of the artiﬁcial 
being that we call the Leviathan. As such, dissimulation is a necessary part of the 
power arrangement, and there must necessarily be a distance between the perso­
nal being and the external representation of the professional persona. 
Nevertheless, there was one type of deception that Hobbes warned against – 
the one where people in power do not recognize the terms of power. Thus, he 
wanted such people to accept their proper place in the power hierarchy, and play 
their roles as power-holders honestly. Hypocrisy – the separation of the beliefs of 
the private person, and the actions of the public persona – may be inevitable, 
since politics is a wholly artiﬁcial performance. However, when those in power 
are hypocritical about this fact, the system of power starts to corrode. In other 
words, we should not worry about hypocrisy, but rather about hypocrisy about the 
need for hypocrisy. Runciman thus suggests that 
we need politicians who are sincere, but that does not mean that we should 
wish them to be sincere believers in everything they do. Instead, we need 
them to be sincere about the system in which they ﬁnd themselves, and 
sincere in their desire to maintain the stability and durability of that system, 
even if it comes at the cost of their own ability to say what they mean and 
mean what they say. 
(Runciman 2008: 213) 
Again, Runciman’s and Hobbes’ warnings against this hypocrisy about hypocrisy 
are clearly founded in politics. This goes in particular for the concern for the 
maintenance durability of a political system of representation deeply reliant on 
mask-wearing. The design profession does not have to bear such considerations in 
mind. Still, “second-order hypocrisy” does constitute a problem in design. We do 
need designers who are sincere about the system of power in which they may 
ﬁnd themselves. This entails recognizing the problems of good intentions out­
lined in Chapter 4: Social means does not necessarily produce social ends. Just as 
in the case of the politics of good intentions epitomized by Tony Blair, references 

Hypocrisy 107 
to sincerely held convictions, personal values or grand ambitions are not valid 
excuses for bad outcomes. 
Being sincere about power structures also entails recognizing some of the pro­
blems raised in Chapter 5: Even though empathy and participation are worthy 
foundations to start from, such virtues should not distract our view from the fact 
that design does not necessarily empower the disempowered. The point here is 
not that designers should only engage in truly radical work; to engage in the art 
of the weak. Nor is it to state that it is wrong for designers to engage in the 
emerging modes of designerly statecraft. The point is that designers should be 
sincere about the roles that they play, and the masks that they wear. Their good 
intentions will not save them from betrayal or corruption, or acting like 
hypocrites to some degree. 
So, to summarize, Hobbes’ work gives us some hints to solve the “Shklar 
problem”: Which types of hypocrisy are to be avoided, and which ones should be 
quietly accepted, but also taken into account – ugly as they may be. Still, there is the 
other problem that was raised in the introduction to this chapter: How does one 
balance the recognition of the realities of power without succumbing to wholesale 
cynicism? In concluding this chapter, we will address that question, while also 
picking up a loose end: The question of the vice of misanthropy, as raised by Shklar. 
Dare to be naïve – redux 
When introducing Ordinary Vices, we suggested that among the vices covered by 
Shklar, hypocrisy and misanthropy serve as reference points for this chapter. 
Hypocrisy has been discussed at length, but what about misanthropy? This vice is the 
last one that Shklar discusses, and her treatment of it is – as the reader may expect at 
this point – one that emphasizes its ambiguity. On the one hand, she places herself 
squarely on the pessimist side on the debate on human nature. Humans are, sadly, 
ﬂawed creatures: “Most moral psychology of any worth is a scream of disgust, as is 
much of what philosophers since Plato have told us about ourselves” (Shklar 1984: 
193). Thus, those intellectuals who express a “loathing of humanity” do, in fact, 
provide us with essential knowledge: They teach us how to be suspicious towards 
people in power, since they may be capable of cruel conduct. However, when we 
engage in misanthropy ourselves, we risk becoming “intolerant, cruel, and, at the 
very least, socially impossible”. Therefore, 
misanthropy is politically a paradox. Disdain and fear could and did serve as 
the basis of political decency, legal restraint, and the eﬀort to create limited 
government that would attenuate the eﬀects of the cruelty of those who 
rule. Misanthropy can, however, also initiate slaughter. 
There are further grounds for avoiding misanthropy. For one, misanthropy may 
be expressed in the unproductive kind of anti-hypocrisy that Shklar warns against. 

108 Hypocrisy 
Further, misanthropy must be moderated, so as not to produce aimlessness. She 
refers back to Montaigne’s objections to Machiavelli. As we saw in Chapter 5, 
Machiavelli suggests that virtú must replace traditional virtues, so that the prince 
can complete great causes and attain glory. However, if excessive misanthropy 
prevails, how can there be a collective understanding of what great causes and 
glory entails? “Montaigne was right. Machiavelli’s disdain is, in the end, self-
defeating” (213). 
In other words, misanthropy must be moderated. We must learn from 
Machiavelli, yet avoid becoming all-out “Machiavellians”. Rather than fostering a 
“disappointed idealist” sensibility that leads us away from the grubby business of 
doing either politics or design, we must ﬁnd a nuanced and pragmatic position. 
This is not just a matter of retrieving some element of hope that something good 
comes out of our labors; this is not merely about fooling ourselves that politics or 
design can produce meaningful change in the world. It is also the case that too 
bleak a vision of these activities can simply give a false impression of the very 
workings of either politics or design. 
Here, one may learn from another philosopher that Runciman discusses in his 
book on hypocrisy: Jeremy Bentham. One of the things that Western philosophy 
has inherited from Plato was the idea that democracy may look nice, but it is 
much worse than it looks. It may be wrapped in a language of ideals and virtue, 
but that veneer merely hides the ugly truth: Under the shiny surface, there is only 
lies and deceit. Runciman suggests that Bentham was wary of how “high-ﬂown 
language of democratic principle can be a mask for the power relations that lie 
behind it” (Runciman 2008: 206), but equally suggested that such a view is too 
simplistic. This is because it, in turn, conceals “the extent to which democracy 
relies on a set of ﬁctions about its own essential character”. So, while politicians 
may overindulge in “highfalutin cant” about democratic ideals, it is also the case 
that critical analysts may overindulge in “technical cant” that reduces such ideals 
to mere ﬁction. Bentham’s contribution – at least as Runciman sees it – is to 
point out that democracy cannot operate without such ﬁctions. In a subsequent 
book, Runciman (2013) makes a similar argument about democracy: It rests on 
the fact that citizens are conﬁdent that democracy actually delivers – that their 
participation is worthwhile, that they have a voice, that democracy produces 
good results. This conﬁdence may, at times, rest on a ﬁction, but that ﬁction is 
nevertheless fundamental to the very functioning of democracy. 
In the end, a similar understanding may underpin a realist understanding of 
design. For sure, the notion of design as “devising courses of action aimed at 
changing existing situations into preferred ones” (Simon 1996: 111), or “the 
planning and patterning of any act toward a desired, foreseeable end” (Papanek 
1971: 3), can be interpreted in overly idealist or moralist manners. In so doing, 
the design process may be understood in all too voluntaristic terms. Still, a belief 
in the value of attaining the preferred, desired, and reasonably foreseeable future 
state must be retained, even for the hard-nosed realist. So is it then, a matter of 

Hypocrisy 109 
suspension of disbelief – of a cautious embrace of “highfalutin cant” and useful 
ﬁctions? Is it, after all, a question of daring to be naïve? The naïveté that 
Buckminster Fuller wrote about was supposed to instill a belief that creation and 
innovation is still possible, even after th e g r e a ta d v a n c e so fm o d e r nl i f e . F o rt h e 
realist designer, it may be the case that an element of naïveté is necessary for the 
fostering of a realist creative imagination that deﬁes the cynicism that emerges 
from zero-sum experience. 

7 
CLOSING PROPOSITIONS 
After empathy, Realdesign 
Given the previous chapters’ recurring refrain – that contemporary design practices 
unfold in an imperfect world, in which the existence of betrayal, corruption, cun­
ning, and hypocrisy, is an incontrovertible fact of social life – we hope to ﬁnish this 
book with a set of propositions for how designers can view and relate to such a 
world. Thus, as hinted earlier, we suggest an approach to participatory design that has 
- - -a taint of Ludwig von Rochau’s original take on Realpolitik (/raˈälpoliˌ tek/). The aim 
- -here is to sketch the outlines of the perspective we call Realdesign (/raˈäldəˈzın/), that 
is, a design that does not stand solely on ideals, a bit less sentimental, and foremost, 
with a sober perspective on power. 
With the concept of Realdesign, we strive to bring von Rochau’s warning 
against the implicit predominance of Gefühlspolitik (sentimental politics) and Prin­
zipenpolitik (principled politics) to bear with design: The unquestioned dominance 
of sentimental and ideal designs stripped of power. Again, Realdesign is not 
trading in impossibilities or distanced armchair cynicism. In alignment with von 
Rochau, we are concerned with the two overarching points tackled in the pre­
vious chapters – ﬁnding a realism not bogged down with cool and critical pas­
sivity, and a drive towards utopianism that is not moralist – with the aim of 
emphasizing the creative and imaginative elements inherent in design, elements 
that we argue can also be present in realism. Thus, with the term Realdesign we seek 
to imply a designerly realism, utopic, imaginative, and visionary; yet backed with a 
sober perspective on the Machiavellian characteristics of politics and the recalcitrance 
of social relations. Informed by a strategic insight, bolstered by alliances, Realdesign 
does not shy away from aﬃrming a need to take a realist perspective on power, or 
from leveraging or claiming power where needed. 
A perspective of Realdesign is not meant to celebrate the Machiavellian or 
authoritarian auteur. Its purpose is to defend and develop the democratic 
DOI: 10.4324/9781003281443-7 

Closing propositions 111 
elements in participatory processes or co-design. Here, democratic contrivances 
work in the Nietzschean sense as quarantine measures against the lust for power: 
As necessary as they are boring (Flyvbjerg 1998: 225). The realist but democratic ele­
ments of co-design are tedious yet essential to limit the drift towards the Machiavellian 
auteurship of unilateral decision-making and project authoritarianism. 
As pointed out in previous chapters, with the interest in socially engaged processes 
growing over the last decade, designers must be careful how they utilize processes of 
participation, social innovation, and co-design. Processes of user engagement are not 
a panacea for producing socially beneﬁcial outcomes. Instead, there is a recurring risk 
that the focus on social processes masks the fact that such processes often fail to 
deliver equitable results. The ideal of how designers want things to be,o b f u s c a t e s t h e 
confusion, tensions, and corruption taking place within the “material” of social 
sculpture. With voluntary blinders on and dazzled by democratic virtue, the fact that 
outcomes so seldom match intentions is an urgent problem for developing more 
eﬀective and equitable forms of public design processes. The agnosticism regarding 
social outcomes of social design is curious, given that design history and theory tends 
to focus heavily on the outcomes of the design process. 
Summing up, social design processes need to tackle a series of underlying issues 
present within social relations. We aim to recount some of them here. The prin­
cipal question to be addressed in this concluding discussion is then “what basic 
relations of social power undermine the visions designers set out with and come to 
shape co-design processes?” Echoing Flyvbjerg’s (1998) examination of rationality 
and power, we set out to suggest a series of propositions for Realdesign. 
Proposition 1: Design must get real 
Designers suﬀer from the common conviction that the best idea wins, and thus 
see design as a game of persuasion. Designers need to be better at recognizing that 
it is not virtuous ideas or intentions that get made or have real eﬀects. Design 
backed by power becomes reality, and this new reality upholds the powers 
invested. Be aware; as Latour has pointed out many times, in the social processes 
of actants and design, more often than not, Might is Right. Again, in line with 
Latour’s point in the construction of scientiﬁc proof, design proposals get acti­
vated in tensions between networks of power, incentives, and inﬂuences. 
As a means to prevent social practice-informed design from generating negative 
outcomes, designers ought to acknowledge the limits of idealist “what if?” start­
ing points. “What if” questions are strong visionary starting points, but not 
enough to make things real. In order to balance such idealism, designers ought to 
place more focus on the realist “who whom?”– who beneﬁts from the social 
innovation, and who pays the price for the change in order? 
As Geuss stresses, the basic political question has to be an extension of Lenin’s 
realist “who whom”, to become “Who <does> what to whom for whose ben­
eﬁt?” (Geuss 2008: 23). The doing part above is not limited to the actual practice, 

112 Closing propositions 
but also signiﬁes the potential threat of doing, as in the peril of punishment, or 
withdrawal of doing, as in termination of support, agency, or care. Here, in refer­
ence to the discussion above on the universalism of participatory approaches, it is 
worth remembering that the “who whom” question includes an interrogation of 
whose perspective remains dominant in a process of participation, in whose 
interests the participants are doing what they do. 
Designers could attune their practices to the propensity of social relations to be 
aﬀected by Machiavellian politics. In the end, also in social design processes “form 
follows function”, as the famous dictum goes. But for social processes, this means 
form is dictated under the functional values of the most powerful. The ultimate 
“function” of most designs is to preserve the status quo, serving the client in 
alignment with power politics. 
“Who whom” remains a key question for the realist designer. 
Proposition 2: The process of change is a process of conﬂict 
Someone’s utopia is someone else’s dystopia. The desired “change” that designers 
play with through design thinking and visioning is not a neutral process. Change 
alters power dynamics; someone’s position and interests will be advanced, and 
most often to the disadvantage of some other stakeholder. Realist politics, as 
Geuss (2010: 23) notes, means “to think politically is to think about agency, 
power, and interests, and the relations between these”. Seen from the perspective 
of power, what is right, and even laws, are minor politics: It is the enforcement of 
laws that is the real political matter, that gives some stability to political action. 
Politics then is about exercising power to attain various ends; it is not an 
inherent rule-governed activity, but one better understood as the conjunc­
tion of diﬀerent actors making context-speciﬁc judgments, taking advantage 
of unexpected opportunities, and innovating. There is nothing unnatural in 
exercising power […] and part of the process of exercising such power might 
be to subject those under one’s control to rules of one’s choice. 
(2010: 51) 
A designer must not shy away from the power that comes with the profession, 
but also not hide his or her responsibilities behind users or participants, or 
toothless principles. Engaging in social processes means diving into conﬂict with 
imperfect and unpredictable collaborators. 
Rather than idealist “what if?” questions, designers – so used to thinking in terms 
of “the user”– may ask questions like: Who is the “user” and who is being “used”? 
As Geuss suggests, every social order is always a social achievement, and such an 
undertaking is “generally achieved only at a certain price” (Geuss 2008: 22). A new 
order inevitably comes at the price of one or several persons’ disadvantage, and “one 
person’s disorder is sometimes another’s freedom”, not too unlike how US Secretary 

Closing propositions 113 
of Defense Donald Rumsfeld for instance called the civil war in Iraq as an instance of 
“untidy freedom” (Geuss 2008: 22f). 
“Disruption” is a trendy form of untidy freedom, which all too often does not 
disrupt the capitalist and colonial paradigm under which it operates. Instead, the 
disruption tends to fracture the social structures that make labor stable and 
threatens to dissolve long-established solidarities. Disruption in design-speak is 
another word for political destabilization, in correspondence with how William 
Galston summarizes politics as the tension between stabilization and destabiliza­
tion: “politics is always and everywhere a tension between the drive for and the 
goods of stabilization and consensus, on the one hand, and the drive for and 
goods of destabilization and conﬂict on the other” (Galston 2007: 9). 
One of the reasons change is slow in social structures is that social bonds take a long 
time to establish. People notice shifts in power and when facing threat from the out­
side, and tacit bonds of loyalty between peers tend to fray slowly. Disruptive designs 
bypass and break apart established loyalties, overriding social emotions (such as shared 
empathy) with technological and organizational streamlining, leaving social bonds 
shattered and redundant. The realist remindsu st h a t c h a n g ei sab u s i n e s s f a ru n t i d i e r 
than most designers tend to recognize; think twice before you disrupt social relations. 
At its base, social design is an engagement conﬂict that breaks up and rearranges 
relationships. Even in what looks like win-win situations, there will most certainly 
be losers down the line. A realist designer must ask: Who cleans up the mess and 
heals the social wounds after the “design disruptors” have left with the valuables? 
Proposition 3: There is something rotten in design, and the user is 
the victim 
Designers need to engage with the negative valence of power politics, a necessity of 
dealing with Machiavelli. That is, a designer engaging in social processes needs to 
practice power literacy in design. As mentioned earlier, empathy and creative processes 
of visioning, as in “design thinking”, are not enough. The realist perspective here 
acknowledges how design processes arrange and allocate agency, assets, and power, 
between client and user, and this process remains largely distant by the idealist-driven 
“what if” questions of the designer. 
The types of co-design that emerge from a lens of Scandinavian social-democratic 
and consensus-driven perspective on politics suﬀer from the common delusion that 
the user (or citizen) stands at the foundation of power. When not congratulating 
themselves by associating with the genius of turtleneck-clad entrepreneurs, the more 
democratically oriented designers tend to think there is a “We the users”,o r r a t h e r , 
“We the consumers”, as the spirit guiding design progress. In a history of social 
design, it may appear that power emerges from the conscious consent of the user, 
seduced to change behavior by the grand visions proposed by designers or entre­
preneurs. The user, or “stakeholder”, is in charge, designers tell themselves, or at least 
write so in their project applications. 

114 Closing propositions 
Do not fool yourself. Users are hostages. They are held by designers as human 
shields. If the vision works, it is to the merit of the excellent and good-hearted 
designer, stripping users from praise. But when the project fails, the users are left 
adrift, exhausted and betrayed, while the designer searches for a new trendy issue to 
address, fresh calls for project applications, or better participants to curate towards 
photogenic results. Under the delusion of user-power, both use-situations and users 
themselves are designed to align with the designated distribution of power and 
agency set by the stakeholders in power, such as clients, institutions, or funding 
agencies. “User-centric” means the shaping of user agency, and the actions of the 
user are at the center of the process. As Steve Woolgar (1990) has so poignantly 
shown, it is users who are themselves designed in what is called user-driven processes. 
Eﬀective social design work must not be mistaken as good-hearted volunteerism, 
where the designer does good for a speciﬁc user, hoping the eﬀort makes a dif­
ference in this isolated instance while failing to push the bigger issue. Nor is social 
design solutionism, where a cybernetic and data-driven ﬁx can bypass power games 
as the situation is optimized in favor of the user (or at least it seems so because it is 
“transparent”). 
By shying away from how users are continuously abused in contestations of 
power, designers betray the people they are saying they support. Recognizing the 
unavoidable conﬂicts users are stuck in must be the starting point for engagement. 
Proposition 4: Good is dead 
Even if inhabiting a world where it seems Might is Right, designers should not 
abandon a quest for empathy and participation. So just because designers may use 
a realist lens to view the world and see their practice in relationship to invested 
interests and power dynamics, does not mean ethics are jettisoned. But it is a fair 
question to ask; is Good dead? 
First, empathy, as the guiding principle of design, is not a cure-all. Rather, we 
must remember that perverted forms of empathy are also present in torture, as 
Elaine Scarry (1985) points out, where feeling is used as a weapon needed in 
unmaking the physical and psychological world of others. An eﬀective torturer 
knows, and feels out, how to break down and unmake the emotional world of 
the victim to reach the desired outcome. 
Second, empathy may not produce the emotional bandwidth between designer 
and user as is commonly suggested. While empathy does strive to connect with 
others, it is still self-referential: The foundation of the connection emerges from 
my own feelings and experiences. Similarly, the others that I build my experience 
on, are the ones I normally connect with, who are within my own “ingroup”,o r 
at least beings onto whom I can project my own sensibilities. That is, I empathize 
with my peers, and perhaps mammals, but it is harder empathizing with people I 
disagree with, with enemies or organisms whose bodies and senses are foreign to 
me, such as insects. 

Closing propositions 115 
Third, as pointed out by Cameron Tonkinwise (2010), the focus on the good 
experience for the user also misses contexts of aggregation, systemic issues, and 
political conﬂict. The lens of empathy fails to recognize questions of scale, historic 
struggles, gains or compromise. For the armchair-design thinker, empathy does 
not register sacriﬁces made in violence and negotiations. It is too easy to overlook 
the perspective of silenced voices and trauma, as they may not appear on the 
readable emotional spectrum to the outsider designer. Instead, driven by his or 
her own feelings (or the current social, economic, or political agenda) of what 
appears just and right, the designer quickly moralizes the subject empathized 
with, searching for the “right” empathy with the “right” people. Whereas 
empathy presents itself as neutral and ethical, we must remember that what is 
called “populism” is empathetic politics, enforcing itself on the public realm by 
provocative, irrational, and feeling-driven struggles over power (Ahmed 2004). 
Along the lines of Ahmed, Philippe Mengue (2005: 393) sees the use and 
exploitation of the passions as instrumental in order to rule: 
Politics as an activity does not consist of the elimination of the passions 
(which is impossible), but in knowing how they can be used, how to play oﬀ 
one against the other, in order to assure a minimum of unity, of stability and 
agreement in civil society. This emotional core […] needs continually to be 
reproduced, because by itself it is unstable, changing and transitory. 
Along the lines of Flyvbjerg’s (1998) warning of the political use of rationality as a 
disguise of power politics, (what is “functional” is that which supports the type of 
power which thrives under that function) also empathy is easily converted into a tool 
to further the designers’ own status and prestige. Similarly, it is easy to attack the use 
of empathy from a similar angle as Flyvbjerg’s critique of rationality, that what is 
considered empathic is only that which translates to operational guidelines and can 
increase the power and status of those inside the organizations. To the point where 
few proponents of empathy will use it if the empathy works against them. 
So a reader may ask, what are designers to do with their empathy, the capacity 
they have come to believe is their superpower? The point of Realdesign is; when 
it is not misguided or deluded, empathy is not enough. The methods seeking 
empathy, such as participation, inclusion, and co-design, are too easily nulliﬁed 
when it comes to actually representing user interests in a ﬁnal design. Without a 
backing of political agency and action, virtuous ambitions are edgeless. From this 
standpoint, Good is dead. Good, as a free-standing vision of designerly practice 
guided by high-minded intentions, does not per deﬁnition win the competition. 
In this way, Realdesign does not put focus on the “why”, thinking that good 
enough reasons will convince reality to take form along virtuous courses of 
action. Instead, Realdesign strives to move beyond the “why”, to the crueler 
emphasis on how to mobilize power to leverage and change the outcomes in 
favorable ways. 

116 Closing propositions 
The Machiavellian perspective of Realdesign highlights how we have to separate 
empathy and morals from political power. This is a delicate strategy in a time 
where politics, in many places, has become the extension of morals; as today, 
anyone with an opinion is a meme-politician, each staking out their own moral 
claims rather than mobilizing a realistic political program. If Realpolitik is a sphere 
separate from morals, delineating the public from the personal; Realdesign focuses 
on the distribution of power and agency, over ethics and the empathy of personal 
experiences and opinions. 
It is not that virtue as a goal is disqualiﬁed. But remember, as both Machiavelli 
and von Rochau showed with their sordid perspectives on politics, that the quest 
for the good needs to be backed by a realistic perspective on power. 
Proposition 5: Realdesign is a creative form of realism 
The idealist designer would complain and say, “If you remain stuck in zero-sum 
power-games, you limit the creative visioning and potential of design!” While the 
possibilities of Post-its may appear inexhaustible, we must not limit the realist 
perspective to merely a trade in impossibilities or cynical modes to close down 
creative processes with an appeal to “stay realistic”. 
First, power is at least as creative as the various forms of resistance to it. Even 
conservation of power needs creativity. While “holding onto power” may seem 
intrinsically defensive and reactive, maintaining the status quo requires relentless 
innovation. As Geuss (2010) warns, we must not mistake idealism as being more 
“creative” or “imaginative” than realism just because it deals in possibilities. 
Realists invent enemies and excuses for war, fuel conﬂict with creative work, 
circumvent embargos and create alternative networks of diplomacy and smug­
gling, in the end remaking whole new ideological realities, just to name some 
examples. Realists operate vibrant forms of imaginative storytelling (not least 
imagining “weapons of mass destruction” and inventing “enhanced interroga­
tion”). But compared to the idealists, they see their creation within a framework 
of contestation and conﬂict, not sunk into the radiant mire of virtue. 
In a similar vein, we must not think that realists cannot “speculate” about the 
future like critical designers do. Realists make no more claims to truth than the 
idealists do, and the suspension of disbelief in “speculation” should raise as much 
suspicion as skeptical suppositions. The idealist’s guidance by virtue looks away 
from how the same sentiments and cultural norms spur many forms of relational 
violence in the name of virtue (Fiske & Rai 2015). As Geuss (2010: x) notes, 
conservation and conformism require imagination just like innovation, even if it is 
an imagination of a diﬀerent kind. Claims and works of power are just as creative, 
imaginative, innovative, and possible, as resistance, critique, and virtuous fantasies. 
So, while realism may often appear as the antonym of speculative or imaginary 
it must not be seen as relentlessly negative or a cynical retreat to Might is Right. 
Realdesign is not more or less imaginary than any other design. The plasticity of 

Closing propositions 117 
the world is still there, even if tied to power struggles. While a Realdesigner may 
have a stark realization of the forces that shape both possibilities and impossi­
bilities, these are like most other obstacles or problems in design; they form the 
parameters for the innovation space the designer has to deal with. Here, the 
obstacles emerge from social relations of power, and cannot be dreamed or 
argued away with beautiful renderings, appeals from users or citizens, or claims of 
moral superiority. 
The creative part of design likes to see itself as dealing in “constructive criti­
cism”. Design deﬁes the negative, as the very foundation of design lies in its 
shaping of the artiﬁcial, suggesting change, however small, as its core activity, and 
here lies also its political potential (Dilnot 2015). Nevertheless, any form of cri­
tique must analyse, evaluate, compare, and judge, as Geuss (2014: 70) notes. 
Thus, the primary question for the designer must be “constructive under what 
conditions and constraints?” That is, constructive for whom? Geuss notes, 
What might count as ‘constructive’ for us, that is, what we, given who we are, 
could do about something, given our identities and possibilities, need not be 
the same as what is constructive for them (given their identity and situation) 
(Geuss 2014: 80) 
For designers, valuable constructive critique can judge things both positive and 
negative, and, perhaps more importantly, open, enrich, and reconﬁgure the issue 
at hand for better future decisions to be made. But the Realdesigner must know 
the limits to the frameworks used to judge things “constructive” in the ﬁrst place 
to circumvent the repressive attempts to limit critique to that which aligns with 
the current dominant political program or ideology. 
So, to sum up, Realdesign is not more or less utopian than idealism, but acts 
on “utopian realism” (Geuss 2016), that is, building on visions while backed by a 
real perspective on power. This means not falling for the situationist trap of 
restraining realism, claiming it deals merely in the impossible (or “be realistic, 
demand the impossible” as the situationists would say). The project of the Real-
designer is supported by political principles, not moral ones, it is “one of ‘wishful 
visions,’ (rather than ‘wish-dreams’)” (Geuss 2016: 48). 
Proposition 6: Ask realist questions 
Taking a realist approach to design should not mean an endorsement of 
Machiavellian power politics, but our main concern is in line with the perspective 
framed by von Rochau and discussed in the second chapter. While visions of 
design can concern realization of ideals, the realist must put focus on the attain­
ment of concrete ends, to back visions with a sober look on power. Realdesign is 
design that builds towards a political program and to one’s best abilities eliminates 
self-delusion. 

118 Closing propositions 
“How” and “what” are recurring questions in design processes, and they take 
diﬀerent form in traditional linear design briefs versus the more complex issues at 
hand in socially entangled issues. Instead of putting the “how” question at the 
center of design processes, socially aware design practices have cultivated a culture 
where questioning the brief is a recurring theme. “What” is really needed is a 
question that opens up to more socially aware forms of inquiry to reach the “real 
problem” sought by Papanek. Whereas the “how” question may point to best 
practices, the “what” question is explicitly context-driven and requires a designer 
equipped with curiosity and ample time for listening and empathy. However, to 
once again paraphrase Papanek, a practitioner of Realdesign needs to engage with 
the realist tensions that underpin the designer’s share of the problem. Underneath 
the traditional “how” and “what”, a chasm of power now opens. 
As noted in Bew’s (2016) discussion on von Rochau’s ideas, engaging with 
power requires a clear-sighted understanding of who holds, and is given, power in 
the current arrangement (which does not mean power is necessarily in the rightful 
hands). But power games in the social realm are complex as populations align with 
the prevailing cultural context and dominant ideological undercurrents of their 
time. Mobilizing enough social momentum to make an idea real means aligning 
purposefully with these currents, adapting means to ends. Social interventions 
require clear-sightedness; when suggesting and implementing a new design, this 
will most certainly be a manipulation of the status quo that in turn will rearrange or 
cement diﬀerent positions of power – the question is how and to what degree. 
What to design? 
Who/Whom? 
FIGURE 7.1 The what or who/whom problem. 

Closing propositions 119 
Under these conditions, what is the room aﬀorded for realizing one’s design 
visions, and what are the risks? 
Though seemingly simple, such questions may act as a useful starting point for 
engaging with social design, serving as an antidote for detrimental eﬀects that may 
inadvertently emerge from endeavors that start from a purely idealist position. 
The Realdesign perspective on social design practices can be elicited by a series 
of questions: 
�	 Who is the user or stakeholder? What is in their interest? 
�	 Who is the client? What is in their interest? 
�	 Who do you report to? What is in their interest? 
�	 Who is invited/included and who is excluded in present and historical context? 
�	 What are the structural and institutional frameworks around this action? 
�	 How is agency and power redistributed, who is oﬀered power, who is it 
withdrawn from? 
�	 How is the “social” (relations of loyalty and commitments) redistributed or 
reformed? 
�	 Who earns what in the end? 
�	 How can reimbursement/compensation for the weakest be guaranteed, or 
how must they mobilize to enhance their leverage? 
What we hope the concept of Realdesign brings about to the design ﬁeld, is a 
more earnest review of the possibilities that participation and social forms of 
design have to actually implement the visions emerging from the community 
engaged in the design process. The purpose is not to suggest an all-knowing 
technocratic designer to decide simply because he or she “knows best”. 
Rather, just like social relations are a recalcitrant and unruly material, power is 
slippery material to engage, work with, and give shape to as a designer. While 
power is a material that will not go away, it is a material that can be taken, secured, 
and distributed, using design agency. Betrayal, corruption, cunning, and hypocrisy 
are inherent parts of political power games and must be accounted for without self-
deception. This is the important lesson of von Rochau to designers; for visions to 
be implemented, they need to be backed by a clear-sighted political perspective 
and accompanied by mobilization of power. Relying on the benevolence or mercy 
of the surrounding world leaves visions toothless. But what the republican tradition 
teaches is that to avoid the abuse of power, it is better distributed and controlled 
through the dissemination of agency and control, checks and balances. 
Despite the critique against prevailing idealisms put forward in this book, we 
strongly believe that social forms of design are much needed across societies to 
enhance the agency of users and citizens. To the practitioner of Realdesign, the 
challenge is to persist in asking that uncomfortable question: “Who whom?” 

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS
 
Books are often slow in the making, and this one is no exception. We started 
working together in 2005, in an urban planning process that sparked our joint 
interest in the politics of co-design. We would subsequently reﬂect on this 
experience in the context of a 2007–2009 artistic research project funded by 
the Swedish Research Council and based at the University of Gothenburg’s 
School of Design and Crafts. In conversations with project participants – 
notably sociologist Catharina Thörn, asw e l la sp h i l o s o p h e r M a t s R o s e n g r e n 
and architect Peter Ullmark – the proposition that co-design is a mere post-
political charade emerged as a point of contention. Some years later, in 2015, 
we published a write-up of this experience in a special issue of CoDesign guest 
edited by Cristiano Storni, Thomas Binder, Per Linde, and Dagny Stuedahl. 
In subsequent articles, the project of fusing design theory with realist political 
thought started to emerge. 
Thus, Chapter 3 of this book – the one that describes the above-mentioned 
2005 planning process – is an extended version of the article “Quasi-Quisling: 
Co-Design and the Assembly of Collaborateurs”, published in CoDesign, volume 
11, issue 3, in 2015. Moreover, Chapter 4 features material from the article 
“Social Means Do Not Justify Corruptible Ends: A Realist Perspective of Social 
Innovation and Design”, published in She-Ji, volume 2, issue 4, in 2016. Finally, 
Chapter 5 features material from the article “Designing Consent: Can Design 
Thinking Manufacture Democratic Capitalism?”, published in Organizational 
Aesthetics, volume 5, issue 2, 2016. We hereby extend a thanks to the editors of 
the journals that published early iterations of the arguments of this book – that is, 
CoDesign, Design & Culture, Organizational Aesthetics, and She-Ji. We similarly 
extend a thanks to the anonymous reviewers that have generously spent time on 
helping us develop these arguments.

Further, we would like to acknowledge that it has been a pleasure to work with
